->From Plutarch’s Lives<- <note>original: "τοῦ Πλουτάρχου τῶν βίων" [tou Ploutarchou ton bion]. Plutarch was a Greek philosopher and biographer (c. 46–119 AD) famous for his "Parallel Lives," which paired famous Greeks with famous Romans to compare their characters.</note>

An ornate rectangular headpiece featuring interlocking geometric and foliate scrollwork patterns, rendered in reddish-brown ink.

# + THESEUS

<margin>+</margin>Just as in geographic maps, O Sosius Senecio, historians crowd those things which escape their knowledge into the furthest parts of their charts, sometimes adding explanations and writing over certain places that "what lies beyond are waterless and beast-haunted sands," or "dark mud," or "Scythian cold," <note>original: "Σκυθικὸν κρύος" [Skythikon kryos]. Scythia was a vast region north of the Black Sea, which the Greeks associated with extreme cold and nomadic tribes.</note> or "a frozen sea"; so for me, in the writing of my ***Parallel Lives***, having passed through that time which is accessible to probable reasoning and provides a firm footing for the history of facts, it would be well to say concerning those earlier times: "but what lies beyond are wonders and tragedies, the realm of poets and myth-writers, no longer possessing credibility or clarity." <note>original: "Σόσσιε Σενεκίων" [Sossie Senekion]. Quintus Sosius Senecio was a distinguished Roman politician and a close friend of Plutarch to whom these biographies were dedicated.</note>

Plutarch, Parallel Lives, Theseus, Sosius Senecio, geography, history

<summary>This page contains the opening of Plutarch’s "Life of Theseus," featuring a famous preface where the author compares the chronological boundaries of history to the edges of maps where geography gives way to myth. It marks the transition from historical biography into the legendary past of the founders of Athens.</summary>
<keywords>Plutarch, Theseus, Sosius Senecio, Parallel Lives, historiography, geography, Greek mythology, Athenian history</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the general comparison of Theseus and Romulus to the specific lineage and birth of Theseus, focusing on the reputation of his grandfather Pittheus and the mysterious circumstances of his father Aegeus’s visit to the oracle at Delphi.</meta>

Pittheus, the grandfather of Theseus, dwelling in Troezen—though it was not a large city—possessed a reputation greater than all others, being a man of letters and the wisest of his time. It seems that the nature and power of that wisdom was similar to that used by **Hesiod** <note>A famous early Greek poet known for his instructional and moralizing poetry.</note>, who was especially renowned for the maxims in his *Works and Days*. Indeed, they say one of these belongs to Pittheus:
<margin>where?</margin> "Let the reward offered to a friend be sufficient." <note>original: "Μισθὸς δ' ανδρι φιλῳ εἰρημενος ἀρκιος ἔστω." This line is found in Hesiod's 'Works and Days', line 370.</note> This has even been stated by **Aristotle** the philosopher. And **Euripides**, when
<margin>51</margin> addressing **Hippolytus** <note>The grandson of Pittheus and son of Theseus.</note> as the "pupil of holy Pittheus," demonstrates 
<margin>inquiry</margin> the high regard in which Pittheus was held. Now to **Aegeus**, who was in need of children, 
they say the **Pythia** <note>The high priestess and oracle at the Temple of Apollo in Delphi.</note> gave that famous oracle, 
commanding him to have relations with no woman before arriving in Athens. But as she seemed to speak this not at all clearly, he went to Troezen and shared with Pittheus the voice of the god, which was as follows:
<margin>oracle</margin> > Do not loosen the protruding foot of the wineskin, best of men,
> before you reach the land of the Athenians. <note>original: "Ἀσκου τον προὔχοντα ποδα φερτατε λαων, μη λυσης πριν δῆμον Ἀθηνῶν εἰσαφικεσθαι." This is a riddle; the "foot of the wineskin" refers to the spout of a wine bag, but metaphorically warns Aegeus against sexual intercourse until his return.</note>

Pittheus, understanding something of the oracle, persuaded Aegeus to sleep with his daughter **Aethra**. When Aegeus realized the secret <gloss>the secret conception</gloss>, he left behind a sword and a pair of sandals, hiding them under a great rock which had a hollow inside it, sized exactly to contain the objects. Telling only Aethra about this and instructing her that if a son should be born to her, and if upon reaching manhood he was able to lift the rock and take what had been left there, she should send the boy to him carrying these things without anyone else knowing. He wished for it to remain as hidden as possible 
<margin>+ beyond this account</margin> from everyone. For he strongly feared the **Pallantidae** <note>The fifty sons of Pallas, the brother of Aegeus, who hoped to seize the throne if Aegeus remained childless.</note>, who were plotting against him and looked down on him because of his lack of heirs. There were fifty sons born of Pallas.
<margin>he departed</margin> While Aethra was already in labor, Aegeus departed.

<summary>This page recounts the wisdom of Pittheus and the trickery used to conceive Theseus after King Aegeus received a cryptic oracle. It concludes with Aegeus hiding a sword and sandals under a rock as tokens for his unborn son to find.</summary>

...he was named Theseus because of the "placement" <note>The Greek word for "placement" is *thesis* (θέσις), punning on the name Theseus (Θησεύς).</note> of the tokens.

They do this remembering and honoring him much more justly than they honor Silanion and Parrhasius, who were painters and sculptors of the images of Theseus.

Since it was still a custom at that time for those transitioning from childhood, upon coming to Delphi, to offer the first-fruits of their hair to the god, Theseus went to Delphi. And they say a place there is still named "Theseis" after him <gloss>indeed</gloss>. But he shaved only the front part of his head, just as Homer said the Abantes <note>A warlike tribe from Euboea mentioned in the Iliad.</note> did <gloss>and thus clearly</gloss>; and this kind of haircut was named "Theseid" because of him <gloss>Theseus</gloss>. 

The Abantes were the first to cut their hair in this manner. They were not taught this by the Arabs, as some believe, nor did they imitate the Mysians; rather, they were warlike and close-fighting men, having learned more than all others to press into hand-to-hand combat with their enemies. As Archilochus <note>A famous Greek lyric poet known for his verses on war.</note> also bears witness in these lines:

Thus, Aethra led him to the rock and, explaining the circumstances of his birth, ordered him to take up his father’s tokens and sail to Athens. But Theseus, getting under the rock, easily
<margin>+ he heaved up [anēōse], instead of he pushed [eōse]; as if one were to say, that by moving it from the earth he heaved it up. It was difficult to make the journey to Athens by land, as no part of it was clear or free from danger due to robbers and criminals. 

For that time produced men who were, it seems, extraordinary and untiring in the works of their hands, the speed of their feet, and the strength of their bodies. But they used their nature for nothing reasonable or beneficial; instead, they took pride in savagery, insolence, and arrogance, <gloss>taking advantage of strength</gloss> **T**hey took advantage of their power with cruelty and bitterness, and by conquering, violating, and destroying whatever fell in their path.

Heracles, while passing through, cut down and destroyed some of these men; others, hiding as he passed, crouched and withdrew; <gloss>they were let go</gloss> and were neglected, living humble lives. But when Heracles met with misfortune and, having killed Iphitus, departed for Lydia and <margin><gloss>is humbled</gloss></margin> served there as a slave to Omphale <note>A queen of Lydia whom Heracles was forced to serve as penance.</note> for a long time—imposing this penalty on himself for the murder—the affairs in Lydia enjoyed great peace and security. But in the regions around Greece, vices once again blossomed and broke out, since there was no longer anyone <margin><gloss>hindering</gloss></margin> pressing them down or restraining them. Therefore, the journey was deadly.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Theseus’s youth. Having just discovered the tokens left by his father, Aegeus, Theseus now weighs the choice between a safe journey by sea or a perilous trek by land to Athens. This section explores his internal motivation, drawing a parallel between his ambition and that of the legendary Heracles.</meta>

<margin>
**A Noble Kinship**
**Pelops** : **Hippodameia**
(Grandparents)
/ \

(Siblings)
| |

| |
**Theseus** <-> **Heracles**
**COUSINS**
</margin>

For those traveling on foot from the Peloponnesus to Athens, the way was fraught with danger. Each of the robbers and criminals there had been dealt with <note>The Greek text here implies Theseus's desire to replicate the justice meted out by other heroes.</note>, and yet Theseus chose not to take the safe route by sea. It seems that for a long <gloss>time</gloss> <note>original: "kronou," likely a misspelling of "chronou" (time)</note> he had been secretly stirred by the fame of the excellence of Heracles. He was a most eager listener to those who described what kind of man Heracles was, especially those who had actually seen him in action or heard him speak. It was clear to everyone that he felt exactly what Themistocles experienced in a later time, when he said that the "trophy of Miltiades" <gloss>would not let him rest</gloss> would not let him sleep <note>Miltiades was the hero of the Battle of Marathon; Themistocles was so inspired by his predecessor's glory that it drove him to his own greatness.</note>. In the same way, Theseus admired the excellence of Heracles; by night his deeds appeared in his dreams, and by day his zeal sparked and goaded him, as he meditated on performing similar feats.

He also shared a certain bond of kinship, as they were cousins. For <gloss>he is the brother of Pittheus</gloss> Aethra was the daughter of Pittheus, and Alcmene was the daughter of Lysidike. Lysidike and Pittheus were siblings, born of Hippodameia and Pelops. Therefore, he considered it a terrible and unbearable thing that while Heracles was traveling <gloss>by land</gloss> everywhere to purge both the land and the sea of wicked men, he himself should run away from the labors that stood in his path. He felt he would be shaming his father by name—calling himself the son of a king—and by nature—carrying the tokens of his lineage—if he brought his real father only the recognition of the sandals and the unstained sword, without also providing a visible character of his noble birth through his own works and actions. 

With such a spirit and such reasoning, he set out, intended to do no one any wrong, but to defend himself against those who offered violence. And first, in the region of Epidaurus, he encountered a man who used a club <gloss>in this way</gloss> and for this reason was nicknamed "the Club-bearer" <note>original: "Korynetes," referring to Periphetes, a son of Hephaestus who murdered travelers with an iron club.</note>. When this man laid hands on him and tried to stop him from passing <gloss>against him</gloss>, Theseus engaged him in combat and killed him. Being delighted with the club, he took it and made it his own weapon.

<summary>Theseus decides to travel to Athens by land to emulate the heroic deeds of his cousin, Heracles, rather than taking a safe sea voyage. He begins his journey by defeating the bandit Periphetes in Epidaurus, claiming the villain's iron club as his own.</summary>
<keywords>Theseus, Heracles, Pelops, Pittheus, Miltiades, Themistocles, genealogy, Epidaurus, Periphetes, Club-bearer, heroism</keywords>

Terrible things are said. Some say that Phaea was not a beast, but a murderous and unrestrained female robber <note>The text refers to the "Crommyonian Sow," a monstrous wild boar that ravaged the countryside. Some ancient rationalists argued she was actually a human bandit nicknamed "Sow" due to her filthy habits.</note>, and that she was born there and lived in Crommyon. She was given this name <note>original: "eponomasthenai"</note> because of her character and her way of life. Later, she was killed by Theseus. 

As for Sciron, <gloss>Theseus</gloss> destroyed him <gloss>he destroyed</gloss> before reaching the territory of Megara by throwing him down from the rocks. According to <gloss>that</gloss> popular story, he was a robber who preyed on passersby. But as some others say, out of arrogance and violence <term>hybris</term> <note>arrogant pride or insolence, often leading to a downfall</note> he would stretch out his feet to strangers and command them to wash them, then kick and push <gloss>them</gloss> while they were washing him into the sea. 

However, the writers from Megara, <gloss>as if</gloss> walking against the tide of common rumor, and <gloss>bearing witness</gloss> through the long passage of time and <gloss>in</gloss> the testimony <gloss>of those</gloss> who fought in wars, say that Sciron was neither a robber nor a violent man. Instead, they say he was a punisher of robbers and a kinsman and friend to good and just men. For Aeacus is considered the most pious of the Greeks, and Cychreus <gloss>the</gloss> Salaminian is honored in Athens. Furthermore, the virtue of Peleus and Telamon is known by everyone. Now Sciron, <gloss>they say</gloss>, became a relative by marriage to Aeacus, being the father-in-law to <gloss>Peleus</gloss> and Aeacus. He was the grandfather of Peleus and Telamon, who were born of Endeis, the daughter of Sciron and Chariclo. 

It would not be likely, then, that the best men would enter into a family alliance <gloss>for the sake of</gloss> receiving and giving the greatest and most precious things with a wicked man. They say Theseus killed Sciron not when he first traveled to <gloss>the</gloss> Athens, but later when he captured Eleusis from the Megarians, having outwitted Diocles the ruler. These matters, then, involve such contradictions.

In Eleusis, <gloss>Theseus</gloss> wrestled and killed Cercyon the Arcadian. And going a little further, <gloss>he killed</gloss> Damastes in Hermione, <gloss>also</gloss> <gloss>called</gloss> Procrustes <note>The "Stretcher." Theseus did these things in imitation of Heracles. For that hero also punished attackers with the very same methods they used on others.

Previously, then, no hope of safety was held; therefore, they used to send the ship with a black sail as a sign of manifest calamity. But Paion of Amathus <note>Amathus was one of the most ancient royal cities of Cyprus, famous for its cult of Aphrodite.</note> has published a peculiar account concerning these matters. For he says that Theseus, being driven by a storm to Cyprus and carrying Ariadne who was pregnant—and in a poor state <gloss>and</gloss> distressed by the tossing of the sea—set her ashore alone. But while he was assisting the ship, he was carried back out to the open sea away from the <gloss>land</gloss> land. Therefore, the local women took Ariadne in and cared for her, as she was despondent due to her abandonment. They even brought her forged letters, as if Theseus were writing to her. And during her labor, they shared her suffering and helped her. But <gloss>Ariadne</gloss> died and they buried her before she could give birth. 

When Theseus returned and was overcome with grief, he left money for the locals and ordered them to sacrifice to Ariadne. He set up two small statuettes, one of silver and the other of bronze. In the sacrifice, on the second day of the sixth <gloss>month</gloss> month of Gorpiaios <note>Gorpiaios was a month in the Macedonian calendar, roughly corresponding to September. <meta>This page continues the biography of Theseus, shifting from his return to Athens and the mourning of his father Aegeus to the origins of the Oschophoria festival and his political unification of the Attic tribes into a single city-state.</meta>

And they led her away; for they do not say that he took out only virgins at that time, but rather two of his close young male companions. These two appeared feminine in voice and were youthful, but in their souls they were manly and eager. By means of warm baths, avoiding the sun <note>original: "skiatraphiais" — literally "rearing in the shade" to ensure the pale complexion associated with high-born women of the time.</note>, and through ointments and grooming for the hair, smoothness of skin, and complexion, they were prepared so as <gloss>out of</gloss> to <gloss>most certainly</gloss> <gloss>completely change</gloss> their hair—which became quite golden <gloss>in the hair and complexions</gloss>—as well as their voice, appearance, and gait. Thus, they were made to resemble <gloss>the</gloss> virgins as much as possible and appeared in no way different, allowing Theseus to place them in the number of the virgins and escape everyone's notice. 

When he returned, he himself led the procession, along with the young men dressed in the same way that those who carry the branches <note>original: "oschophoroi" — vine-branch bearers in the Athenian festival of Oschophoria.</note> are dressed today. They carry these branches to honor Dionysus and Ariadne because of the myth, or more likely because they returned home at the time of the fruit harvest. The meal-bearers <note>original: "deipnophoroi" — women who carried food in the ritual procession.</note> also assist and share in the sacrifice, imitating the mothers of those who were chosen by lot to be sent away; for those mothers used to visit them, bringing meat and bread. They are also said to tell myths, because those mothers told stories to their children to cheer them up and comfort them. 

These things, then, have also been recorded by Demon <note>A 4th-century BCE historian of Attica.</note>. A sacred precinct was also set apart for Theseus, and he ordered the households of those who had provided the tribute to pay for a sacrifice to him from their own resources. The <gloss>kinsmen</gloss> of the Phytalidae <note>A noble Athenian clan who supposedly purified Theseus after his labors.</note> took care of the sacrifice, Theseus having granted this to them as a reward for their hospitality. 

After the death of Aegeus, conceiving a great and marvelous deed in his mind, he settled the inhabitants of Attica into one city. He declared them one people of a single city, though they had previously been scattered and difficult to summon <gloss>for the common safety</gloss> for the common interest of all; indeed, there were times when they were at odds and even at war with one another. Therefore, he went among them and persuaded them by district and by clan. While the private citizens and the poor <gloss>quickly</gloss> accepted his invitation...

<meta>This page continues the biography of Theseus, shifting from his return to Athens to his pivotal political reforms, known as the 'Synoikismos' or unification of Attica, where he transitioned the government from a monarchy to a structured democracy.</meta>

<margin>
First, the history <note>original: "historian"</note>. <unclear>Which</unclear> is <unclear>believed</unclear> by the <unclear>historians</unclear> <unclear>before</unclear> the <unclear>reconstruction</unclear> <note>This marginal note likely refers to the factual basis of Theseus’s life before it was embellished by later poets and myth-makers.</note>.
</margin>

...of them. To the powerful, he proposed a government without a king and a democracy, where he alone would serve as the leader in war and the guardian of the laws, while the state would provide an equal share to everyone else. Some he persuaded of <gloss>these things</gloss>. Others, <gloss>fearing</gloss> his power, which <gloss>truly</gloss> was <gloss>both</gloss> calm and so great, preferred to concede to everything by being persuaded openly rather than by being forced. 

Therefore, having abolished the town halls <term>Prytaneia: the administrative heart and communal hearth of a Greek city-state</term>, council chambers, and magistracies in each individual tribe, and having established one common town hall and council chamber for everyone in the place where the city now stands, he called the city Athens. He also established a common sacrifice called the Panathenaia <note>The "All-Athenian" festival, which became the city's most important religious event.</note>. Furthermore, he established the Sacrifice of Settlement <note>original: "Metokia"</note> on the sixteenth day of the month of Hekatombaion <note>The first month of the Athenian year, falling around July or August.</note>, which they still perform today. 

Having set aside the kingship as he had agreed, he began to organize the state, starting with the gods. This oracle <gloss>from the gods</gloss> came to him from Delphi when he consulted the god about the city:

> Son of Aegeus, Theseus, offspring of Pittheus' daughter,
> My father has indeed placed within your city-stronghold
> many boundaries and spinning-points <note>original: "chrostêras" — likely referring to the turning points of destiny or the city's geographic limits.</note>.
> But do not be too distressed in your inner heart 
> while planning; for like a wineskin in the swelling surge, you shall travel the sea.

They <gloss>say</gloss> that the Sibyl <note>A legendary prophetess of the ancient world.</note> later uttered a similar prophecy regarding the city, when those performing the sacred rites proclaimed:

> A wineskin, you may be dipped, but it is not lawful for you to sink.

Wishing to increase the city even further, he invited everyone on equal terms. They say that the cry "Come hither, all ye people!" was a proclamation made by Theseus when he was establishing a sort of assembly of the whole population. However, he did not allow the democracy to become disordered or mixed into a confused mass by the flood of people pouring in. Instead, he was the first to separate them into classes: the Nobles, the Farmers, and the Artisans. To the Nobles <term>Eupatridai: 'those of good fathers,' the hereditary aristocracy of Athens</term>, he granted the duty of understanding divine matters...

<summary>Theseus unifies the tribes of Attica into the single city-state of Athens, establishes the Panathenaia festival, and receives a famous oracle comparing the city's resilience to a wineskin that floats but never sinks.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Theseus, shifting from his return to Athens to his pivotal political reforms, known as the 'Synoikismos' or unification of Attica, where he transitioned the government from a monarchy to a structured democracy. This page specifically details the division of social classes, the minting of currency, and the establishment of the Isthmian Games.</meta>

And [Theseus] gave notice to everyone that they should be the teachers of the laws, the keepers of holy things, and the interpreters of sacred rites for all the people, calling them together as if to equality. In reputation, the Noblemen <term>Eupatridae: the traditional hereditary aristocracy of Athens</term> [excelled] <gloss>in the</gloss> needs [of the state], then the Husbandmen <term>Geomoroi: the class of small-scale farmers</term>, and in multitude, the Artisans <term>Demiurgoi: the class of skilled craftsmen and laborers</term>. It seemed that the [nobility] were superior in some way. 

<margin>† <unclear>from elsewhere</unclear>, or under the earth, <unclear>or</unclear> <unclear>because</unclear> <unclear>it was</unclear> <unclear>not</unclear> <unclear>notable</unclear> <unclear>and</unclear> <unclear>visible</unclear>.</margin>

Theseus was the first to incline toward the common people, as Aristotle says, and he gave up the monarchy. Homer also seems to testify to this in the *Catalogue of Ships* <note>A famous section of the Iliad listing the Greek forces; Theseus's era is considered the dawn of Athenian identity here.</note>, addressing the Athenians alone as a "people" <note>original: "demos"</note>. He also created coinage, engraving an ox <gloss>upon it</gloss>. This was either because of the Marathonian bull, or because of Minos's general <note>Minos's general was named Taurus, which means 'bull' in Greek.</note>, or perhaps he was encouraging the citizens toward agriculture. From this, they say, came the name for the Athenian coin <note>The "ten-ox" coin.</note>. And this and that.

Having added the territory of Megara to Attica, he firmly set up the famous pillar on the Isthmus. He inscribed a poem defining the country upon it in two trimeters <note>A poetic meter consisting of three measures.</note>. Through these he said:

<gloss>these are the boundaries</gloss>
> This way looks toward Asia, which <gloss>is</gloss> not the Peloponnesos, but Ionia. <note>original: "τάδε προσείδ’ Ἀσία" — Most versions of Plutarch read "This is not Peloponnesos, but Ionia." The scribe here has substituted 'Asia' for 'not Peloponnesos'.</note>
> The other way looks here, which is the Peloponnesos, not Ionia.

And he was the first to establish the games, out of zeal for Hercules. Just as for [Hercules] the Olympic games were dedicated to Zeus, so for himself he ambitiously led the Isthmian games for Poseidon for the Greeks. For the games established there for Melicertes <note>A minor sea deity; the earlier games were a somber, local affair.</note> were performed at night, having more the nature of a mystery rite <term>telete: a secret religious initiation or ritual</term> than a spectacle or a public festival. 

<margin>It is also written [of] Cercyon.</margin>

But some say that the Isthmian games were established by Theseus for Sciron <note>A legendary bandit killed by Theseus.</note> as an expiation for his murder because of their kinship. For Sciron was said to be the son of Canethus and Henioche, the daughter of Pittheus. Others, however, say it was for Sinis <note>Another bandit known as the 'Pine-Bender'.</note> and not Sciron, and that the games were established for him by Theseus. 

He therefore arranged and stipulated with the Corinthians that they should provide a place of honor to those arriving <gloss>from</gloss> Athens for the Isthmian games, as much space as [is covered] <gloss>by</gloss> the sail of the sacred ship <note>original: "theoris" — the ship used for sacred embassies.</note>, as Hellanicus and Andron of Halicarnassus <note>The MS writes "Knikasteus," a likely scribal error for "Halicarnasseus."</note> have recorded. 

<margin>drowned in the sea!</margin>

And he sailed into the Euxine Sea <note>The Black Sea.</note> <gloss>by Theseus</gloss>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Theseus, moving from his political organization of Athens to his legendary military exploits, specifically his expedition against the Amazons and the varying historical accounts of how he encountered Queen Antiope.</meta>

As Philochorus <note>An Athenian historian (c. 340–261 BCE) known for his work on the history of Attica.</note> and some others similarly state, [Theseus] campaigned with Heracles against the Amazons and received Antiope as a prize of valor <note>original: "geras aristeion"</note>. However, the majority of writers—among whom are Pherecydes, Hellanicus, and Herodorus—say that it was later than Heracles <gloss>about Heracles</gloss> that Theseus sailed on his own private expedition <note>original: "idiostolon"</note> and took the Amazon captive, which is a more plausible account. For no one else among those who campaigned with him is recorded to have taken an Amazon captive. <gloss>Hellanicus says</gloss> But Hosius says he tricked them and went away after taking her. The Amazons were by nature friendly to men, and they did not flee Theseus when he moored in their country, but even sent him gifts of hospitality. <margin>This was not reasonable</margin> He, however, invited the woman bringing the gifts to come on board the ship. Once she had boarded, he set sail. 

A certain Menecrates, who published a history of the city of Nicaea in Bithynia, says that Theseus, having possession of Antiope, spent some time in those places. It happened that three young men from Athens were campaigning with him, who were brothers to one another: Euneos, Thasas, and Soloïs. This Soloïs fell in love <gloss>secretly</gloss> with Antiope, and although he kept it hidden from the others, he eventually told one of his close companions. That companion then approached Antiope and spoke to her about the matter. She strongly rejected the attempt, yet she handled the situation with both discretion and gentleness, and she did not <gloss>nothing</gloss> report any of it to Theseus. 

But Soloïs, having lost all hope, threw himself into a certain river and perished. When Theseus finally learned the cause and the suffering of the young man, he took it heavily. In his distress, he recalled a certain <gloss>oracle</gloss> oracular response from the Pythia <note>The high priestess of the Temple of Apollo at Delphi who delivered prophecies.</note> for himself. For it had been commanded to him at Delphi by the Pythia that whenever he should be in a foreign land and feel the greatest grief or become deeply sorrowful, he should found a city there and leave some of his followers as its leaders. Because of this, he founded the city and, after the god, named it Pythopolis. He named the nearby <gloss>river</gloss> river Soloïs, [after the young man].

In the fourth month, a treaty was reached through the mediation of Hippolyta; or <gloss>or Antiope</gloss> Antiope—for this is the name given to the woman who lived with Theseus <note>Plutarch is navigating the common confusion in mythology over whether Theseus married Hippolyta or her sister Antiope</note>, and not "Antiope" [as a separate person]. Some say, however, that the woman fell in battle after Theseus's time, having been struck by a javelin <gloss><unclear>thrown</unclear></gloss> by Molpadia <note>A prominent Amazon in the legendary battle against Athens</note>. For they also say that the wounded <gloss><unclear>weakly</unclear></gloss> Amazons were secretly sent by Antiope to Chalcis <note>A major city on the island of Euboea</note> to receive care, and that some were buried there, near what is now called the Amazoneion. A tomb of the Amazons is also shown to the people of Megara <note>A city located between Athens and Corinth</note> near the place called the Stream, as one walks from the marketplace to the area where the "Rhombus" <note>A diamond-shaped landmark or building in ancient Megara</note> stands. It is said that others died near Chaeroneia and were buried by the small stream which, it seems, was anciently called the Thermodon, but is now called the Haemon <note>meaning "The Bloody River," named for the carnage of the battle</note>. Regarding these things, I have written in the *Life of Demosthenes* <note>Plutarch often cross-references his own "Parallel Lives"</note>. It appears the Amazons did not pass through Thessaly without trouble either; for their graves are still shown to this day around Skotoussa and the "Dog's Heads" (Cynoscephalae) <note>Hills in Thessaly where a major battle occurred</note>. 

These things, then, are worthy of memory regarding the Amazons. As for the uprising <gloss>uprising</gloss> of the Amazons described by the poet of the *Theseid* <note>An ancient, now lost, epic poem detailing the deeds of Theseus</note>—where Antiope attacked during the marriage of Theseus and Phaedra, and her fellow Amazons defended her while Heracles killed them—this clearly resembles a myth and a fabrication.

<margin>note on the marriage of Phaedra</margin> After Antiope died, he married Phaedra, having already a son, Hippolytus, by Antiope. But as Pindar says, the son's name was Demophon. As for the tragedies concerning her and their son, since nothing in her story contradicts the tragic poets...

### The Character and Marriages of Theseus

...as a matter of history, it must be examined in this way, just as all those others have done. <margin>this is babbling</margin> Now, the rest of the writers concerning the marriages of Theseus have others [to tell of], having avoided the theatrical stage. <note>Plutarch refers here to "avoiding the stage" to distinguish historical accounts from the more sensationalized versions found in Athenian tragedies.</note> For they possess neither judgment nor noble authority. It is said that he snatched away unworthy women in "woman-kidnapping," and after killing <gloss>Sciron</gloss> Sinis <note>original: "Σκίρωνα σιννίον" — Two famous bandits Theseus defeated; the manuscript glosses or corrects the names.</note> and Cercyon, he committed violence against their daughters. He is also said to have married Phrisia, the mother of Aias <note>Ajax the Great</note>, and later Phereboia, and also Iope, the daughter of Iphicles. 

And for this reason, he is accused <gloss>of</gloss> women <note>original: "γυναίκων"</note> as has been said, concerning the abandonment of Ariadne <note>Theseus famously left Ariadne on the island of Naxos after she helped him escape the Labyrinth</note>, which was neither honorable nor good. Furthermore, there was the kidnapping of Helen, which filled Attica with war <note>Theseus and Pirithous kidnapped the young Helen of Troy, leading to a retaliatory invasion of Athens by her brothers, the Dioscuri</note>, and ultimately led to his own flight and death. Concerning these matters, I shall speak a little later. 

Though many great deeds were performed by the best heroes, Herodorus <note>A 5th-century BCE historian who specialized in the myths of Heracles</note> thinks that Theseus took part in none of them, except alone with the Lapiths in the battle against the Centaurs. <margin>to participate together with those in the battle at the Lapiths which was the Centauromachy.</margin> Others, however, say that he was both with Jason at Colchis <note>The expedition of the Argonauts</note> and helped Meleager slay the boar. And because of this, there is a proverb: "Not without Theseus." <note>original: "οὐ κἄνευ Θησέως" — A phrase used to describe an indispensable person or an event where a hero's presence is assumed.</note> 

He himself, however, needed no ally, and accomplished many noble labors. And the saying, "This man is another Heracles," <margin>concerning | concerning</margin> <margin>an advocate | a spouse or kinsman</margin> prevailed in his time. <gloss>But</gloss> he also assisted Adrastus <note>The King of Argos</note> in the <gloss>but not</gloss> recovery of those who fell at the Cadmeian city <note>Thebes</note>. This was not done as Euripides <note>The famous playwright</note> depicted in his tragedy, by conquering the Thebans in battle, <gloss>but</gloss> rather <margin>of a difficult race</margin> by persuading them and making a treaty. For most <gloss>historians</gloss> do not say [what Euripides does]. Philochorus <note>An Athenian historian and expert on local religious customs</note> says that this was the first time a treaty was ever made for the recovery of the dead. But Heracles was the first to give back the dead to the enemies, as is written in the accounts concerning Heracles. <gloss>By him</gloss> The deeds <gloss>of Theseus</gloss> are shown clearly for many at Eleutherae, and for the leaders, near Eleusis. And this was a favor Theseus did for Adrastus. <margin>And from which it is the same as he demonstrates having</margin> And they also bear witness to the works of Euripides in the *Suppliant Women*, which <gloss>not</gloss>... in which these things will also be said to have been done. <margin>to those in battle, for those who loved to perform the recovery [of the dead]</margin>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Plutarch's "Life of Theseus," following the discussion of his controversial marriages and the historian Herodorus's accounts. ### The Friendship of Theseus and Pirithous

They <gloss>say</gloss> that his friendship with Pirithous <gloss>Pirithous</gloss> <note>Pirithous was the King of the Lapiths and became Theseus's lifelong companion and partner in several myths, including a disastrous attempt to kidnap Persephone from the Underworld.</note> came about in this way: Pirithous had a very great reputation for strength and courage. Wishing <gloss>therefore</gloss> <gloss>Pirithous</gloss> to test Theseus and take his measure, he drove <gloss>the</gloss> cattle out of Marathon itself. When he learned that Theseus was pursuing him in full armor, he did not flee, but turned back and met him. 

When one saw the other, each admired the other's beauty and was struck by his daring; they <gloss>held back</gloss> <gloss>each</gloss> from battle. Pirithous was the first to stretch out his right hand, calling on Theseus to be the <gloss>judge and auditor</gloss> <note>original: "δικαστὴν καθήκοντα καὶ εὐθύνην" — terms referring to legal officials in Athens who reviewed the accounts of public officers.</note> of the cattle-theft. He said he would willingly submit to whatever penalty the other should set. Theseus, however, released him from the penalty and invited him to be his friend and ally. 

They then confirmed their friendship with an oath. Following this, when Pirithous married Hippodamia <note>The daughter of Atrax or Butes; her wedding became the site of the famous Centauromachy.</note>, he asked Theseus to come and see the country and <gloss>meet</gloss> with the Lapiths. It happened that he had also invited the Centaurs <note>Mythical creatures with the upper body of a human and the lower body of a horse, often representing wildness or lack of self-control in Greek art.</note> to the feast. When, <gloss>through drunkenness</gloss> <margin>the hero’s departure is a fiction</margin> <note>The marginal note "πλαστὴ ἡ μετάστασις τοῦ ἥρωος" appears to be a skeptical scholarly comment on the nature of Theseus's movements or his eventual legendary passing.</note> they turned to insolence and did not keep their hands off the women, the Lapiths turned to defend themselves. Some of the Centaurs they killed; others they overcame in war and later drove out of the country, with Theseus fighting as an ally and comrade-in-arms. 

Herodorus, however, says that these things did not happen quite this way. Instead, he claims that when the war was already underway, Theseus arrived to help the Lapiths. It was then, he says, that Theseus first recognized <gloss>by sight</gloss> Heracles, having performed a great deed, meeting him near Trachis when Heracles had ceased from his wandering and his labors. Their meeting was conducted with honor, friendliness, and much praise from both sides. 

Nevertheless, one might pay attention to <gloss>others indeed</gloss> who often record that they met each other and celebrate the sacred initiation <note>original: "μύησιν" — likely referring to the Eleusinian Mysteries, the most famous secret religious rites of ancient Greece.</note>, which Theseus was eager for. And also the purification before the initiation, which he required because of certain involuntary acts <note>In Greek religion, even accidental or "involuntary" killings required a ritual of "katharmos" (purification) to remove the spiritual stain or "miasma."</note>. At that time, he was fifty years old...

As Hellanicus <note>Hellanicus of Lesbos was a 5th-century BCE Greek historian. Plutarch frequently cites him as a source for early Athenian myths.</note> says, he carried out the affair regarding Helen, not at a suitable age <note>Helen was famously kidnapped by Theseus when she was a young girl, long before the Trojan War. Traditions vary on her age, but some suggest she was only seven or ten years old, while Theseus was already over fifty.</note>. Theseus, along with Pirithous, planned the matter, though some who seek to correct the story say that he did not kidnap Helen himself. Rather,
<margin>i. w</margin>
<margin>following</margin>
Idas and Lynceus <note>Twin brothers and rivals of the Dioscuri.</note> kidnapped her, and having received her as a deposit <note>original: "καταθήκην" — a legal term for property left in trust.</note>, Theseus kept her and would not give her up to the Dioscuri <note>The "sons of Zeus," Castor and Pollux, who were Helen's brothers.</note> when they demanded her. Or even that her father handed her over to him, fearing Enarsphorus the son of Hippocoon, who was trying to force Helen while she was still an infant.
<margin>+ Plutarch</margin>
But the probable account, and the one with the most witnesses, is as follows:
<margin>+ Theseus and Pirithous</margin>
They both went to Sparta, and having snatched the girl while she was <margin>* dancing</margin> dancing in the temple of Artemis Orthia <note>A famous sanctuary in Sparta where ritual dances and initiations took place.</note>, they fled. Since those sent in pursuit did not follow further than Tegea, They found themselves in safety.
<margin>+ Theseus and Pirithous</margin>
And having crossed the Peloponnese, they made a pact that the one who won Helen by lot should have her as his wife, but should assist the other in obtaining another marriage. On these terms they cast lots, and Theseus won; and receiving the maiden, who was not yet of an age for marriage, he brought her to Aphidnae. And having settled his mother <note>Aethra.</note> with her, he entrusted her to Aphidnus, who was a friend, ordering him to guard her and keep the matter secret from others. He himself, returning the favor to Pirithous <margin>+ Theseus</margin>, went abroad with him to Epirus to seek the daughter of Aidoneus, the king of the Molossians.
<margin>Name of the...</margin>
This king gave the name Persephone to his wife, Kore to his daughter, and Cerberus to his dog.
<margin>+ to Aidoneus</margin>
He ordered those who wooed the girl to fight the beast, and the one who conquered it would receive her. When he learned that Pirithous and his companions had come not as suitors but to kidnap her, he arrested them. And he immediately did away with Pirithous by means of the dog, but he imprisoned and guarded Theseus. During this time <margin>Meanwhile, Aidoneus keeping [him]</margin>, Menestheus—the son of Peteos, the son of Orneus, the son of Erechtheus—was the first, they say, to attempt to play the demagogue <note>original: "δημαγωγεῖν" — to lead the common people. Plutarch uses this term to describe the beginning of political populism and factionalism in Athens.</note> by currying favor with the people. And he spoke to the crowd to gain their favor, for he stirred up accusations against the nobles...

[Menestheus] incited them, for they had long been ill-disposed toward Theseus, thinking that they had been stripped of their individual rule and kingdom <note>Theseus’s "Synoikismos" (unification) had forced the local noble families—the Eupatrids—to give up their local sovereignty and move to Athens, which caused significant resentment.</note> in their respective districts, and that he had penned them all together into one city to treat them as subjects <gloss><unclear>he there</unclear></gloss> and slaves. He disturbed and slandered these men, saying they saw only a dream of freedom, but in reality, they were deprived of their homelands and shrines, so that instead of many good, noble, and legitimate kings, they looked toward one master who was an outsider and a stranger. 

These <gloss>certainly rouses a greater matter</gloss> things were further stirred up by Menestheus while the Tyndaridae <note>The Tyndaridae are the "sons of Tyndareus," Castor and Pollux (the Dioscuri), who were Helen's brothers.</note> were pressing their attack. Some <margin><unclear>something of the sort</unclear></margin> say they were entirely persuaded by him to invade. At first, they did no harm, but demanded their sister back. When those <gloss>and Helen</gloss> in the city replied that they neither had her nor knew where she had been left, they turned to war. 

But Academus <gloss>learning by inquiry</gloss> told them the way to her hiding place in Aphidnae <note>A fortified town in northern Attica.</note>. Because of this, honors were given to him while he lived by the Tyndaridae, and many times later when the Lacedaemonians <note>The Spartans, descendants of the people the Tyndaridae ruled.</note> invaded Attica and laid waste to the whole country, they spared the Academy for the sake of Academus. 

However, Dicaearchus <note>A student of Aristotle and a geographer.</note> says that Echedemus and Marathus, who had joined the campaign with the Tyndaridae from Arcadia, were the namesakes: from the one, the place now called the Academy <note>The site where Plato would later found his famous school.</note> was named Echedemia, and from the other, the district and town of Marathon <margin>Polemo</margin> <note>The margin likely refers to Polemon of Athens, a geographer who wrote about Attic monuments.</note>, since he voluntarily offered himself to be sacrificed before the battle in accordance with an oracle. 

So they came to Aphidnae, and having won in battle, they took the place. There they say Alycus fell, the son of Sciron, who was then campaigning with the Dioscuri, and from him the place in the Megarian territory is called Alycus, as his body was buried there. >> "Fighting in the wide-spaced plain of Aphidna,

<note>original: "Τὸν δ' ἐν εὐρυχόρῳ πεδίῳ Ἀφίδνῃ μαρνάμενον, Θησέος ἕνεκ' ἐϋπλοκάμοιο κελεύειν." This verse is attributed to the historian Hereas, describing the struggle for Helen.</note>

It was not likely, however, that while he was present, the gate and the city of Athens itself would have been captured. But when Aphidnae was taken and the people in the city were terrified, Menestheus persuaded the common people to receive the Tyndaridae <note>The Tyndaridae are Castor and Pollux, Helen’s twin brothers, also known as the Dioscuri.</note> into the city and to treat them with friendship. He claimed that they were making war only upon Theseus, who had first used violence, but that they were the benefactors and saviors of everyone else. His words were confirmed by the brothers' own conduct. For although they were masters of everything, they asked for nothing except to be initiated into the Mysteries <note>The Eleusinian Mysteries, the most sacred religious rites of ancient Greece.</note>, since they were no less related to the city than Heracles. This was granted to them, and they were entertained by Aphidnus, who adopted them as his sons, just as Pylius had adopted Heracles <note>In Greek myth, outsiders had to be adopted by a citizen to be eligible for initiation into the Mysteries.</note>. They received divine honors and were addressed as **Anakes** <note>original: "Ἄνακες"</note>, either because of the "truce" <note>original: "ἀνοχὰς" (anochas)</note> that occurred, or because of the "care" and "guardianship" <note>original: "ἐπιμέλειαν καὶ κηδεμονίαν"</note> they exercised in ensuring that no one suffered harm while such a large army was within the walls. For we say that those who care for or guard anything act "carefully" <note>original: "ἀνακῶς" (anakos). Plutarch is providing a linguistic theory for why the brothers are called 'Anakes' or Lords.</note>. It is perhaps for this reason that kings are called **Anaktes** <note>original: "ἄνακτας"</note>. There are others who say they are called **Anakes** because of the "appearance" of stars <note>The brothers were associated with the constellation Gemini.</note>, for the Athenians call the "upward" direction **anekas** <note>original: "ἀνεκὰς"</note> and the "from above" direction **anekathen** <note>original: "ἀνεκαθέν"</note>.

They say that Aethra, the mother of Theseus, was taken captive and carried off to Lacedaemon <note>Sparta</note>, and from there to Troy with Helen. Homer bears witness to this when he says:
>> "Aethra, daughter of Pittheus, and large-eyed Clymene." <<

...at the house of Asterion. In the confusion of affairs caused by Menestheus, Aethra <note>Aethra was the mother of Theseus. She was captured and became a slave to Helen.</note> was led away as a captive, having been caught there. Regarding this, there is much agreement among historians. 

But when Aidoneus <note>original: "Ἀϊδωνεύς". In Greek mythology, this is another name for Hades, god of the Underworld. Plutarch, following a historical rationalization, treats him here as a mortal King of the Molossians.</note> the Molossian was hosting Heracles, he happened to mention the situation of Theseus and Pirithous <note>Theseus and his friend Pirithous had traveled to the land of the Molossians to kidnap the King's daughter (mythologically, Persephone). They were captured and imprisoned.</note>. Heracles took it hard when he learned what they had suffered, as some had been killed and others were being held. Regarding Pirithous, Heracles' pleas were of no avail; but for Theseus, he begged that he be granted his release as a personal favor. 

Aidoneus granted this, and Theseus, being freed, returned to Athens. His friends had not yet been completely overcome by the opposing faction. All the sacred lands <term>temene: plots of land dedicated to a god or hero</term> that previously belonged to him, which the city had set apart for his use, he now dedicated to Heracles. He renamed them **Heracleia** instead of **Theseia**, except for four of them, as the historian Philochorus has recorded. 

But when Theseus wished to rule again and re-establish the government as before, he fell into civil strife and turmoil. He found that those he had left behind now hated him; because they no longer feared him, they had added genuine malice to their feelings. Among the common people, he saw much corruption; they wanted to be courted and flattered by demagogues instead of doing what they were commanded by their leader. 

When he attempted to use force to bring them back to order, he was outmaneuvered by political rivals and factionalism. Finally, despairing of the situation, and fearing for his children, he secretly sent them away to Euboea to Elephenor, the son of Chalcodon. He himself, after pronouncing solemn curses against the Athenians at Gargettus—at the place now called the **Araterion** <term>Araterion: The Place of Cursing</term>—sailed away to the island of Skyros, where he believed he had friends and an ancestral estate. 

Lycomedes was then king of the people of Skyros. Having come to him, Theseus asked to recover his lands, intending to live there. Some say he also asked Lycomedes to help him against the Athenians. But Lycomedes, whether through fear of the reputation of the man...

<margin>Death of Theseus</margin>
Then he died; for Lycomedes <note>Lycomedes was the King of the island of Scyros, where Theseus sought refuge.</note>, leading him up to the high points of the land as if to show him the fields from there, pushed him down the rocks and destroyed him. As for the great name of Rome, and from where it has spread among all mankind—which has been the cause of both envy and glory for the city—historians are not in agreement. For some say that the Pelasgians <note>A term used by ancient Greek writers to refer to the indigenous, pre-Greek inhabitants of the Aegean and Italy.</note> named the city in this way because of their "strength" <note>original: "ῥώμην" (rhōmēn). In Greek, *rhōmē* means strength or might.</note> in arms. Others say that when Troy was captured, some who escaped and found ships were carried by the wind to the land of Tuscany <note>original: "τυρρηνῇ γῇ" (Tyrrhenian land).</note> and arrived at the Tiber River. Their women, being distressed and weary of the sea, were led by one who was of noble birth and seemed the most wise, named Roma; she suggested they burn the ships. When this was done, the men were at first angry. But later, out of necessity, they settled near the Palatine Hill. Within a short time, things turned out better than they hoped, as the land was good and the neighbors received them well. To show great honor to the woman Roma, they called the city after her.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the various legendary origins of Rome. The previous page ended mid-sentence describing the abandonment of children in a vessel on a flooded river. This page provides two distinct versions of the Romulus and Remus story: a supernatural tale involving King Tarchetius and the more widely accepted historical account involving the brothers Numitor and Amulius.</meta>

...that from her would be born a most famous son, distinguished in excellence, fortune, and strength. When Tarchetius told the prophecy to one of his daughters and commanded her to consort with the phallus <note>original: "φαλλῷ"; In this specific local legend, a phantom phallus appeared on the king's hearth, signifying a divine or supernatural omen.</note>, she thought it beneath her and sent a handmaid in her place. Tarchetius, taking the deception poorly, ordered both women to be imprisoned. However, Hestia <note>The goddess of the hearth and home, who oversaw the sacred fire.</note> appeared to him in his dreams and forbade him from the execution; instead, she told him to set the girls to work on a loom, saying they would be given in marriage once they finished the weaving. So they wove during the day; but by night, while Tarchetius slept, others would unravel the web. When the handmaid gave birth to twins from the phallus, Tarchetius ordered them to be killed; but the man carrying them out placed them near the river. A <insert>she-wolf</insert> visited them and gave them her teat, while birds of all kinds brought morsels of food and placed them in the infants' mouths; until at last a certain cowherd saw it and, marveling at the sight, dared to approach and take up the children. Having been saved in such a manner, the children grew up and attacked Tarchetius and overcame him. 

Now, a certain Promathion <note>An early author of an Italian history, whose work is mostly lost to us.</note> has stated these things in his compilation of Italian history. But the most credible account, having the most witnesses and being the most authoritative in its details, was first published among the Greeks by Diocles of Peparethos <note>A Greek historian from the 3rd century BCE who is often cited as the primary source for the Roman foundation myth.</note>; whom Fabius Pictor <note>The earliest Roman historian to write in Greek, often called the "Father of Roman History."</note> followed in most respects. There are, however, other variations regarding this. In outline, the story is this: of the kings descended from Aeneas at Alba <note>Alba Longa, the ancient city in the Alban Hills that predates Rome.</note>, the succession fell to two brothers, Numitor and Amulius. When Amulius divided the <insert>entire</insert> inheritance into two parts—setting the kingdom against the treasure and the gold brought from Troy—Numitor chose the kingdom. Amulius, therefore, possessing the wealth and being more powerful because of it, easily took the kingdom away from Numitor. And fearing...

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the legendary founding of Rome. The previous page detailed a supernatural version of the birth of the twins involving King Tarchetius; this page transitions to the more famous historical account involving Numitor’s daughter and the usurper Amulius.</meta>

From the offspring of his younger brother, Amulius appointed the girl who had been born to him as a priestess of Hestia <note>original: "ἑστίας"; in a Roman context, this refers to a Vestal Virgin, who was bound by a vow of chastity to tend the sacred fire of the state.</note>, decreeing that she live forever unmarried and as a virgin. Some call this woman Ilia, others Rhea, and others Silvia. Not long afterward, she was discovered to be pregnant, contrary to the law established for the priestesses of Hestia. To prevent her from suffering the ultimate penalty <note>original: "ἀνηκέστων" (literally "incurable things"); a euphemism for the death penalty, which for Vestals usually involved being buried alive.</note>, the king’s daughter, Antho, interceded on her behalf by entreating her <term>father</term> <note>original abbreviation: "πρς" (πατρός)</note>, Amulius. Silvia was nonetheless imprisoned and kept under guard, so that her delivery would not escape Amulius’s notice. 

She gave birth to two boys, extraordinary in both their size and beauty. Because of this, Amulius became even more afraid and ordered a servant to take them and cast them away. Some say the name of this servant was Faustulus; others say this was not his name, but rather the name of the man who later picked them up to save them. Having immediately placed the infants into a trough, the man hurried to the river. But seeing the current rushing with great force and turbulence, he was afraid to go further; setting them down near the bank, he departed. 

When the river subsided, the flood took up the trough, lifted it, and carried it gently to a pleasant spot that they now call Cermanum. In ancient times, it seems it was called Germanum; <margin>Say, for</margin> for they call brothers *germani* <note>original: "γερμανούς"; Plutarch is explaining the Latin etymology to his Greek readers, linking the place name to the Latin word for "brothers."</note>. Nearby stands a wild fig tree, which they used to call the Ruminalis. This was named either after Romulus, as most believe, or because the ruminating cattle used to rest there in the shade because of the cool air; or, more likely, because of the suckling of the infants, since the ancients called the teat *ruma* <note>original: "ῥοῦμαν"; another Latin etymological explanation connecting the word for "breast" to the name of the fig tree and the city's founder.</note>. And they call a certain goddess who is thought to watch over the rearing of children Rumina; they offer her wineless sacrifices and make libations of milk. They tell that while the infants lay there, a she-wolf came and suckled them, and a certain woodpecker helped to nourish and protect them. Both these animals are considered sacred to Ares <note>The Greek equivalent of the Roman god of war, Mars, who was the legendary father of the twins.</note>. And the woodpecker...

...and they differ from the bronze statues <note>The author is likely referring to statues of the twins being suckled, which were common in Rome.</note>, and to us it is like... But the story which has gained the longest credit and is most clear is the one claiming the infants were from Ares <note>original: "Ἄρεως"; the Greek name for Mars, the Roman god of war.</note>. Yet they say she suffered this because she was deceived by Amulius, who ravished her while appearing in armor.
<margin>The name.</margin> And as for the flesh... Others, however, say the name of the nurse, through a double meaning, turned into a myth and provided the basis for the rumor. For the Latins used the term **she-wolves** <note>original: "λουπας" (lupae); in Latin, this word meant both the animal and was a slang term for a prostitute, leading to the dual nature of the legend.</note> in their speech for both wild beasts and for those women who lived as courtesans. They say that she was the wife of Faustulus, the man who took up the infants and raised them. Her name was Acca Larentia. To her, the Romans offer sacrifices, and the priest of Ares <note>The Flamen Martialis, one of the high priests of Rome.</note> pours libations to the departed in her honor; they call the festival the Larentia.

And they tell of another Larentia, arising from a cause such as this: the temple keeper <term>neokoros</term> <note>a Greek term for a temple official or warden responsible for the upkeep of a sanctuary; here Plutarch applies it to a Roman context.</note> of Hercules, being idle as it seems due to leisure, proposed to the god that they play dice together. The stakes were that if he won, he would get something good from the god; but if he lost, he would provide the god with an abundant table and a beautiful woman to sleep with him. On these terms, he cast the dice, first for the god and then for himself; he was shown to have lost. He immediately gave what he had promised. And as it was just to abide by the terms, he prepared a dinner for the god and, having hired Larentia—who was in her prime but not known to all—he feasted her in the temple, having spread a couch for her. After the dinner, he locked her in, as if the god truly possessed her. And the myth says the god met with the woman and commanded her at dawn to go out into the marketplace, and whoever she met first, to greet him and make him her friend. There met her then a certain citizen, advanced in years and having gathered sufficient wealth, but childless and having lived without a wife; his name was Tarutius. He became known to Larentia and he loved her. At his death, he left her heir to many...

Even as infants, their physical forms and stature immediately revealed their noble nature by their size and appearance. But Romulus seemed to use his judgment more and to possess a political understanding in his dealings with neighbors regarding pasturing and hunting, giving much evidence of himself as one born for leadership rather than mere subjection. Thus, they were dear to their peers or those of lower station; but as for royal overseers and masters <gloss>leaders</gloss>, whom they considered no different from themselves in virtue, they looked down upon them, caring for neither their threats nor their anger. <meta>This page continues the narrative following the death of the usurper Amulius and the restoration of Numitor to the throne of Alba Longa. # The Life of Romulus

Yet to some, this story seems like a drama and is entirely mythical; however, we should not distrust Fortune, seeing what kind of "poems" <note>original: ποιημάτων (*poiēmatōn*); here Plutarch uses the word for 'poems' or 'literary works' to describe the dramatic events orchestrated by Fate or Fortune.</note> she is the creator of. For [the twins] did not bring about small things, seeing as they did not rest before their power [was established], nor did they receive an ordinary beginning, nor one lacking greatness or the extraordinary. 

Thus, when [the king Amulius] had died and affairs were settled, they did not value the glory of ruling for themselves, nor did they wish to rule while their maternal grandfather [Numitor] was alive. Having restored the leadership to him and paid fitting honors to their mother, they decided to found a city for themselves in the places where they were originally raised. Otherwise, this was the most honorable of reasons: it was necessary to dwell thus because many people had gathered to them all at once, and they would either have to be completely dispersed or live together in a place of their own. 

That the inhabitants of **Alba Longa** did not think it right to mix slaves with themselves, nor to accept them as citizens, was shown first by the bold deed concerning the abduction of the women. This was not done out of insolence or from a voluntary use of force, but from a lack of marriages; for having seized them, they honored them exceedingly. 

Then, as the city was receiving its first foundation, they established a sacred sanctuary for those who fled <note>original: φυξίμιον (*phyximion*); a place of refuge or a sanctuary for fugitives.</note>, which they called the temple of the <term>God of Asylum</term> <note>Asylaeus: a deity presiding over the right of sanctuary.</note>. They received everyone, surrendering neither slaves to masters, nor debtors to creditors, nor murderers to magistrates; instead, they claimed they were confirming the <term>right of sanctuary</term> <note>original: ἀσυλίαν (*asylian*); the origin of our word 'asylum,' meaning protection from seizure.</note> for all by a Pythian oracle. Thus, the city quickly became populous. They say the first hearths numbered no more than a thousand houses. 

But regarding the settlement, there was a dispute about the location. Romulus founded what is called <term>Roma Quadrata</term> <note>Literally "Square Rome," referring to the original four-sided layout of the city on the Palatine Hill.</note>—which means **Square Rome**—and he wished to build the city on that spot. Remus, however, settled a certain strong position on the **Aventine Hill**, which was named *Remonium* after him, but is now called *Remoria*.

<summary>Romulus and Remus decline the throne of Alba Longa to found their own city where they were raised, establishing a sanctuary for fugitives to grow their population. A dispute arises between the brothers regarding the specific location of the new city, with Romulus favoring the Palatine and Remus the Aventine.</summary>

# The Omen of the Vultures and the Death of Remus

Having agreed to settle their dispute by the flight of auspicious birds, and sitting apart from one another, they say that six vultures appeared to Remus, but twice as many appeared to Romulus. Some say that Remus truly saw his birds, but that Romulus lied; however, when Remus arrived at the spot, only then did the twelve appear to Romulus. For this reason, even now, the Romans especially make use of vultures in their auguries <note>original: οἰωνοῖς (*oiōnois*); the practice of interpreting the will of the gods by observing the flight, behavior, and sounds of birds.</note>. 

Herodorus of Pontus records that even **Hercules** was glad when a vulture appeared at the start of an undertaking. For it is the most harmless of all creatures; it damages nothing that men sow, plant, or pasture. Furthermore, it lives on dead bodies, yet it does not kill or harm anything that possesses a soul. Out of a sense of kinship, it does not even approach dead birds, whereas eagles, owls, and hawks strike and kill their own kind while they are still living. As the poet says, "How can a bird that eats a bird be pure?" <note>This is a reference to Aeschylus's *Suppliants*. The vulture's refusal to eat other birds made it a symbol of ritual purity in the eyes of some ancient observers.</note> Furthermore, vultures are a rare sight, so to speak, and it is not easy to find anyone who has happened upon the nestlings of a vulture. This rarity has even led some suspicious people to think that they arrive here from some other distant land. The unusual and infrequent nature of their appearance is what I believe makes them suitable as divine omens; they do not appear by a mere law of nature or of their own accord, but are shown by the gods.

<margin>Toward Celer: the Celeres.</margin>
When Remus realized the trickery, he grew angry and enraged. As Romulus was digging a trench where he intended to encircle the city with a wall, Remus began to mock the work and physically obstructed it. Finally, as he was jumping over the trench in mockery, he died—some say Romulus himself struck him, but others say he fell by the hand of **Celer**, one of Romulus's companions. **Faustulus** <note>original: Φαίσυλος (*Phaisylos*); the shepherd who found and raised the twins.</note> also fell in the battle, as did **Pleistinus**, who was the brother of Faustulus and is said to have helped raise Romulus and his brother. Celer, for his part, moved away to **Etruria** <note>original: Τυῤῥηνία (*Tyrrhēnia*)</note>. From his name, the Romans thereafter called swift and nimble men "*Celeres*." <note>The *Celeres* later became the name of the personal bodyguard of the Roman kings, literally "the swift ones."</note> And as for...

<meta>This page continues the account of the founding of Rome, moving from the omens of the vultures to the death of Remus, the ritual digging of the "mundus," the marking of the city boundaries with a plow, and the establishment of the Parilia festival.</meta>

...of Remus <note>original: Ῥελλου (*Rhellou*); likely a scribal error or local variant for Remus (*Rhemou*).</note>, and his father having died, he himself together with a few groups of gladiators performed the rites. Marvelling at the scale of the preparations, they called him **Celer** <note>Celer was, in some accounts, the man who struck Remus for leaping over the wall; his name later became associated with the *Celeres*, the royal bodyguard.</note>. Nor was **Romulus** discouraged; admiring his foster-parents, he set about settling the city. Having sent for men from the wilderness <note>likely referring to Etruria, as other versions of Plutarch specify Romulus sought Etruscan priests to lead the rituals</note>, who led the way in every detail according to sacred customs and writings and taught the people how to perform each rite. 

For a pit was dug around what is now the **Comitium** <note>The open-air public meeting space of Ancient Rome.</note>, and in a circle were placed the first-fruits of all things which were considered good by law. By nature, as they were things necessary for life, they were deposited there. And finally, each man bringing a small portion of earth from the land where he was born, they threw these in and mixed them all together. They call this pit by the same name as the heavens <note>original: Ὄλυμπον (*Olympon*)</note>: the **mundus** <term>mundus: a Latin word meaning both "world/universe" and "clean/ordered."</term>. Then, as if describing a circle around a center, they marked out the city's boundaries. 

The founder, fitting a bronze plowshare to a plow, and yoking a bull and a cow together, himself drove a deep furrow around the boundaries. It is the task of those following behind to turn inwards the clods of earth which the plow turns up, and to ensure that no clod is left turned outwards. By this line they define the wall; and it is called, by a contraction of words, the **pomerium** <note>From *post moerium*, meaning "behind or beyond the wall."</note>, signifying the space behind or between the walls. Where they intended to place a gate, they pulled out the plowshare and lifted the plow, leaving a gap. For this reason, they consider the entire wall to be sacred, except for the gates. They do not regard the gates as sacred <gloss>because</gloss> they believe it is not possible to be blessed if one cannot send out through them the things that are necessary but not pure <note>i.e., waste and the dead.</note>. 

The founding of the city took place on the day eleven days before the **Calends of May** <note>original: καλανδῶν μαΐων (*kalandōn maiōn*); this corresponds to April 21st.</note>, as is generally agreed. The Romans celebrate this day even now, calling it the "Birthday of the Fatherland." In the beginning, they say, they sacrificed nothing that possessed a soul <note>meaning no living animals were killed.</note>. Rather, they thought it right to keep the festival named after the day pure and bloodless for the sake of the fatherland. However, even before the founding...

...founding <note>original: σεως (*-seos*); the suffix of *ktiseos*, meaning 'of the founding,' continuing from the previous page.</note>. Desiring to align his own festival, both the day and the **Parilia** <note>The festival of Pales, an ancient deity of shepherds, celebrated on April 21st. But that day on which he founded the city, he proclaimed to be exactly <gloss>precisely</gloss> the thirtieth day <gloss>to be sound</gloss>; and that there was a conjunction of the moon with the sun <note>A solar eclipse.</note>. This was also known by **Antimachus**, the epic poet of **Teos**, as having coincided in the year of the [sixth] Olympiad. 

In the times according to **Varro** <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BCE), an immensely productive Roman scholar known for his works on history and language.</note> the philosopher, a man most learned <gloss>exceptionally erudite</gloss> in Roman history, there was **Tarutius**, a companion of his, who was a philosopher as well as a mathematician. From the experience which he once had with the astronomical table <gloss>approach</gloss> method for the sake of theory—and appearing to be the first in that art—Varro proposed to him to calculate the birth of **Romulus** back to the day and hour. He was to make this deduction from the events that once befell the man, including his hardships, just as the solutions to geometric problems provide guidance. 

For it belongs to the same theory: given the time of a man's birth, to predict his life; and given the events of a life, to hunt down the time of birth. Therefore, **Tarutius** did what was commanded; and having looked at the sufferings and the deeds of the man, and his lifespan, and the manner of his death, and having synthesized all such things very well, he concluded that the conception of Romulus in the womb occurred in the first year <margin>28th</margin> of the second Olympiad <note>The Greeks dated history by four-year Olympic cycles; the marginal note "28th" likely refers to the year of Rome's era or a specific calendar day.</note>, in the Egyptian month of **Choeac** <term>Choeac: the fourth month of the ancient Egyptian calendar, used by astronomers for its mathematical regularity.</term>, on the twenty-<insert>twenty</insert><gloss>nd</gloss> second day, at the third hour, during which the sun was totally eclipsed. 

And his natural birth occurred in the month of **Thoth** <term>Thoth: the first month of the Egyptian calendar.</term>, on the twenty-first day, around sunrise. And Rome was founded by him on the ninth day of **Pharmouthi** <gloss>the month of April</gloss> as it began, between the second and third hour. For they believe that a city's fortune, just like a man's, has a sovereign time determined from its first origin, observed according to the positions of the stars. These things may perhaps be spoken of; but for the strange and extraordinary, something more will be added—

...which will trouble those in the <unclear>city</unclear> <gloss>city</gloss> with pestilence <note>original: διαλοιμωδῶσ (*dialoimodous*). This refers to a plague that, according to legend, struck Rome shortly after its founding.</note>, though not to all of them.

Once the city was established, first, as many of the army as were of age <note>Men of military age, usually between 17 and 46.</note>, he divided into a body and companies. Each body consisted of three thousand infantry and three hundred cavalry. This was called a **legion** <note>original: λεγεών (*legeon*); from the Latin *legio*, derived from *legere* meaning 'to choose' or 'select'.</note>. It was so named <gloss>chosen</gloss> because they were the ones selected for combat from the whole population. After this, for the others, they became the "people" <note>original: δῆμοσ (*demos*)</note>, and were called the civic multitude. From the best men, he appointed councillors, and these he called **Patricians** <note>original: πατρικίουσ (*patrikious*). These were the noble families of Rome.</note>, and the body itself he named the **Senate** <note>original: σενᾶτον (*senaton*)</note>. 
<margin>Perhaps from their age</margin>
The word **Senate**, then, signifies a council of elders <note>original: γερουσίαν (*gerousian*). Plutarch explains the Latin term using the Greek word for a council of old men.</note>. Some say the councillors were called **Patricians** because they were the fathers of legitimate children. Others say it was because they were able to point to their own fathers <note>implying they were of noble or free birth</note>, which was not the case for many of those who first flocked to the city. Others say the name comes from "patronage" <note>original: πατρωνείασ (*patroneias*)</note>. 
For so they call the act of protection; and even now the **Romans** use this term. They call a **patron** <term>Patron: a high-ranking protector who provided legal and social aid to a lower-class client</term> one of those who stands over men as a guardian of those more humble. He is a helper from whom the name of the practice is derived. One would most likely hit upon the truth if they thought that **Romulus** believed the first and most powerful men should provide fatherly <note>original: πατρικὴν (*patriken*)</note> guardianship and care for the humbler folk, teaching the commoners not to be afraid or feel any resentment toward the power of their superiors, but to look upon them with affection and address them as fathers; and thus he named them.

For even now in the Senate, while outsiders call them "lords" or "leaders," the Romans themselves call them **Conscript Fathers** <note>original: πατέρας συγγεγραμμένουσ (*pateras syggegrammenous*); the Greek translation of the Latin *Patres Conscripti*.</note>. This title carries the greatest dignity and honor, yet is free from the names that excite envy. At the beginning, they called them simply "Fathers." Later, as more were added to the number, they addressed them as **Conscript Fathers**. By this name, they improved the relationship between the senate and the common people, removing the pride of status...

<summary>Plutarch describes the organization of the Roman military into legions and the political establishment of the Senate and the Patrician class, exploring the etymology of "Patrician" as a role of fatherly protection.</summary>
