<meta>This page continues Plutarch’s "Life of Romulus," moving from the social stratification of the early Roman state to the legendary abduction of the Sabine women. It builds on the previous page's discussion of the Senate and Patricians by defining the relationship between the upper and lower classes.</meta>

...difference of status. He separated the powerful from the common people, calling them **Patrons** <note>original: πάτρωνας (*patronas*); from the Latin *patronus*, implying a father-like protector or legal guardian.</note>, which means protectors. He called the others **Clients** <note>original: πελάτας (*pelatas*); the Greek term refers to 'those who approach' a superior for protection, corresponding to the Roman *cliens*.</note>. At the same time, he instilled in them a kind and wonderful goodwill, combined with a sense of justice. For these patrons acted as interpreters of the laws and defenders for those in legal trouble; they provided themselves as advisors and guardians in all matters. The clients, in turn, served their patrons, not only by showing them honor, but also by helping provide dowries for the daughters of those who were poor and by paying off their debts. No law or magistrate could force a patron to testify against his client, or a client against his patron. In later times, they considered it shameful and ignoble for the powerful to accept a money fee from the lower classes <gloss>those of humbler status</gloss> for these rights.

So much, then, for these matters. In the fourth month after the founding of the city, as Fabius <note>Fabius Pictor, the earliest Roman historian, who wrote his accounts in Greek around 200 BCE.</note> records, the daring act of the abduction of the women took place. Some say that Romulus himself, being warlike by nature and convinced by certain oracles that Rome was destined to reach its greatest height by being nourished and increased through warfare, initiated this conflict against the **Sabines** <note>An ancient Italic tribe and neighbors to the early Romans.</note>. They claim he did not take many women, but only thirty virgins, which looked more like an act intended to provoke a war than a genuine need for marriage. 

But this is not likely. Rather, seeing the city immediately filling up with settlers—a mixed crowd of poor and obscure people whom few neighbors would associate with—he realized they were looked down upon and expected they would not remain together as a stable community. He hoped that this injustice would provide a starting point for a mixture and partnership with the Sabines, if they were to bind the women to them through marriage laws; and so he attempted the deed in the following way.

It is said by Romulus himself that he discovered an altar of a certain god hidden underground. The name of the god was **Consus** <note>An ancient Roman deity associated with the storage of grain and with secret plans.</note>. He was perhaps a god of counsel, for even now the Romans call a deliberative body a **council** <note>original: κονσίλιον (*konsilion*), from the Latin *consilium*. Plutarch is linking the god's name to the word for advice or assembly.</note>...

<summary>This page describes the patron-client social system established by Romulus and details the motivations behind the abduction of the Sabine women, framing it as a strategic move to ensure the city's survival and growth.</summary>
<keywords>Patrons, Clients, Romulus, Sabines, Fabius Pictor, Consus, social classes, abduction of the Sabine women, early Rome</keywords>

They also call the high officials **Consuls** <note>original: κονσούλους (*konsoulous*)</note>, as if they were "counselors" <note>original: προβούλους (*proboulous*)</note>, or perhaps after **Poseidon the Horse-God** <note>original: ἵππιον ποσειδῶνα (*hippion poseidona*)</note>. For his altar still exists in the Great Circus <note>The Circus Maximus</note>; it is hidden and kept underground the rest of the time, only being uncovered during the horse races. Some say that since a council’s deliberations should be secret and truly unseen, it is not unreasonable that the altar remains hidden <unclear>by engraving</unclear> <gloss>he thought</gloss>. Furthermore, **Romulus** <term>Romulus: The legendary founder and first king of Rome.</term> announced a magnificent sacrifice and a series of games. These were held as a public festival to conclude the celebrations. Many people gathered for the event. Romulus himself sat at the front among the nobles, dressed in a purple robe. The signal for the start of the uprising was for him to stand up, fold his purple robe, and then wrap it around himself again. Many of the men had swords and kept their eyes fixed on him. When the signal was given, they drew their swords and rushed forward with a great shout, seizing the daughters of the **Sabines** <note>An ancient Italic people whose territory bordered Rome; the Romans lacked women and sought to take them from their neighbors by force.</note>. They allowed the men to flee and let them escape. They say the women were seized not out of lawless lust, but to fulfill a purpose—from which the name **Thalassios** <term>Thalassios: A traditional Roman wedding cry, though its exact origin was debated; some linked it to a hero of that name who participated in the abduction.</term> is derived. For they did not seize them because they were in love, but later these women and <unclear>everywhere</unclear>... for there were thirty or even six hundred virgins taken. Perhaps he [the historian] signifies by the Roman [word] that he did not take a woman for himself, as there was only one woman he loved whom he had chosen. This act was not an imitation of insolence, nor was it done out of a desire to do wrong. Rather, it was a plan to mix the two peoples and bring the tribes together into one through the strongest of bonds. Regarding **Hersilia** <term>Hersilia: In Roman tradition, she was the only married woman taken, who eventually mediated peace between the Romans and Sabines.</term>, some say she was taken by mistake, though she was for one of the most prominent Romans. Others say she was for **Romulus** himself. --- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues Plutarch’s account of the abduction of the Sabine women, specifically investigating the origins of "Talassio," a traditional cry used at Roman weddings. It transitions from the act of the kidnapping to the etymological and historical theories surrounding this ritual exclamation.</meta>

Among those who were seizing the young women at that time, it happened that some followers of certain prominent men were carrying off a maiden who was far superior to the others in both her beauty and her stature. When they were met by some men of higher rank who attempted to take the girl away from them, those who were carrying her shouted out that they were bringing her to **Talassius** <note>original: Ταλασίῳ (*Talasiō*)</note>, a man who was young, but also well-reputed and worthy. 

Upon hearing this, the others cheered and clapped their hands in approval. Some even turned around and followed along out of goodwill and joy, shouting the name of **Talassius** <note>original: τὸ τοῦ Ταλασίου (*to tou Talasiou*)</note>, and thus the name was transformed into a ritual cry. This is the very "Talassio" that the Romans sing at weddings to this day, just as the Greeks sing the **Hymenaeus** <term>Hymenaeus: The Greek god of marriage and the name of the wedding song sung while escorting a bride.</term>; for they say that Talassius was exceedingly fortunate in his marriage to that woman. 

However, **Sextius Sylla** <note>A historian and friend of Plutarch</note> the Carthaginian—a man lacking neither the favor of the Muses nor the Graces—says that the word was actually a signal established during the abduction. The signal given to those within sight was for everyone carrying off a young woman to shout "Talassio!" <note>original: Ταλάσσιον (*Talassion*)</note>. For this reason, the custom has remained at weddings. 

But most people believe the term refers to wool-working <note>original: ταλασίαν (*talasian*)</note> and the path of domestic duty; it is a summons and an exhortation to industriousness, for Greek names were often mixed into the Italian tongue in those days. If this is not an incorrect claim, and the Romans did indeed use Greek words at that time just as we do, one might guess another more plausible reason. 

For when the **Sabines** <note>The neighboring tribe from whom the women were taken.</note> were reconciled after warring against the Romans, treaties were made regarding the women, stipulating that they should perform no other labor for their husbands except **wool-working** <note>original: ταλασίαν (*talasian*)</note>. 
<margin>wool-working</margin>
It therefore remained a custom for those marrying in later times to shout "Talassio" in a spirit of playfulness, either when giving the bride away or escorting her, calling witnesses to testify that the woman was being brought into the home for no other labor than wool-working <note>original: ταλασίαν (*talasian*)</note>. This also explains the custom that persists even now, where the bride herself is not allowed to step over the threshold of the house, but is instead lifted and carried inside. This is because, in that ancient time, the women were carried in by force.

<summary>Plutarch explores three theories for the Roman wedding cry "Talassio": a specific man named Talassius, a military signal for kidnappers, or a reference to the Greek word for wool-working (talasia) symbolizing a wife's domestic duties.</summary>
<keywords>Talassio, Roman weddings, Sabine women, wool-working, marriage customs, Talassius, Plutarch, domestic labor, thresholds</keywords>

Concerning these matters, I have spoken at greater length in my *Roman Questions* <note>original: ἐν τοῖς αἰτίοις (*en tois aitiois*). The month was renamed in 8 BCE to honor Emperor Augustus.</note>, the day on which they celebrate the festival of the **Consualia** <term>Consualia: An ancient Roman festival in honor of Consus, a god associated with secret counsel and the storage of grain.</term>.

As for the **Sabines**, they were a numerous and warlike people. They lived in unwalled villages, as they felt it was appropriate for them to be proud and fear nothing, being a colony of the **Lacedaemonians** <note>The Spartans, famous for their military prowess and lack of city walls.</note>. Nevertheless, seeing that they were bound by great hostages <note>Referencing their abducted daughters.</note> and fearing for their safety, they sent ambassadors proposing fair and moderate terms: that **Romulus** should return the girls to them and undo his act of violence, and instead act through persuasion and law to establish friendship and kinship between the two peoples. 

But Romulus would not give up the girls; instead, he invited the Sabines to accept a shared partnership. While the other Sabines spent their time deliberating and making preparations, **Acron**, the king of the **Caeninians** <note>original: κινιντῶν (*kinintōn*), likely a scribal variant of *Kaininitōn*</note>—a high-spirited man and skilled in warfare—had held Romulus’s first bold attempts in suspicion. Now, considering what had happened to the women to be a danger to all and intolerable if it were not punished, he was the first to rise up for war. 

He marched against Romulus with a great force, and Romulus marched out to meet him. When they came into sight of one another and had drawn up their ranks in a quiet position, Romulus issued a challenge while the armies stood still in their armor. Romulus then made a vow that if he should conquer and strike his opponent down, he would personally carry and dedicate the man’s armor to **Jupiter** <note>original: τῷ Διὶ (*tō Dii*). The Greek text uses the name of the Greek king of gods, Zeus, to represent the Roman Jupiter Feretrius.</note>. 

Having conquered him, he struck him down, and the enemy army was put to flight after a battle took place. He also took their city; yet, he did not harm those who were captured within it. Instead, he ordered them to demolish their houses and follow...

...e back the women, but instead invited them to dwell in Rome as citizens on equal terms. Now, while this might have increased the <gloss>greatest</gloss> <gloss>trust</gloss> in Rome and made it law-abiding, they were not satisfied. But **Romulus**, wishing to make his victory most pleasing to the goddess and a delightful sight for his citizens, found a <unclear>massive</unclear> oak tree <unclear>growing</unclear> <unclear>in</unclear> the <unclear>camp</unclear>, <unclear>cut it down</unclear>, and shaped it like a trophy. Then, lifting the trophy upright and <unclear>resting</unclear> it on his right shoulder, he marched along, leading a victory hymn <note>original: παιᾶνος (*paianos*). A paean is a song of praise or triumph, originally dedicated to Apollo.</note>, while his army followed in arms, being received by the citizens with joy and wonder. This procession, then, provided the beginning and the inspiration for the **Triumphs**. The trophy was dedicated as an offering named after **Jupiter Feretrius**. For the Romans call "to strike" *ferire* <note>original: τύπτειν, φέρι (*typtein, feri*). Plutarch is explaining the etymology of the epithet 'Feretrius' by linking it to the Latin verb *ferire*.</note>; and Romulus had prayed to strike the man and cast him down. **Varro** <gloss>says</gloss> that the <unclear>spoils</unclear> <note>original: σκῦλα (*skyla*). Refers to the armor stripped from a defeated enemy.</note> are called *opimia* <note>The *spolia opima* or "rich spoils" were the highest honor in the Roman military, won only when a general killed the enemy's leader in single combat.</note>, just as they call abundance *opes*. But others say they are so named from "acting," for a great deed is called *optimum*. For these "spoils of honor" are granted for the dedication by a general who has slain the opposing general. 

And this has happened to only three Roman leaders: first to **Romulus**, for killing **Acron of Caenina**; second to **Cornelius Cossus**, for slaying the Etruscan **Tolumnius**; and finally to **Claudius Marcellus**, for defeating **Britomartus**, king of the **Gauls**. Cossus and Marcellus entered in <unclear>four-horse</unclear> <insert><gloss>triumphal</gloss></insert> chariots, <gloss>on horses</gloss>, carrying the trophies themselves. Others say that **Publicola** was the first to triumph in a chariot.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the early Roman-Sabine conflict. Having established the Triumphal tradition on the previous page, Plutarch now describes the expansion of Roman territory through the defeat of nearby towns and the subsequent Sabine invasion of Rome led by Tatius, focusing on the betrayal of the Capitol by Tarpeia.</meta>

# 7

One can see the statues of **Romulus** in Rome carrying trophies, <margin>The Sabines</margin> and they are all represented on foot. After the relinquishing of treasures, from which the horses <insert>X</insert> of the Sabines <gloss>except that thirty-</gloss> were being prepared, they established peace. The people of **Crustumerium** and **Antemnae**, who were living nearby, marched against the Romans, and a battle took place; they were likewise defeated, and they permitted Romulus to take their cities and divide their land. They even moved themselves to settle in Rome. **Romulus** divided the land among the citizens in a spirit of goodwill. To those who lived there in holy peace, he took away what their fathers had held, yet he allowed them to keep those same lands <note>Plutarch is describing a policy of land redistribution that aimed to integrate the conquered peoples rather than just displace them.</note>. At these events, the rest of the **Sabines**, taking it poorly, appointed **Tatius** as their general and marched against Rome.

The city was difficult to approach, having as a defense the place now called the **Capitol** <note>The Capitoline Hill, one of the seven hills of Rome, which served as the city's citadel.</note>, in which a guard had been stationed. **Tarpeia** was the leader there. She was not the "Tarpeia the Virgin" <note>original: παρθένος (*parthenos*). Some ancient traditions suggested she was a Vestal Virgin, but Plutarch clarifies her parentage here.</note> as some say, portraying Romulus as <unclear>acting</unclear>. Rather, being the daughter of **Tarpeius**, the commander of the hill, she betrayed the **Capitol** to the Sabines. She had a desire for the golden bracelets which she saw them wearing, and for their betrayal, she asked for "what they carry on their left arms." When this was agreed upon by **Tatius**, Tarpeia opened one of the gates by night and received the Sabines.

It seems she was not only as **Antigonus** <note>Likely Antigonus I Monophthalmus, a successor of Alexander the Great.</note> said—that he loved those who were in the act of betraying, but hated them once they had finished. Nor was she like the case mentioned by **Caesar** <note>Referring to Augustus Caesar.</note> regarding the Thracian **Rhoemetalces**, when he said, "I love the treason, but I hate the traitor." <note>original: Φιλεῖν μὲν προδοσίαν, μισεῖν δὲ προδότην (*Philein men prodosian, misein de prodotên*). A famous ancient maxim regarding the utility and subsequent disposal of informants.</note> Instead, this is a common feeling that people have toward the wicked when they need them: just as they have need of the venom and gall of poisonous beasts. They welcome the fortune they bring while they are useful, but they loathe the malice of the deed itself. 

This, then, was how **Tatius** felt toward **Tarpeia**. He ordered the **Sabines**, remembering their agreement, to grudge her nothing of what they wore on their left arms. He was the first to take the bracelet from his arm <insert>X</insert> and he also threw his shield at her. All of them did the same. Pelleted by the gold and buried <note>A grim irony: the Sabines wore both gold jewelry and heavy shields on their left arms. By giving her "what was on their left arms," they crushed her to death under the weight of their shields.</note>—

<meta>This page continues the account of Tarpeia's betrayal of the Roman citadel to the Sabines. ...crushed by the shields, by their sheer number and weight, she died. **Tarpeius** also was convicted of treason by **Romulus**, as **Juba** <note>original: Ὄβας (*Obas*). Juba II was a scholarly king of Numidia and Mauretania who wrote histories in Greek.</note> says in his history. **Sulpicius Galba** also records this; however, the stories told about **Tarpeia** are altogether incredible. Some historians say she was the daughter of **Tatius**, the leader of the **Sabines**, and that she was forced to live with **Romulus**, but did these things and suffered this fate at the hands of her father; **Antigonus** is one of these writers. **Simylus** the poet is also completely implausible, as he believes that **Tarpeia** betrayed the **Capitol** not to the **Sabines**, but to the **Celts**, having fallen in love with their king. He says the following:

<margin>></margin> -> And **Tarpeia**, dwelling near the Capitoline hill, <-
<margin>></margin> -> became the cause of Rome’s downfall, <-
<margin>></margin> -> she who, desiring to reach the marriage bed of the **Celts**, <-
<margin>></margin> -> did not guard the homes of her sceptered father: <-

-> and a little later, concerning her death: <-

<margin>></margin> -> Neither the **Boii** nor the countless tribes of **Celts** <-
<margin>></margin> -> cast her into the currents of the **Po**; <-
<margin>></margin> -> but they, with the weight of their warlike hands, <-
<margin>></margin> -> laid a murderous fate upon the hateful girl: <-

<note>Simylus’s account is historically confused, conflating the Sabine conflict of the 8th century BCE with the Gallic/Celtic sack of Rome in 390 BCE.</note>

Since **Tarpeia** was buried there, the hill was named **Tarpeian**. This lasted until King **Tarquinius** <note>Likely Tarquinius Superbus, the last king of Rome, who built the Great Temple of Jupiter.</note> dedicated that place to **Jupiter**, at which time her remains were moved and the name of **Tarpeia** fell out of use for the hill itself. However, they still call the cliff on the **Capitol** the **Tarpeian Rock**, from which they used to throw criminals. 

With the citadel held by the **Sabines**, **Romulus** challenged them to a battle in the valley below, and **Tatius** accepted the challenge boldly, seeing that they had a clear path of retreat if they were defeated. For the space between the hills where they were to clash, being surrounded by many heights, would provide an acute and difficult struggle for both sides due to the rough terrain, and only narrow avenues for flight.

<meta>This page continues Plutarch’s "Life of Romulus." The previous page concluded the story of Tarpeia’s betrayal and the Sabine occupation of the Roman citadel. This page describes the subsequent battle in the valley between the hills (the future Roman Forum), focusing on the geological hazards of the site, the legend of the Lacus Curtius, and Romulus’s prayer to Jupiter.</meta>

->+<-

And you would deem them fortunate; for had the river not flooded <note>original: ἐμνήσατο (*emnēsato*), literally "remembered," a poetic way of saying the river overflowed its banks.</note> not many days before, retreating to leave the ground wonderfully revealed yet treacherous <note>original: τυφλὸν (*typhlon*), literally "blind." This implies the ground looked solid but was actually a deep mire.</note> amidst seemingly level plains—and the existing path was not clear to the eye, nor was it easy to guard against—it was already difficult and deceptive <note>original: ὕπουλον (*hypoulon*), literally "festering under a scar," describing ground that looks firm but collapses under weight.</note>. Into this, the **Sabines**, moving forward with inexperience, met with a stroke of fortune. For a man named **Curtius** <note>Mettius Curtius, a Sabine champion.</note>, distinguished in reputation and proud in spirit, who was on horseback, was advancing far ahead of the others. When the chasm <note>A boggy pit in the Forum area.</note> swallowed his horse, he tried for a time to drive it out by using his whip and shouts of encouragement. But since it was impossible, he abandoned the horse and saved himself. Therefore, that place is named the **Lacus Curtius** <note>original: κούρτειος λάκκος (*kourteios lakkos*), the "Curcian Pit" or "Lake Curtius."</note> after him.

The **Sabines**, having avoided the danger, fought a fierce battle, yet it remained indecisive. And yet many fell, among whom was **Hostilius** <note>Hostus Hostilius, the Roman champion.</note>. They say this man of courage and virtue was the grandfather of that **Hostilius** <note>Tullus Hostilius, the third king of Rome.</note> whom we learn reigned after **Numa**. Furthermore, as many struggles occurred together in a short time, as is likely, they remember only one—the last one. In this fight, they say **Romulus** was struck by a stone <note>original: πληγῇ (*plēgē*).</note>, and being nearly on the verge of falling and ceasing to hold his ground, the **Romans** gave way to the **Sabines**.

And they began to flee toward the **Palatine Hill**, being pushed out of the level ground. But **Romulus**, recovering from the blow, wished to return to arms and oppose those who were fleeing. Shouting loudly, he exhorted them to stand and fight. But as the flight encompassed them on all sides and no one dared to turn back, he stretched his hands toward heaven and prayed to **Jupiter** <note>original: Διὶ (*Dii*), referring to Zeus/Jupiter.</note> to stay the army and not to look upon the Roman cause as it fell, but to set it upright. When the prayer was made, a sense of shame before their king took hold of many, and courage suddenly returned to those who were fleeing. They made their first stand where the **Temple of Jupiter Stator** <note>original: Διὸς Στάτορος ναὸς (*Dios Statoros naos*). "Stator" means "the stayer" or "the stopper," commemorating the god's role in stopping the Roman retreat.</note> is now established.

<summary>This page describes the Battle of the Lacus Curtius, where the Sabine leader Curtius narrowly escapes a bog, and Romulus rallies his retreating troops by praying to Jupiter, leading to the vow of the Temple of Jupiter Stator.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Plutarch’s "Life of Romulus." The previous page described the battle in the valley and the legend of the Lacus Curtius. ...whom one might describe as standing still, then creating a silence again, as much as those who are carried toward the ships from the stadiums <note>original: σταδίοις (*stadiois*). And there was the temple of **Vesta** <note>The goddess of the hearth; her temple was a central point in the Roman Forum.</note>. For all the **Sabine women** were seen coming from the open air with cries and shouting and the tearing of their garments, running through the clash of weapons and the piles of the dead. As if possessed by a divine spirit <note>original: κάτοχοι (*katochoi*), meaning "held" or "possessed," often used for prophetic or Dionysian frenzy.</note>, they fell upon their husbands and toward their fathers. Some carried infant children in their arms; others held out their loosened hair in supplication; and all called out using the dearest names, now to the **Sabines**, and now to the **Romans**. Both sides were moved to pity, and they made way for the women to stand in the middle of the battle lines. A wailing sound passed through the entire assembly, and there was great compassion at both the sight of them and their words. Finally, one of them <note>Historically identified as **Hersilia**, the wife of Romulus.</note> spoke with a mixture of reasoned justice and boldness. For they said:

<meta>This page continues the speech of Hersilia, the leader of the Sabine women, who has intervened in the battle between the Romans (their husbands) and the Sabines (their fathers). > > "...as this war is named. Take us back along with our sons-in-law and our children, and give back to us our fathers and kinsmen. Do not begrudge us our children and our husbands, we implore you, so that we do not become captives once again." While **Hersilia** <note>Hersilia was the wife of Romulus and the only married woman among the original Sabine captives.</note> made many such pleas, and the other women made their own requests, the leaders were persuaded and came together for negotiations. Meanwhile, the women brought their husbands to meet their fathers and brothers, and they brought food and drink to those who needed it. They also carried the wounded home to tend to them. They demonstrated that they were the mistresses of their own households, and their husbands were attentive to them, granting them every honor with great goodwill. It was further decreed that those women who wished to remain living with their husbands should be exempted from all labor and every form of servitude, except for **wool-working** <note>original: ταλασίας (*talasias*). In Roman culture, spinning and weaving wool was the mark of a virtuous and high-status matron.</note>. The city was to be inhabited jointly by the **Romans** and the **Sabines**, and the city was to be named **Rome**, after **Romulus**, but all the citizens were to be called **Quirites** <note>original: κυρίτας (*kyritas*). This name was taken from Cures, the home city of the Sabine leader Tatius.</note> after the homeland of **Tatius**. They were to rule jointly and lead the army together. The place where they first came together was to be called the **Comitium** <note>original: κωμίτιον (*kōmition*). Plutarch derives this from the Latin verb for 'coming together.'</note>; for the Romans call the act of meeting together *comire*. With the size of the city now doubled, one hundred **Patricians** <note>The aristocratic class of Rome.</note> were added from among the Sabines. The **legions** <note>The main military unit of the Roman army.</note> came to consist of six thousand infantry and six hundred cavalry. They established three **tribes**: those following Romulus they named the **Ramnenses**; those following Tatius they named the **Tatienses**; and the third group they named the **Lucerenses**, after the **grove** <note>original: ἄλσος (*alsos*). Plutarch is referencing the Latin word *lucus*, meaning a sacred grove.</note> into which many had fled for sanctuary and thus shared in the citizenship. They call such groves **lucus** <note>original: λούκους (*loukous*). Plutarch explains the Latin term for a grove to his Greek readers.</note>. That there were this many tribes is evidenced by their name; for they call tribes **tribus** <note>original: τρίβους (*tribous*). Plutarch notes the connection between the number three (*tri-*) and the word for tribe.</note>, and the leaders of the tribes they call **tribunes**. Each tribe contained ten **phratries** <note>A 'phratry' or *curia* was a smaller social and political subdivision of the tribe.</note>, which some say were named after those famous women. This seems to be...

...is a lie. For many of the wards take their names from localities. Yet, at the same time, they did grant many privileges to the women to show them honor. For example, they include these: that men must give way to women when they meet on the road; that it is shameful to speak any indecency <note>original: γαμητῆ (*gamētē*). While literally referring to marriage, in this context it implies the use of foul or sexually explicit language that would be disrespectful to a matron's dignity.</note> in the presence of a woman; that no man should be seen naked by them; and that they should not be summoned to appear before judges in cases involving murder. 

Their children were also permitted to wear the so-called **bulla** <note>The *bulla* was a protective amulet, often made of gold for the wealthy, worn by free-born Roman boys until they reached manhood.</note>, which takes its name from its shape, as it resembles a bubble; they also wore a purple-bordered cloak. This was a custom previously reserved for kings, but it did not immediately come from them...

These steps, also known as the *Scala Caci*, led from the Palatine down toward the Circus Maximus.</note>. These are located near the descent to the **Circus** <note>The Roman chariot racing track.</note> from the Palatine. 

In this place, they say the sacred **cornel tree** <note>term: κρανεία (*kraneia*). The cornelian cherry tree was highly valued in antiquity for its incredibly hard wood, often used for making spear shafts.</note> once grew. The legend goes that Romulus, testing his strength, threw a spear from the **Aventine Hill**; the shaft of this spear was made of cornel wood. When the spearhead sank deep into the earth, no one was able to pull it out, although many tried. The earth, being full of life, gave birth to the wood and sent up shoots, eventually nourishing a large and sturdy trunk of a cornel tree. 

Those who lived after Romulus guarded and venerated this tree as one of the most sacred of objects, and they built a wall around it to protect it. If any person approaching it thought that it did not look vigorous or green, but appeared to be withered and failing, he would immediately alert those he met with a shout. Then they, as if running to put out a fire, would cry, "Water! Water!" and people would run from all directions, carrying vessels full of water to the spot. 

However, they say that when **Gaius Caesar** <note>Julius Caesar.</note> was repairing the steps and the craftsmen were digging in the vicinity, the roots were accidentally damaged entirely, and the plant withered away. Thus, the **Sabines** were joined with the **Romans**. And regarding them, all that was noteworthy has been written in the...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the unification of the Romans and Sabines under Romulus and Tatius. It transitions from the social privileges granted to women to the establishment of religious festivals and the etymological origins of Roman traditions, specifically the Carmentalia and the Lupercalia.</meta>

-> + <-

...preserved the name. Romulus also made use of their [the Sabines'] shields, and he changed the weaponry of both them and the Romans. For previously, the Romans used small shields. They also shared sacrifices with one another, not abolishing those which the tribes had before, but establishing new ones. Among these is the festival of the **Mabortia** <note>original: Μαβορτίων (*Mabortiōn*). This likely refers to a festival associated with Mars (Mavors), or perhaps the Matronalia, as Plutarch notes it was established at the cessation of the war.</note>, which seems to resemble the others, being founded upon the end of the war.

And then there is the festival of the **Carmentalia**. Some believe **Carmenta** is a Fate <note>original: μοῖραν (*moiran*)</note> who presides over human birth, which is why mothers honor her. Others, however, say she was the wife of the Arcadian [Evander], a prophetess and divinely inspired woman who gave rhythmic oracles, and they say she was named Carmenta because the Romans call verses *carmina* <note>original: κάρμινα. This is the Latin *carmina*, meaning songs or poems.</note>. Today, it is generally agreed that this is her proper name. 

But some interpret "Carmenta" in another way, explaining it as "deprived of mind," due to the wild frenzies she experienced during her divine inspirations. For the Romans use the word *carere* <note>original: καρέραι (*karerai*). Latin: *carere* - to be without or to lack.</note> to mean "to be deprived," and they call the mind *mentem* <note>original: μέντε (*mente*). Latin: *mens* - mind.</note>. I have spoken previously about the "mock suns."

Regarding the **Lupercalia**: by its timing, it would seem to be a rite of purification. It is performed during the "ill-omened" days of the month of February <note>original: Φεβρουαρίου (*Febrouariou*)</note>, a name which one might interpret as "the purifying month." Indeed, in ancient times, they called that day *Febrata*. The name of the festival, in Greek, means "Wolf-Festival" <note>original: λύκαια (*lykaia*)</note>. For this reason, it seems to be extremely ancient, brought from the Arcadians who came with Evander. This is the common view; however, the name could also have come from the she-wolf [who nursed Romulus and Remus]. For we see the **Luperci** <note>The priests of Lupercus who ran the ceremonial circuit.</note> beginning their ceremonial run from the very spot where the myth says the infant was exposed. 

The rituals performed, however, make the original cause difficult to discern. They sacrifice goats; then, two youths of noble birth are brought to them. Some touch the foreheads of these youths with a bloodied knife, while others immediately wipe the blood away, applying wool soaked in milk. The youths must laugh after the—

<meta>This page continues Plutarch’s exploration of early Roman religious institutions. Having just mentioned the Carmentalia, the text now transitions into the specific rituals and origin myths of the Lupercalia, including the sacrifice of dogs and the appointment of the first Vestal Virgins.</meta>

->+<-

...to wipe it away. After this, using the wool <note>original: ἰούλου (*ioulou*). In this ritual, the blood on the foreheads of the youths was wiped away with wool dipped in milk.</note>, and having cut up the skins of the goats, they run through the city naked, except for a loincloth, striking those in their way with the leather thongs. Women of child-bearing age do not avoid being struck, believing that this helps toward easy childbirth and conception.

A peculiar feature of the festival is the sacrifice of a dog to the **Luperci** <note>The Luperci were the "Brothers of the Wolf," the priests who presided over the Lupercalia.</note>. Someone, recording mythical causes in the works of certain mediocre Roman poets, says that Romulus and his followers, after defeating **Amulius** <note>The usurper king of Alba Longa who tried to kill the infant twins.</note>, ran with joy to the spot where, when they were infants, the she-wolf offered her teat; and the festival is celebrated as a representation of that run. These descendants run through the streets, striking those in their way, just as Romulus and Remus then ran from **Alba** <note>Alba Longa, the ancient city that preceded Rome.</note> with their swords drawn.

<margin>></margin> But **Butas** <note>A Greek poet who wrote about Roman origins.</note> says... or what of Remus? And he [Remus] placed a single burden on his shoulder, a symbol of murder and danger. They say the purification by means of milk is a reminder of their nourishment [by the wolf].

<margin>></margin> **Gaius Acilius** <note>A Roman senator and historian who wrote in Greek.</note> records that before the founding of the city, the cattle of Romulus and his followers went missing. After praying to **Faunus** <note>An ancient Italian god of the wilderness and fertility.</note>, they ran out naked to search for them, so that they might not be stained by sweat. And for this reason, the Luperci run about naked.

As for the dog, one might say that if the sacrifice is a **purification** <note>original: καθαρμός (*katharmos*)</note>, the dog is sacrificed because they are using it as a purifying agent. For the Greeks also, in their rites of purification, carry out puppies, and in many places use what are called "purifications by puppies" <note>original: περισκυλακισμοῖς (*periskylakismois*). This was a ritual where a puppy was rubbed against a person or object to absorb impurity.</note>. But if they perform these rites as thanks and payment for the nursing and preservation of Romulus by the she-wolf, the slaughter of the dog is not out of place, for it is an enemy to wolves; unless, by Zeus, the animal is punished for annoying the Luperci when they run about.

It is also said that Romulus first established the holy rites concerning the sacred fire, appointing sacred virgins, who are called **Vestals** <note>original: ἑστιάδας (*hestiadas*), referring to the priestesses of Vesta (Hestia).</note>. Others, however, attribute this to **Numa** <note>Numa Pompilius, the second king of Rome, traditionally credited with most religious reforms.</note>. But history records that these holy things were established because Romulus was a god-fearing man.

<meta>This page continues from the discussion of early Roman rites and the founding figures, specifically moving from the Lupercalia festival into the symbols of religious authority and the civil laws established by Romulus. ...in a different way; it is also **prophetic**. They wear for the purpose of prophecy the so-called **lituos** <note>original: λίτυον (*lituon*). A curved staff without knots used by Roman augurs to mark out the ritual space in the sky for observing omens.</note>. This is a curved staff, which the **augurs** <note>The Roman priests who interpreted the will of the gods by observing the flight of birds or other natural signs.</note> use when sitting to mark out the signs from the heavens <note>original: διοσημεῖα (*diosemeia*), literally "signs from Zeus/Jupiter."</note>. They say this staff was carefully guarded, but disappeared around the time of the **Gallic capture** <note>original: τὰ κελτικὰ (*ta keltika*). This refers to the Sack of Rome by the Gauls in 390 BCE.</note> of the city. However, after the barbarians were driven out, it was found buried deep under the ashes by the fire, among the ruins and destroyed things; it appeared **new** and untouched by the flames.

He [Romulus] also established certain **laws**; among them, a severe one is that which does not allow a wife to leave her husband. However, it allows a husband to dismiss his wife for **poisoning children** <note>This likely refers to abortion or infanticide.</note>, or for **substituting keys** <note>original: κλειδῶν ὑποβολῆι (*kleidon hypobole*). This refers to a wife surreptitiously making copies of her husband's keys to steal from the household storerooms, particularly the wine cellar, which was often forbidden to women.</note>, or for committing adultery. If anyone should send away his wife for any other reason, one part of his property belongs to the wife, and the other he commands to be **sacred to Demeter** <note>Plutarch uses the Greek name "Demeter" for the Roman goddess Ceres.</note>. Furthermore, the man who sells his wife is to be sacrificed to the **chthonic gods** <note>Gods of the underworld. This implies the man becomes an outcast or is "devoted" to death.</note>. 

A peculiar thing was that he set no specific penalty against **parricides** <note>Those who kill their parents.</note>. Instead, he defined all homicide as "parricide," considering the former to be a clear crime, but the latter to be **impossible**. For a long time, he seemed to have correctly judged such a crime to be unthinkable, for no one committed such a deed in Rome for nearly six hundred years. But the first recorded after the **Hannibalic War** <note>original: ἀννιακὸν πόλεμον (*anniakon polemon*). The Second Punic War (218–201 BCE).</note> is said to be **Lucius Hostius** <note>original: λεύκιος ὁ στίος (*Leukios ho Stios*).</note>, the parricide. 

These things then are sufficient regarding Romulus. But in the fifth year of the reign of **Tatius** <note>Titus Tatius, the Sabine king who ruled jointly with Romulus.</note>, certain kinsmen and relatives of his, meeting on the road ambassadors from **Laurentum** <note>An ancient city of Latium.</note> who were walking to Rome, attempted to take their money by force. When the ambassadors would not give it up but defended themselves, the kinsmen killed them. Since this terrible deed had been dared, Romulus immediately thought it necessary to seize the wrongdoers. But Tatius warded this off and diverted the legal process. This alone was the cause for them of a **manifest disagreement**; in all other matters, they ordered themselves as law-abiding rulers and managed affairs together in harmony. <insert>⸓</insert> But for the sake of the slain <insert>⸓</insert> they lay excluded from all legal justice...

<meta>This page continues the biography of Romulus, focusing on the period of joint rule with the Sabine king Tatius. It follows the establishment of early Roman laws and transitions into the political shifts caused by Tatius's assassination and Romulus's subsequent military expansions.</meta>

...at a council meeting, they fell upon Tatius and killed him, while showing reverence to **Romulus**. Tatius, however, they sent away with words of praise, regarding him as a just man. Romulus then brought the body back and buried it in **Laurentum** <note>original: Λαυρέντῳ (*Laurentō*). A city in ancient Latium, southwest of Rome.</note>. He now lies near the place called the **Armilustrium** <note>original: ἁρμιλούστριον (*armilustrion*). A site on the Aventine Hill where the Roman army’s weapons were ritually purified at the end of the campaigning season.</note>. As for the trial for his murder, it was entirely neglected.

Some historians report that the people of Laurentum had previously asked for the killers of Tatius to be handed over, but Romulus released them, saying that **murder was atoned for by murder** <note>original: φόνον φόνῳ λύεσθαι (*phonon phonō luesthai*). A reference to the idea that Tatius’s death was a divine retribution for his relatives' earlier crimes.</note>. This gave some people cause for suspicion that he was pleased by what had happened to his kinsman, as it meant he was rid of his co-ruler <margin>me</margin>. However, this did not disturb political affairs or cause the **Sabines** to revolt. Instead, some out of goodwill toward him, some out of fear of his power, and others treating him as if he were a god, continued to admire him with complete devotion.

Many people outside of the city also admired Romulus. The ancient **Latins** <note>The neighboring tribes in the region of Latium.</note> sent envoys to him and established friendship and alliance. He also captured **Fidenae**, a city neighboring Rome. As some say, he sent his cavalry suddenly and ordered them to cut through the **hinges** <note>The OCR reads τροφὰς (*trophas*, nourishment), but the historical text is στροφὰς (*strophas*, hinges or pivots). This refers to a tactic to make the city gates collapse.</note> of the gates; then he himself appeared unexpectedly. 

Others say that the people of Fidenae attacked first, driving off plunder and greatly insulting the countryside and the suburbs. Romulus led an ambush against them, destroyed many of their men, and took the city. He did not, however, destroy it or raze it to the ground; instead, he made it a **colony** <note>original: ἀποικίαν (*apoikian*). A settlement where Roman citizens were sent to live, serving as a permanent garrison.</note>, sending two thousand five hundred settlers there on the **Ides of April** <note>original: εἰδοῖς ἀπριλλίαις (*eidois aprilliais*). The 13th of April in the Roman calendar.</note>. 

After this, a **plague** <note>original: λοιμός (*loimos*). Ancient historians often linked sudden natural disasters or plagues to political murders or unresolved blood-guilt.</note> broke out, bringing sudden deaths to the people without any prior sickness. It also affected the land, causing a failure of crops, and led to the barrenness of livestock. It even **rained blood** in the city, so that great superstition was added to the unavoidable sufferings. And since also...

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Romulus’s reign following the death of his co-ruler Tatius. It details the divine retribution via plague, the subsequent military campaigns against Cameria, and the beginning of the conflict with the powerful Etruscan city of Veii.</meta>

Since such events had occurred, things did not proceed smoothly. It seemed to everyone that the divine anger of the gods was driving both cities because of the blood shed at the grave of the righteous and the murdered ambassadors. When the murderers were finally handed over and punished by both sides, the terrors visibly abated. Romulus then purified the cities with rituals; historians record that these purifications are still performed today at the **Ferentine Gate** <note>original: Φερελίνης πύλης (*Pherelinēs pylēs*). Historically known as the Porta Ferentina in Rome, named after the goddess Ferentina, whose grove was a meeting place for the Latin League.</note>.

But before the pestilence had ceased, the people of **Cameria** <note>An ancient city of the Latins that frequently clashed with early Rome.</note> attacked the Romans and overran their territory, thinking they were unable to defend themselves due to the suffering caused by the disease. Romulus immediately marched against them and, prevailing in battle, killed six thousand of them. He captured their city and relocated half of the survivors to Rome. 

Of the remaining inhabitants, he settled twice that number of Roman citizens in the city of Cameria, <gloss>in no more than twenty years, or in the third year after it <unclear>not</unclear> was founded</gloss> <note>The glossator is debating the chronology of Rome's expansion; the "third year" likely refers to the years following the city's foundation.</note>. Romulus had such a surplus of citizens that he could nearly double the population of Rome while still colonizing elsewhere. Among the spoils of his triumph, he also brought a bronze **four-horse chariot** <term>chariot: quadriga, a vessel or vehicle drawn by four horses abreast, often used in Roman triumphs.</term> from Cameria. This he set up in the temple of **Hephaestus** <note>The Greek name for the Roman god Vulcan. The "Volcanal" was one of the oldest shrines in the Roman Forum.</note>, including a statue of himself being crowned by **Victory**. 

As his power grew in this way, the weaker neighbors yielded, being content to find safety rather than harm. But the powerful ones, fearing and envying him, thought they should not overlook <gloss>Romulus</gloss> or allow his expansion to continue, and decided to check his progress. The first of the **Etruscans** <note>original: Τυῤῥηνῶν (*Tyrrhēnōn*). This was not only unjust but also absurd, for they had not helped the people of Fidenae when they were in danger and under attack; instead, they had allowed the men to perish and then demanded the houses and land from those who now held them. They pressed upon the army at **Fidenae** <note>An ancient town situated north of Rome on the Tiber, often a rival in the city's early history.</note>. From the direction of Rome, those who held the advantage at Fidenae killed two thousand Romans. However, being defeated by Romulus, they lost over eight thousand men. Once again, they fought near Fidenae. It is said that the greatest part of the work was Romulus’s own, as he displayed every kind of daring. He seemed to have utilized a strength and speed that were superior and beyond human nature, a fact which all historians agree upon.

However, what is said by some is entirely mythical: that out of fourteen thousand who fell, more than half were killed by Romulus’s own hand. In this, the **Messenians** <note>Inhabitants of Messenia in the Peloponnese.</note> seem to boast when they say that **Aristomenes** <note>A legendary hero of the Second Messenian War against Sparta.</note> offered the **sacrifice for a hundred kills** <term>sacrifice for a hundred kills: original ἑκατομφόνια (*hekatomphonia*), a ritual sacrifice offered by a warrior who had personally slain one hundred enemies in battle.</term> three times against the **Lacedaemonians** <note>The Spartans.</note>.

The inhabitants did not resist after such a great disaster occurred, but instead begged for terms and made a treaty of friendship for one hundred years. Among them was the leader of the **Veientes** <note>The people of the Etruscan city of Veii.</note>, an old man who was thought to have handled affairs foolishly and without experience, unbecoming of his age. Because of this, even now when they offer sacrifices for victory, they lead an old man through the **Forum** <note>The central public square of Rome.</note> to the **Capitol** <note>The Capitoline Hill, Rome's religious citadel.</note> wearing a purple robe and having a child’s **bulla** <term>bulla: a protective amulet or locket worn by free-born Roman children; here it is used to mock the elderly general's lack of wisdom.</term> <gloss>hanging</gloss> around him. This was the last war that Romulus fought. Afterward, he suffered what many—indeed, nearly all—suffer who are raised up by great and unexpected successes...

The leader of those near Rome sent a herald, having deliberated <gloss>with the</gloss> Latins; he ordered the Romans to send their women to the coast, since their kinship had already faded away <note>The Latins claimed kinship with the Romans through the early marriages of the founding generation, but felt these ties were no longer respected.</note>. He demanded that by mixing the races again through new marriages, they should send both virgins with their relatives and the women who were widows, so that there would be peace and friendship between them, just as had existed previously under similar conditions. 

When the Romans heard this, they feared the war, yet they considered the surrender of their women to be no better than being taken into captivity. While they were in this state of doubt, a certain handmaid—called Philotis, or as some say, Tutola—advised them to do neither. Instead, she suggested that by using a trick, they might escape both the war and the giving of hostages. 

The trick was as follows: they were to dress Philotis herself and all the other most attractive handmaidens as if they were free-born ladies, and send them to the enemies. Then, during the night, Philotis would raise a torch. The Romans would then attack with their weapons and fall upon the enemies while they were asleep. 

These terms were accepted, and the Latins were persuaded. Then Philotis held up the fire, having screened it from behind with covers and hangings made from a wild fig tree <note>original: "ἐρινεός" (erineos). ### Comparison of Theseus and Romulus

<gloss>It is</gloss> now time for the contest of memory regarding the things that have occurred <gloss>to us</gloss> concerning Romulus and Theseus. 

First, it appears that Theseus <gloss>Theseus</gloss> acted from his own choice, with no one forcing him, when it was possible for him to rule as king without fear and having inherited a realm that was not without glory. He himself, of his own accord, reached out for great deeds. But Romulus <gloss>Romulus</gloss>, because of the flight from current slavery and the punishment being brought upon him, became brave—simply as in Plato's hypothesis <note>A reference to Plato’s idea that necessity often drives virtuous action.</note>—and through the fear of suffering the worst extremes, he was brought by necessity to perform great deeds. 

Furthermore, regarding Theseus <gloss>Theseus</gloss>, his greatest achievement was the slaying of one of the tyrants of the world. For Romulus <gloss>of Romulus</gloss>, these were merely side-projects and preliminary contests: the slaying of Sciron, Sinnis, Procrustes, and the Club-bearer <note>These were famous bandits and highwaymen Theseus defeated on his journey to Athens.</note>. By destroying and punishing these men, he delivered Greece from terrible tyrants before those who knew them could even be saved by him. 

And while it was possible for Theseus to be carried across the sea without trouble, suffering no wrong from pirates, it was not possible for Romulus to avoid trouble while Amulius was still living. A great proof of this is that Theseus, even though he himself was not being wronged, set out to take vengeance upon the wicked; whereas the others <note>Referring to Romulus and Remus</note>, as long as they themselves were not suffering evil from the tyrant, they overlooked the fact that everyone else was being wronged. 

And indeed, if it was because of the victory over the fighting Sabines, and the slaying of Acron, and the many military victories in battle, these deeds can be compared to the Battle of the Centaurs and the wars against the Amazons. But Theseus dared the Cretan bond—whether it was some beast <gloss>I mean the Minotaur</gloss>, or a trap within the coastal lands of the man, or whether he was light...

<summary>This page concludes the biography of Romulus and begins the "Syncrisis" (Comparison) between Theseus and Romulus. Plutarch argues that Theseus sought out heroism by choice, whereas Romulus was driven by the necessity of escaping slavery and Amulius.</summary>
<keywords>Romulus, Theseus, Comparison, Syncrisis, Amulius, Sciron, Procrustes, Minotaur, Centauromachy, Amazons, Plutarch</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the comparison between Theseus and Romulus. Having established that Theseus chose his path voluntarily while Romulus was driven by necessity, Plutarch now examines the motivations behind Theseus’s journey to Crete and the subsequent political failures of both leaders.</meta>

It is indeed a strange thing among the stories told; let those insolent men who pride themselves on being <gloss>fought over</gloss> be dismissed. Theseus, of his own will, gave himself up to an inglorious and hopeless fate to sail with a stranger and a young boy <note>A reference to the tribute of Athenian youths sent to Crete to be devoured by the Minotaur.</note>, an act of such multifaceted daring, magnanimity, justice for the common good, or a longing for glory and virtue. Thus, it seems to me that the philosophers do not define Love <note>original: "ἔρωτα" (erota). Plutarch refers to the Platonic idea of Eros as a divine force for good.</note> badly as a service of the gods for the care and salvation of the young. For the love Ariadne felt for him seems to have been, in every way, a good work and a mechanism for the man’s salvation. It is not right to blame the woman for falling in love, but rather to wonder <note>Plutarch suggests that Ariadne's love was a divine intervention to save a hero.</note> why all did not feel the same reverence for such a youth. If she alone experienced this, she likely appeared worthy of love because she was naturally a lover of beauty and goodness, and she should be called one of the best of lovers.

Furthermore, though both were born to a nature of much hardship, neither of them strictly maintained the <term>royal character</term> <note>The "royal character" refers to the balanced middle path of leadership, avoiding the extremes of being too soft or too harsh.</note>. As we examine their ends, the one [Theseus] fell into a <term>popular</term> style <note>original: "δημοτικὴν" (demotiken). Theseus is often credited with founding Athenian democracy by relinquishing some royal power.</note> while the other [Romulus] fell into a <term>tyrannical</term> one. They committed the same error through opposite passions. For a ruler must first preserve the office itself; and it is preserved no less by refraining from what is unsuitable than by holding fast to what is appropriate. He who gives way or exerts too much pressure does not remain a king or a ruler; instead, by becoming either a <term>demagogue</term> <note>A leader who gains popularity by appealing to the desires of the common people rather than using rational judgment.</note> or a <term>despot</term> <note>An absolute ruler who governs through fear.</note>, he causes his subjects to either hate or despise him. However, the former [Theseus] seems to result from fairness and <term>philanthropy</term> <note>original: "φιλανθρωπίας" (philanthropias) – literally, a love for humanity.</note>, while the latter [Romulus] is a sin of self-love and harshness. We must not attribute these things entirely to a <term>daemon</term> <note>In this context, a "daemon" refers to a guiding spirit or divine fate, rather than an evil entity.</note>, but we should seek the differences of passion within the men themselves—such as the unreasoning anger and temper shown by the one toward his brother <note>Romulus killing his brother, Remus.</note>, and by the other toward his son <note>Theseus’s curse upon his son, Hippolytus, which led to his death.</note>. The impulse that moved their anger so disgracefully is excused by the one who was overturned by a greater cause, as if by a more severe blow. For since a lack of caution arises from yourself and from a consideration of conflicting interests, no one would think it right to suddenly change their mind in such a great matter...

<summary>Plutarch argues that Ariadne's love for Theseus was a divine means of his salvation and critiques both Theseus and Romulus for failing to maintain the 'royal' middle ground in leadership.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page concludes Plutarch’s formal comparison between Theseus, the legendary founder of Athens, and Romulus, the founder of Rome. It focuses on the domestic tragedies that plagued both men and evaluates the scale of their political achievements, specifically contrasting how they built their respective states.</meta>

...passion. He says this, however, in reference to the mind of all men: few have escaped such trials. Loves, jealousies, and the slanders of a woman led both to stumble. But what is more significant is that the error of Romulus broke out into naked action and deed, reaching a conclusion that was not fortunate <note>Plutarch refers to the death of Remus, Romulus’s twin brother, who was killed during a dispute over the founding of Rome.</note>; whereas the wrath of Theseus proceeded only as far as words, insults, and an old man’s curse <note>This refers to Theseus’s curse against his son, Hippolytus, whom he wrongly believed had raped his wife Phaedra. The curse led to the young man’s tragic death.</note>. In other matters, the youth [Romulus] seems to have made use of Fortune. If one were to assign <gloss>ballots</gloss> to these deeds as though they were of the smallest weight, this first advantage belongs to Romulus: that he took the most humble beginnings and reached the most brilliant achievements. For while being called slaves and the children of swineherds, before they were even free themselves, they liberated nearly all the Latins. In a single moment, they acquired the most diverse titles: killers of their enemies, saviors of their kin, kings of nations, and founders of cities. 

Romulus did not merely relocate a population, as Theseus did, nor was his city composed of many existing parts or built from established dwellings. Theseus destroyed many cities named after ancient kings and heroes to create one <note>This refers to the "Synoikismos," the political unification of Attica into Athens, which Plutarch views as a dissolution of older, smaller sovereignties.</note>. Romulus, however, did such things only later: forcing his enemies to cast down their own homes and disappear, only to be merged with their conquerors. But at the beginning, he did not merely move or increase a city that already existed; instead, he created one from nothing, acquiring for himself land, a homeland, a kingdom, lineages, marriages, and family ties all at once. He destroyed no one and ruined no one; rather, he acted as a benefactor to those without homes or hearths who wished to be a people and citizens. Though he did not shut out robbers and criminals, he also subdued cities and celebrated triumphs over kings and commanders. 

Furthermore, while the death of Romulus remains a matter of dispute regarding the <term>identity of the killer</term> <note>original: "αὐτόχειρα" (autocheira). Plutarch alludes to the rumors that Romulus was either murdered by the Senate or miraculously vanished to become the god Quirinus.</note>—and most turn the blame upon others—he clearly saved his mother from servitude. He also took his grandfather, who was serving ingloriously and without honor, and seated him upon the throne of Aeneas <note>Romulus restored his grandfather Numitor to the throne of Alba Longa, the kingdom founded by the descendants of the Trojan hero Aeneas.</note>. He served many interests willingly, but he was harmed...

Through a natural deficiency and neglect of the command regarding the loom <note>original: "ἱστὸν"; This refers to the domestic duties or the "thread" of fate/instruction, perhaps relating to the maidens sent to Crete</note>, I think he would hardly escape a charge of maiden-killing at Marathon, even before lazy judges. On the road to the sea, where no attendants were present. And indeed, concerning the transgressions in the abductions of women, on the one hand, [Theseus] exceeded the bounds of a decent nature. First, because he abducted them often, and many at once. There was Anaxo of Troezen. But above all, there was Helen, whom he took when he was past his prime and she was not yet of age <note>In myth, Theseus kidnapped Helen of Troy when she was a mere child, which Plutarch views as a lack of self-control</note>. Because she was a child and without a dowry, he might have seen fit to stop seeking new marriages. Then there was the injustice, for the daughters of the Erechtheids and the Spartans were not unremarkable mothers for children, and together they might have made life flourish in Athens among the descendants of Erechtheus and Cecrops. In these matters, he might have shown wisdom compared to his previous conduct on the road.

Romulus, on the other hand, first abducted not just a few women, but nearly eight hundred in number. However, as they say, he did not take them all for himself, but took only one, Hersilia. The others he distributed among the best of the citizens. Furthermore, by the subsequent honor, affection, and justice <gloss>fair treatment</gloss> shown to them, he demonstrated that that initial violence and the injustice committed against the women was actually a most noble deed and a most statesmanlike act for the sake of community. In this way, he mixed the peoples together and welded the families to one another. He provided a source of future goodwill and power for their affairs. Beyond this, time is the witness of the modesty, friendship, and stability which he established concerning marriages. For in five hundred years <note>original: "three hundred and two hundred years"</note>, no husband dared to desert his wife, nor any wife to leave the partnership of her husband. But just as among the Greeks...

Those who are very focused on this subject can point to the [killing] before Patroclus <note>A reference to a murder mentioned in Homeric lore or earlier myth, likely the death of the son of Amphidamas, contrasting Theseus's violence with the comparative order of Romulus’s reign.</note>. And while that was a killing, all Romans know that **Spurius Carvilius** <note>original: "Σκαρβίλιος Σπόριος"; Spurius Carvilius Ruga (c. 230 BCE) was traditionally the first Roman to divorce his wife, though Plutarch notes here how long Rome went without such a practice—roughly 230 years after the city's founding.</note> was the first to divorce his wife, citing her inability to bear children. The deeds themselves testify to the success of that long span of time; for the kings shared their rule, and the cities shared their civil rights through the intermarriages arising from them. 

But from the marriages of Theseus, nothing matrimonial or beneficial to the community ever came to the Athenians, nor did they lead to any peaceful agreement. Instead, they brought about enmities, wars, and the slaughter of citizens; and finally, the loss of **Aphidnae** <note>An ancient city in Attica, captured by the Dioscuri (Castor and Pollux) to rescue their sister Helen, whom Theseus had kidnapped.</note>, where they only narrowly escaped being forced to bow down before their enemies. Plutarch compares Theseus’s abduction of Helen to the catalyst of the Trojan War.</note>. 

Moreover, the mother of Theseus [Aethra] did not merely face danger, but suffered the fate of **Hecuba**, being abandoned and surrendered by her own son. As for the stories told of her captivity, perhaps these too are myths and falsehoods, as are most other things. Indeed, even the myths concerning divine favor show a great difference. For **Romulus** owed his preservation to the great goodwill of the gods. But the oracle given to **Aegeus** <note>Aegeus was the father of Theseus; the oracle warned him not to "loosen the foot of the wineskin" (have sex) until reaching home, but he ignored it with Aethra.</note>, commanding him to abstain from women in a foreign land, seems to demonstrate that the conception of **Theseus** occurred against the will of the gods. + + +

<meta>Continuity: This page transitions from the comparison of Theseus and Romulus into the biography of Solon, the Athenian lawgiver. It begins by discussing his parentage, his noble lineage from the house of Codrus, and his early relationship with the future tyrant Peisistratus.</meta>

This is the writing of Solon himself to Asclepiades. He was commanded by the laws of Philocles. And Euphorion <note>A writer and poet from Chalcis (3rd century BCE). Most other sources, including the main body of Plutarch's work, name Solon’s father as Execestides.</note> declares Solon’s father to be Euphorion, contrary to the <gloss>the</gloss> opinion of all those who mention Solon. Yet it has reached almost everyone together that he was the son of a man who was, as they say, most likely a man of moderate power among the citizens, though his house was of the first rank by lineage. For he was a **Codrid** <note>A descendant of Codrus, the semi-mythical last King of Athens, known for his self-sacrifice to save the city.</note> from the very beginning. 

Regarding the mother of Solon, Heraclides of Pontus records that she was a first cousin of the mother of **Peisistratus** <note>The future tyrant of Athens. Plutarch highlights their kinship to explain their complex later relationship.</note>. And there was a great friendship between the <gloss>the</gloss> two men, partly because of their kinship, and partly because of Solon’s natural excellence and youthful beauty. Some say that Peisistratus was a lover of Solon while he was growing up. For this reason, it seems, when they later came into disagreement regarding the government of the state, no harsh feeling was secretly nurtured <gloss>endured</gloss> between them, nor any wild passion; instead, those obligations of justice remained in their souls. It guarded, like a smoldering flame of a great fire still living, the memory and grace of their former love. 

That Solon was not resistant to beautiful boys, nor stood up like a boxer against the "unspoken" charms to keep them at arm's length, can be understood from his own poems. He also wrote a law expressly forbidding a slave from "rubbing with dry oil" <note>Using the gymnasium or wrestling school, which were centers of social and physical education for free citizens.</note> or from practicing pederasty <term>paederastein</term> <note>original: "παιδεραστεῖν"; In ancient Athens, this referred to a socially structured relationship between an older male mentor and a younger male. Solon’s law restricted this to free citizens, viewing it as a noble privilege rather than a base act.</note>. He placed this matter in the category of beautiful and dignified pursuits, and in a way, he invited those who were worthy of it while excluding those who were unworthy. 

It is also said that **Peisistratus** was a lover of Charmus, and that he dedicated the statue of Love in the Academy, where those who run the sacred torch race light their fire. 

Solon’s own father, however, diminished his estate through certain acts of philanthropy and kindness, as Hermippus <note>A biographer from Smyrna (3rd century BCE) known for his work "On Lawgivers."</note> says. Solon, unwilling to stop helping those who wished for aid, yet feeling ashamed to receive from others—having come from a house accustomed to helping others—set out while still young toward trade <term>emporia</term> <note>original: "ἐμπορία"; merchant travel. In this period, trade was a respectable way for a nobleman to restore his family fortune and gain knowledge of the world.</note>. Although some say he did so for the sake of great experience...

<meta>Continuity: This page transitions from discussing Solon's lineage and his relationship with Peisistratus to his personal philosophy on wealth and his background in trade. It addresses how he reconciled his noble birth with his commercial activities.</meta>

-> + <-

It is also recorded as a discovery of the historians that Solon was a wanderer. He was an acknowledged lover of wisdom <note>original: "philosopher"</note>, who, even when he was older, said that he was **"ever growing old while learning many things."** <note>A famous quote attributed to Solon (Fragment 18), emphasizing lifelong learning as a core tenet of his character.</note> Yet he did not hold wealth in awe; rather, he says that he is equally wealthy:

> "He who has much silver and gold,
> and fields of wheat-bearing earth,
> and horses and mules, and he who has
> only what is sufficient for his belly, his sides,
> and his feet to experience soft comforts.
> And for children and a wife, when these too
> arrive, and youth, and the season of life is in harmony." +

But elsewhere he says:

> "I desire to have wealth, but I do not wish
> to possess it unjustly; for justice surely follows after." +

<margin><note>A small hand icon (manicule) pointing right, used by a reader to mark a noteworthy passage regarding the dignity of work.</note></margin>
It is good if a virtuous man and a statesman is neither excessively attached to hopes, nor lacks the necessity of essentials, but is satisfied and thinks honorably. For, as Hesiod says, **"work is no shame."** <note>original: "ἔργου οὐδὲν ὄνειδος" — a reference to Hesiod's *Works and Days*, line 311, which Solon used to justify his commercial background.</note> Nor should one find it a burden to carry on the acquisition of wealth. For trade used to bring glory, as it made one acquainted with foreign lands <note>original: "barbarian things"</note> and secured the friendship of kings and made one experienced in many affairs. Some even became founders of great cities, such as the first settler of **Massalia** <note>Modern-day Marseille, France, founded by Phocaean Greeks.</note> among those near the Rhone. They say that **Thales** <note>Thales of Miletus, often called the first Greek philosopher.</note> also practiced trade, and **Hippocrates** the mathematician; and for **Plato**, they say the expenses of his journey were met by the sale of oil <note>This refers to Plato’s travels to Egypt, which he allegedly funded by selling olive oil.</note>. Therefore, they think that a certain luxuriousness <note>original: "ὕβριν" - used here perhaps to mean a certain indulgence or lack of austerity in his lifestyle.</note> and a style more suited to revelry than philosophy in Solon's poetry, as he discusses those who give, was rubbed off from his life as a merchant. For he faced many and great dangers, and in return for his hardships, he made certain sacrifices and enjoyed pleasures. That he did not place himself in the portion of the poor, but rather among the rich, is clear—

<margin>Added later <note>original: "προσ ελθεῖα"</note></margin>
> in these lines: + "For many bad men are rich, while good men +
> are poor; but we will not exchange our virtue +
> for their wealth, since the former is enduring, +
> but money passes from one man to another."

<summary>Solon defends his moderate pursuit of wealth and commercial background, arguing that trade is honorable and that virtue is far more permanent than material riches. He highlights other famous thinkers like Thales and Plato who also engaged in business to fund their travels and studies.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Solon’s character, moving from his views on wealth to his literary pursuits and his place among the Seven Sages of Greece. It specifically bridges the gap between his personal philosophy and his public role as a poet and lawgiver.</meta>

...wisdom, as much as he possessed. <note>original: "φρόνησιν"</note> In his **poetry**, at the beginning, he used it for nothing of serious importance, but rather for play, it seems, and to amuse himself in his leisure time. Later, however, he also set down philosophical maxims, and wove many political matters into his poems—not merely for the sake of history and record, but containing justifications for his own actions, as well as exhortations, warnings, and rebukes directed at the Athenians. Some say that he even attempted to publish his laws by setting them into epic verse. And they record the beginning as having been like this:

<margin>First</margin>
> "First we pray to King Zeus, son of Cronus,
> to grant good fortune and glory to these laws." +

Of **philosophy**, he most embraced the ethical branch—specifically the political—as did most of the wise men. In natural philosophy, <note>original: "φυσικοῖς" (physics/natural science)</note> he is simple and archaic, as is clear from these lines:

> "From the cloud comes the force of snow and hail,
> and thunder arises from bright lightning.
> By the winds the sea is stirred.
> But now <unclear>from afar it is loosed at length</unclear>, of all things it is most just." +

In general, it seems that the wisdom of **Thales** alone reached beyond practical necessity into theoretical contemplation. For the rest, the title of "wisdom" <note>original: "σοφίας"</note> was acquired from their political virtue. They are also said to have met together in one place, and again at Corinth, when **Periander** <note>The tyrant of Corinth and one of the Seven Sages.</note> arranged a certain general gathering and banquet for them. But even more than this, their reputation and glory were established by the circuit of the **tripod**, and its rotation through all of them, and their mutual yielding to one another with honorable goodwill. 

For, as they say, when some Coan fishermen were drawing in a net, and a stranger from Miletus had purchased the catch before it was visible, a **golden tripod** appeared as it was being dragged up. They say it was not brought out by sailors, but that it was cast down there, being mindful of a certain oracle <meta>catchword: ancient.</meta>

<meta>This page continues the account of Solon’s life, transitioning from the gathering of the Seven Sages to the famous legend of the Golden Tripod and the arrival of the Scythian philosopher Anacharsis in Athens. It maintains the biographical narrative common in Plutarch's Parallel Lives.</meta>

When a dispute arose between certain Ionian strangers and some fishermen regarding a **tripod** <term>tripod: A three-legged sacrificial vessel or seat, often made of precious metal and associated with the god Apollo</term>, and the cities involved took up the quarrel to the point of war, the **Pythia** <note>The priestess of Apollo at Delphi who delivered divine oracles.</note> proclaimed to both parties that they should give the tripod to the wisest man. Thus, it was first sent to **Thales** at **Miletus**, the Milesians having willingly granted it as a gift. Previously, they had been at war with all the Milesians. However, **Thales** declared that **Bias** was wiser than himself and sent it to him. From **Bias**, it was sent to <gloss>the</gloss> other; and so, being sent around in this way, it came back to **Thales** a second time. Finally, having been carried from **Miletus** to **Thebes**, it was dedicated to **Ismenian Apollo** <note>A cult title of Apollo in Thebes, where he had a famous temple by the river Ismenus.</note>.

**Theophrastus**, however, says that the tripod was sent first to **Bias** at **Priene**, and secondly to **Miletus** to **Thales**, after **Bias** had sent it on. In this way, it passed through all the Sages until it returned again to **Bias**, and was finally sent to **Delphi**. These things, then, have been recorded by many writers. **Dorion** writes more regarding the tripod, saying some claim it was a vial sent by **Croesus** <note>The famously wealthy King of Lydia.</note>, while others say it was a drinking cup left behind by **Bathycles**.

Separately, they tell of **Anacharsis** <note>A Scythian prince and philosopher known for his "barbarian" wit and blunt wisdom.</note> coming to visit **Solon**, and again of his time with **Thales**; they write of the meeting in this way: They say that **Anacharsis** came to **Athens**, arrived at **Solon's** house, and knocked, saying that he had come as a stranger to establish a friendship and to request hospitality. When **Solon** replied that "it is better to make friendships at home," **Anacharsis** took him up on it and said, "Then since you are at home, you yourself should make a friendship and bond of hospitality with us." **Solon**, marveling at the man’s quick-wittedness, received him kindly and kept him with him for some time. At that time, **Solon** was already managing public affairs and drafting his **laws** <note>original: "νόμους"</note>. **Anacharsis**, learning of this, laughed at **Solon’s** project, thinking...
