--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion between Solon and the Scythian philosopher Anacharsis. It concludes Anacharsis’s famous critique of written laws and transitions into an anecdote regarding Solon’s visit to Thales of Miletus, where Thales uses a cruel ruse to justify his decision to remain unmarried.</meta>

...[laws] written in letters which, intended to record the injustices and greed of the citizens, differed in no way from **spiders' webs** <note>original: "ἀραχνίων" (arachniōn). This famous metaphor suggests that laws only catch the small and weak, while the "large flies" (the rich and powerful) break through them.</note>; for just as those webs hold fast the weak and small things that fall into them, they are torn asunder by the powerful and the rich. They say that **Solon** replied to this by saying that men also observe agreements which it is profitable for neither of the parties who made them to break; and he himself so adapted the laws to the citizens that he showed everyone that doing what is right is better than breaking the law. 

But these things turned out more as **Anacharsis** had guessed than according to the hope of **Solon**. **Anacharsis** also said he wondered at this, having attended an **assembly** <term>assembly: The Ekklesia, the principal sovereign body of ancient Athens where citizens voted on laws and policy.</term>: that among the Greeks, the wise men speak, but the ignorant men decide.

When **Solon** went to see **Thales** at **Miletus**, he wondered why **Thales** completely neglected marriage and the procreation of children. **Thales** remained silent at the time, but after a few days, he arranged for a man to act as a stranger, claiming to have just arrived ten days prior from **Athens**. When **Solon** asked if there was any news in **Athens**—the man having been instructed what he ought to say—he replied that there was nothing else,

<margin>
<note>A manicule (pointing hand) in the left margin points toward the following paragraph regarding the story of the death of Solon's son.</note>
</margin>

except, by Zeus, the **funeral** <term>funeral: The "ekphora," specifically the carrying out of the body in a public procession.</term> of a certain young man, and the whole city escorted him. For he was the son, they said, of a famous man who was foremost among the citizens for his virtue; but the father was not present, for they said he had been traveling abroad for a long time. 

"Oh, what a misfortune!" **Solon** cried. "And what did they call him?" 

"I heard the name," the man said, "but I do not remember it; only that there was much talk of his wisdom and justice." 

Thus, with every answer, **Solon** was brought closer to fear, and finally, overwhelmed by distress, he himself suggested the name to the stranger, asking, "Is the dead youth not called the son of **Solon**?" When the man confirmed it, **Solon** began to strike his head and do and say all the things that happen to those in great grief. But **Thales**, taking hold of him and laughing...

**Vocabulary:** Solon, Anacharsis, Thales, Athens, Miletus, laws, spiders' webs, justice

<summary>Anacharsis critiques Greek law and democracy as fragile and illogical, while Thales of Miletus performs a psychological trick on Solon to demonstrate why he avoids the pain of family attachments.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Anacharsis, Thales of Miletus, Athenian law, spider web metaphor, grief, Athenian Assembly, Miletus</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Solon’s visit to Thales of Miletus. On the previous page, Thales had deceived Solon with a false report of his son’s death to illustrate why he avoided the emotional burden of a family. This page transitions into a philosophical critique of Thales’s logic and a discussion on the nature of human affection.</meta>

-> + <-

Let these things then be said regarding **Solon** concerning marriage and the procreation of children—things which call upon you also to cast [your fears] away more vigorously. But take heart on account of this regarding **Thales**. Does someone not introduce this man? <note>original: "οὗτος δὲ οὐκ εἰσηγῇ τις" – This sentence appears slightly corrupted in the transcription; it likely questions whether one should follow Thales’s example.</note> These things, then, falling into the conversation, are said to be related by **Pataikos**, who used to claim he possessed the soul of **Aesop** <note>Aesop (c. 620–564 BCE) was the legendary Greek fabulist. Pataikos’s claim of possessing his soul suggests he spoke in clever, moralizing anecdotes.</note>. It is strange and ignoble for a man to reject the acquisition of a thing because he fears its loss. For by that logic, one should not welcome health, nor wealth, nor even wisdom when they arrive, for fear of being deprived of them.

For even **virtue** <term>virtue: 'Arete' (ἀρετὴ), meaning excellence or moral goodness, considered the highest human achievement.</term>, than which there is no greater or sweeter possession, we see destroyed by some distraction caused by disease or medicines. **Thales** remains [as an example], but there would be no greater harm if he did not also flee from the acquisition of an inheritance, and of kinsmen and country. But he even held a dear "child" by adopting his sister's son himself.

For the soul, having a natural affection within itself—or being born, as it were, to perceive and to think and to remember—so it is also born to love. But in empty and helpless natures, this affection grows for things for which there is no natural tie. And just as in a house or an empty land lacking legitimate heirs, foreign guests and base-born servants move in and take possession; and along with their love, they instill a sense of care and fear for them. 

So you might see men speaking quite sternly by nature against marriage and the begetting of children; then these same men, when the children of servants or their pampered pets <note>original: "θρέμμασι μαλακοῖς" (thremmasi malakois) – literally 'soft nurslings' or delicate pets.</note> fall ill and die, are overcome with longing and utter ignoble cries. Some have even been shamefully and unendurably affected by grief at the death of dogs and horses. Yet others, having lost good sons, suffered nothing terrible, nor did they do anything shameful; instead, they continued to live the rest of their lives according to **reason** <term>reason: 'Logos' (λόγος), the rational principle that allows a person to govern their emotions through logic and philosophy.</term>. For it is weakness, not affection, that brings endless griefs and fears upon men who are untrained—by reason—towards strength. For such men do not even enjoy...

<summary>This page concludes the anecdote of Solon and Thales, arguing that avoiding family life to escape grief is illogical, as the human soul naturally seeks objects of affection, often replacing lost family with pets or servants if disciplined reason is absent.</summary>

<keywords>Solon, Thales, Pataikos, Aesop, marriage, grief, virtue (arete), reason (logos), parental love, fear of loss</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative following the philosophical discussion of Thales and Solon regarding family and the fear of loss. It transitions from these ethical reflections to Solon's famous political stratagem to recapture the island of Salamis for Athens.</meta>

->+<-

...of that which is placed before us in the present. As for the future, it will always bring pains, fears, and struggles to those who provide for it. One must not be persuaded by <gloss>x</gloss>poverty toward the pursuit of wealth, nor by the absence of friends toward <term>the fear of losing friends</term> <note>original: "φιλαποβολὴν" – literally a "fondness for losing," here used to mean the obsessive fear of loss that prevents one from forming attachments.</note>, nor by childlessness toward the <gloss>x</gloss>death of children. Rather, one must remain steady toward all things. And these things are sufficient for the present, as no more than enough is required. 

When the people in the city had grown exhausted from carrying on a long and difficult war against the **Megarians** for the island of **Salamis**, they established a law: no one was to write or even speak again about how the city must recover **Salamis**, upon pain of death. **Solon**, bearing this disgrace heavily and seeing many of the young men desiring a command for the war but not daring to begin it because of the law, feigned a madness <term>madness: 'mania' (μανία), here meaning a temporary loss of wits or a frantic state</term> and a loss of his faculties. A report was spread from his house into the city that he was in a disturbed state. Then, having secretly composed some verses and practiced them so he could recite them from memory, he suddenly leaped out into the marketplace <term>agora: the central public space in ancient Greek city-states</term>, wearing a small felt cap <note>original: "πιλίδιον" – a cap usually worn by the sick; wearing it in public was a visual cue of his "madness."</note> upon his head. A great crowd having run together, he climbed upon the herald’s stone and went through his elegy in song. Its beginning is:

> I have come myself as a herald from lovely **Salamis**;
> Composing a song, an ornament of words, instead of a speech.

This poem is titled *Salamis*. It consists of one hundred lines and is very gracefully composed. When it had been sung, and the friends of **Solon** began to praise it—especially **Peisistratos**, who urged on the citizens and encouraged them—the **Athenians** were persuaded. Repealing the law, they once again took up the war, placing **Solon** in command. 

Now, the account commonly told in public is this: that having sailed to **Kolias** with **Peisistratos**, and having found all the women there performing the ancestral sacrifice to **Demeter** <note>Demeter was the goddess of the harvest and sacred law; the festival mentioned is likely the Thesmophoria.</note>, he sent a trusted man to **Salamis** pretending to >

<meta>This page continues Plutarch’s "Life of Solon." The previous page concluded with Solon beginning his performance of "feigned madness" in the Athenian marketplace to circumvent a law that forbade mentioning the war for Salamis. This page details the two varying historical accounts of how Solon actually led the Athenians to victory.</meta>

-> + <-

...he ordered a deserter to go to the **Megarians** and tell them that if they wished to capture the foremost women of the **Athenians**, they should sail with him to **Colias** <note>Colias was a promontory on the coast of Attica where there was a temple of Aphrodite.</note> as quickly as possible. When the Megarians were persuaded and sent out men in a ship, **Solon** saw the vessel being rowed from the island. He ordered the women to withdraw out of sight, and taking those among the younger men who were not yet bearded, he dressed them in the women's garments, headbands <term>mitras: 'μίτραις', cloth headbands or turbans</term>, and shoes. 

Having given them hidden daggers <term>encheiridia: 'ἐγχειρίδια', small hand-knives</term>, he told them to play and dance by the sea until the enemy landed and the ship was in their power. So it was that as the Megarians sailed near, they were deceived by what they saw. Approaching close to the shore, they leaped out as if toward women, competing with one another to reach them first, so that not a single one escaped, but all were destroyed. Then the Athenians, sailing to the island, took possession of it. 

Others, however, say that the capture did not happen in this manner. Instead, they claim that first the god in **Delphi** <note>The Oracle of Apollo at Delphi, the most prestigious source of divine guidance in the Greek world.</note> gave him this oracle:

<margin>></margin> "Appease with sacrifices the hero-leaders of the land, 
<margin>></margin> whom the Asopian earth covers in its folds, 
<margin>></margin> <insert>⊕</insert> who, having perished, look toward the setting sun." <note>original: "ἐς ἠέλιον δύνοντα" — This refers to the Greek custom of burying the dead facing the west.</note> +:

After this, Solon sailed by night to the island and immediately performed sacrifices to the heroes **Periphemus** and **Cychreus** <note>Cychreus was a legendary king of Salamis, often depicted as a half-serpent hero.</note>. Then, having gathered five hundred Athenian volunteers—a decree having been passed that if they captured the island, they would be the supreme authorities of the government <term>politeuma: 'πολιτεύματος', the civil administration or body of citizens</term>—he set out. 

With many fishing boats and a thirty-oared galley sailing alongside, he anchored at **Salamis** by a certain jagged reef <term>chele: 'χηλήν', literally a "claw" or a breakwater/rocky point</term> that looks toward **Euboea**. When the Megarians in Salamis heard of this through a rumor that was not yet certain, they hurried to their arms in a great tumult. They dispatched a ship to scout the enemy; and when it approached, Solon captured it and imprisoned the Megarians on board...

...[appease the heroes] and the best of the Athenians, he ordered them to sail against the city, hiding themselves as much as possible. At the same time, taking the rest of the Athenians on foot, he engaged the **Megarians**. While the battle was still raging, the men from the ship succeeded in capturing the city. It seems that the rituals performed even now <note>Plutarch refers to a ritual reenactment of the battle that survived into his own time (c. 1st century AD).</note> bear witness to this account. For an Attic ship sails in, at first at random and by chance; then, toward the place where the ship is being carried, one armed man leaps out with a shout and runs toward the **Sciradian** promontory <note>A headland on the island of Salamis.</note>, joining those coming from the mainland. Nearby is a temple of **Enyalius** <term>Enyalius: 'Ἐνυάλιος', an ancient war god often identified with or seen as an attendant to Ares</term>, founded by **Solon**. He defeated the Megarians, and as many as were not destroyed in the battle, he released under a truce. 

Nevertheless, as the Megarians persisted in causing much trouble, both inflicting and suffering many evils in the war, they made the **Lacedaemonians** <note>The Spartans, known for their legalistic rigor, acted as international arbitrators.</note> mediators and judges. Most people say that Solon enlisted the fame of **Homer** as his ally; for they say he inserted a verse into the *Catalogue of Ships* <note>A famous section of Book 2 of the *Iliad* listing the Greek forces.</note> and read it at the trial:

<margin>></margin> +
<margin>></margin> > **Ajax** brought twelve ships from **Salamis**,
<margin>></margin> + and stationed them where the **Athenian** phalanxes 
<margin>></margin> > stood.
+

The Athenians themselves, however, consider this story to be nonsense. They say that Solon demonstrated to the judges that **Philaeus** and **Eurysaces**, the sons of **Ajax**, received Athenian citizenship and handed over the island to them. They settled in **Attica**, one in **Brauron** and the other in **Melite**. There is even a district <term>demos: 'δῆμον', a local administrative division of Attica</term> named after Philaeus, which was the home of the **Philaïdae** family, to which **Peisistratus** <note>The famous 6th-century BC tyrant of Athens.</note> belonged. 

Wishing to refute the Megarians even further, Solon insisted upon the evidence of the dead, noting that the Megarians do not bury their deceased in the same manner as the Athenians. The Megarians bury their dead facing the East, whereas the Athenians bury them facing the West. However, **Hereas the Megarian** <note>A Megarian historian who wrote to counter Athenian propaganda.</note> objects and says that the Megarians also turn their dead toward the West...

<meta>This page continues Plutarch’s "Life of Solon." ->+<-

[toward the west] <note>The text continues the discussion of burial customs as evidence for land ownership. Athenians buried their dead facing the setting sun (west), whereas Megarians buried them facing the east.</note> the faces of the dead are said to be turned. And an even greater proof than this is that each Athenian has a separate tomb, while those of the Megarians lie with three or four in a single grave. However, **Hermippus** <note>A 3rd-century BC biographer known for collecting anecdotes.</note> says this is not entirely the case, although he does say that certain oracles assisted **Solon**, in which the god <note>Apollo at Delphi.</note> addressed **Salamis** as "Ionian." This case was judged by five **Lacedaemonians** <note>Spartan arbitrators: the Spartans were often called upon to settle international disputes due to their reputation for impartiality.</note>: **Critolaïdes**, **Amompharetus**, **Hypsechidas**, **Anaxilas**, and **Cleomenes**.

Thus, from this time forward, Solon was famous and great. But he was admired and celebrated even more among the Greeks for speaking on behalf of the temple at **Delphi**, arguing that it was necessary to come to its aid and not allow the **Cirrhaeans** to insult the oracle, but to defend the Delphians on behalf of the god. For being persuaded by him, the **Amphictyons** <term>Amphictyons: 'Ἀμφικτύονες', a league of Greek tribes that protected and managed the sanctuary at Delphi</term> set out for the war, as others testify, and especially **Aristotle** in his list of the **Pythian Victors** <note>A lost work by Aristotle cataloging the history of the Pythian Games.</note>, where he attributes the proposal to Solon. He was not, however, appointed as one of the generals for this war, as **Hermippus** says **Evanthes the Samian** states; for neither **Aeschines** the orator has said this in his memoirs concerning Delphi, and **Alcmaeon**, not Solon, is recorded as the general of the Athenians.

The **Cylonian Curse** <note>original: "Κυλώνειον ἄγος" (Kyloneion agos). A famous incident of sacrilege in early Athenian history that haunted the Alcmaeonid family for generations.</note> had already for a long time disturbed the city, dating from the time when **Megacles** the Archon persuaded the accomplices of **Cylon**—who were suppliants of the goddess—to come down from the Acropolis to stand trial. Having tied a woven woolen thread to the statue <note>Presumably the ancient wooden statue of Athena Polias.</note> and holding onto it to remain under the goddess's protection, they began their descent. But when they reached the shrine of the **Venerable Goddesses** <note>original: "σεμνὰς θεάς" (semnas theas), a euphemism for the Eumenides or Furies, whose temple sat at the base of the Areopagus.</note>, the thread broke of its own accord. Megacles, rushing to seize them on the grounds that the goddess had rejected their supplication, arrested them. The others who fled to the altars were slaughtered; only those who petitioned the wives of the magistrates were spared. From this time, those responsible were called "the polluted" <term>enageis: 'ἐναγεῖς', individuals under a religious curse or blood-guilt</term> and were hated.

And the survivors of the Cylonian faction became powerful again. As the civil strife continued for a long time in the city, reaching its height especially...

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Life of Solon." The previous page concluded the legal dispute over Salamis. We now move into the internal Athenian crisis known as the "Cylonian Curse" (an ancient blood-guilt) and the arrival of the Cretan seer Epimenides to purify the city.</meta>

->+<-

And when the people were divided into factions, **Solon** appeared, <insert>who</insert> came forward into the midst of them, along with the most noble of the **Athenians**. By entreating and teaching them, he persuaded those who had become cursed <note>original: "τοὺς ἐναγεῖς" (tous enageis). This refers to the Alcmaeonid family, who were considered religiously "polluted" for killing followers of the rebel Cylon at a sacred altar.</note> to submit to a trial and be judged by three hundred jurors chosen for their merit. With **Myron of Phlya** acting as the accuser, the men were convicted; those who were still alive went into exile, and as for those who had already died, the people dug up their corpses and cast them out beyond the borders. 

During these disturbances, as the **Megarians** <note>The neighboring rivals of Athens.</note> also attacked them, the **Athenians** lost **Nisaea** <note>The strategic port of Megara.</note> and were driven out of **Salamis** once again. Fears born of superstition, as well as ghostly apparitions, gripped the city. The seers then declared that pollutions and defilements requiring purification <term>purification: 'καθαρμῶν' (katharmon), ritual cleansing required to appease the gods after a crime</term> were being revealed through the sacrificial omens.

Thus, sent for by them, **Epimenides of Phaistos** came from **Crete**. Some count him as the seventh among the **Sages**, at least among those who do not accept **Periander** <note>The tyrant of Corinth, often excluded from the list of the Seven Sages due to his cruelty.</note>. He was thought to be a man favored by the gods and wise in divine matters, specifically in the wisdom of religious inspiration and initiatory rites. For this reason, he was called the "son of a nymph" <gloss>named Blaste</gloss>; and the people of that time addressed him as a "new Coures" <note>A 'Coures' (or Kourete) refers to the legendary protectors of the infant Zeus in Crete.</note>. 

Having arrived, and having made a friend of Solon, he assisted him in many ways and smoothed the path for his lawgiving. For he made the sacred rites less costly and made the people more restrained in their mourning, by mixing certain sacrifices directly into the funeral ceremonies and removing the harsh and "barbaric" customs to which most women had previously been prone. Most importantly, by sanctifying and purifying the city with certain atonements, purifications, and ritual foundations, he made it obedient to justice and more inclined toward harmony. 

It is said that after observing and examining **Munichia** <note>A hill in the Piraeus (the port area of Athens) that offered a commanding view.</note> for a long time, he said to those present that "man is blind to the future," for the Athenians would eat it up with their own teeth if they knew how much trouble that place would cause the city <note>This was a prophecy regarding later history, as Munichia was frequently occupied by foreign garrisons to control Athens.</note>. Something similar is also said of **Thales**...

<summary>Solon resolves the Cylonian blood-guilt by putting the accused on trial, and the Cretan seer Epimenides arrives to purify Athens of its religious pollutions and civil unrest.</summary>

<meta>This page follows the account of the Cylonian conspiracy's resolution. It transitions from the religious purification of Athens by the seer Epimenides to the intensifying socio-economic crisis that eventually led to Solon’s appointment as Lawgiver.</meta>

->+<-

...they say that he ordered them to be taken up and even cast out from their graves. <note>Referring to the remains of the "cursed" Alcmaeonid family mentioned on the previous page.</note> It is said he was prompted by a vision to do this. After addressing them, he remarked that such a thing should never again happen. **Epimenides**, for his part, was greatly admired; although the **Athenians** offered him much money and great honors, he wanted nothing except a branch from the sacred olive tree <note>original: "ἱερ-ελαίας" (hier-elaias). The sacred tree on the Acropolis believed to have been given by the goddess Athena.</note>, and having received it, he departed.

But when the **Athenian** disturbances concerning the **Cylonian** affair had ceased and the men had been banished as previously described, they fell into political strife <note>original: "ἐστασίαζον" (estasiazon), a term for civil factionalism or near-civil war.</note> once again. The city was divided into as many factions as the territory had geographical differences. There were the people of the **Highlands** <term>Highlands: 'διακρίων' (diakriōn), the rugged hill country of Attica</term>, who were the most democratic; the people of the **Plains** <term>Plains: 'πεδιέων' (pedieōn), the wealthy landowners of the fertile central plain</term>, who were the most oligarchic; and thirdly, the people of the **Coast** <term>Coast: 'παράλιοι' (paralioi), the merchant and fishing class</term>, who preferred a middle and mixed form of government. These stood in the way and prevented the other groups from gaining control.

At that time, the inequality between the poor and the rich had reached its peak, and the city was in a completely precarious state. It seemed that the only way to establish order and stop the turmoil was for a tyranny <note>In this period, a "tyrant" was a leader who seized absolute power, often to break the deadlock between warring social classes.</note> to arise. For all the common people were in debt to the wealthy. They either farmed for them, paying a sixth of the produce—for which they were called **"Sixth-parters"** <term>Sixth-parters: 'ἑκτημόριοι' (hektēmorioi), tenant farmers who were required to give a sixth of their crop to their landlords</term> and laborers—or they took out loans using their own bodies as collateral, becoming subject to seizure by their creditors. Some were enslaved right there at home, while others were sold into foreign lands. Many were even forced to sell their own children, for no law forbade it. The city was almost entirely abandoned because of the harshness of the moneylenders. 

<insert>But</insert> the majority and the strongest among them began to organize and encourage one another not to stand by, but to choose one man as a faithful leader, to release those who were overdue on their debts, redistribute the land, and completely change the constitution. At this point, the wisest of the **Athenians**, seeing that **Solon** was the only one mostly free from faults—neither sharing in the injustice of the rich nor...

<summary>Epimenides completes the purification of Athens and departs, refusing payment. However, the city immediately fractures into three regional factions and faces a severe debt crisis where the poor are forced into slavery, leading them to call for Solon as a mediator.</summary>
<keywords>Epimenides, Athens, Highlands (Diakrioi), Plains (Pedieis), Coast (Paralioi), Hektemorioi (Sixth-parters), debt slavery, Solon, constitutional reform, economic inequality</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Athens’ socio-economic crisis. It describes the specific pressures placed upon Solon to intervene in the conflict between the debt-ridden poor and the wealthy elite, as well as the temptation for him to seize absolute power as a tyrant.</meta>

...with the poor being oppressed by the necessities of debt; they begged him to come forward to manage public affairs and put an end to the disputes. However, **Phanias of Lesbos** <note>original: "Φανίας ὁ Λέσβιος". A historian and student of Aristotle who wrote about Greek tyrants and lawgivers.</note> records that **Solon** himself used a kind of deception toward both parties for the salvation of the city. He secretly promised to the poor a redistribution of land, and to the wealthy, a confirmation of their contracts <term>contracts: 'συμβολαίων' (symbolaiōn), referring here to the legal bonds of debt held by creditors</term>. 

Phanias says that **Solon**, however, hesitated to take up the administration of the state, fearing the greed of some and the arrogance of others. But he was chosen Archon <term>Archon: 'ἄρχων', the chief magistrate of Athens</term> after **Philombrotus**, acting simultaneously as a mediator and a lawmaker. The wealthy accepted him readily as an acquaintance, and the poor as a man of integrity. It is also said that a certain saying of his was being circulated earlier: "Equality breeds no war." This pleased both the property owners and the penniless; for the former expected "equality" based on merit and virtue, while the latter expected it based on measure and number <note>The wealthy interpreted "equality" as proportional to one's social standing, while the poor interpreted it as an equal split for every citizen.</note>. 

Consequently, a great hope arose, and the leaders of each faction pressed **Solon**, offering him a tyranny <term>tyranny: 'τυραννίδα' (tyrannida). In this era, a tyrant was often a popular leader who seized total power to break the hold of the aristocracy, though the word eventually gained its modern negative meaning.</term> and trying to persuade him to seize control of the city more boldly once he had it in his power. Many of the moderate citizens, seeing that a change brought about by reason and law would be a laborious and difficult task, did not object to placing the most sensible and just man at the head of affairs. Some say that **Solon** even received an oracle from the Pythian priestess <note>The oracle of Apollo at Delphi.</note> like this:

<margin>> </margin> "Sit in the middle, and guide the work of the helmsman;"
<margin>> </margin> "Steer straight, for many Athenians are your helpers." +

But his friends especially reproached him, asking if he was afraid of the name of "monarchy," as if it would not immediately become a kingship through the virtue of the one who took it. They pointed out that the **Euboeans** had previously chosen **Tynondas**, and now the people of **Mytilene** had chosen **Pittacus** as their tyrant. None of these things shook **Solon** from his own purpose, but he said to his friends, as the story goes: "Tyranny is a fair field, but there is no way out of it" <note>original: "καλὸν μὲν εἶναι χωρίον τὴν τυραννίδα, οὐκ ἔχειν δὲ ἀπόβασιν". This famous metaphor suggests that while absolute power is attractive, it is a "trap" from which one cannot safely retire.</note>.

<summary>Solon is appointed Archon to mediate between Athens' warring classes, resisting immense pressure from both his friends and the public to become a tyrant and seize absolute power.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Phanias of Lesbos, Pittacus, Archon, Tyranny, Athens, Equality, Debt Crisis, Oracle of Delphi</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Plutarch’s biography of Solon. Having established the socio-economic crisis in Athens, the text now focuses on Solon’s refusal to seize absolute power as a "tyrant" and his philosophy of governance through compromise rather than radical revolution.</meta>

Regarding Solon, he writes in his poems:

<margin>></margin>
> *If I spared my native land, and did not grasp at tyranny and merciless violence,*
<margin>></margin>
> *staining and shaming my glory, I am not ashamed; for thus I think I shall*
<margin>></margin>
> *more greatly surpass all men.*

From this, it is clear that even before his lawgiving, he held a great reputation. But regarding those who urged him to take the tyranny, many write that they mocked him:

<margin>></margin>
> *Solon was not a man of deep mind, nor a man of wise counsel; for when God*
<margin>></margin>
> *offered good things, he himself did not accept them. Having surrounded the*
<margin>></margin>
> *prey and being struck with amazement, he did not pull the great net tight,*
<margin>></margin>
> *failing in both spirit and mind. For he might have wished, having gained power*
<margin>></margin>
> *and taken boundless wealth, and having been tyrant of Athens for just a single*
<margin>></margin>
> *day, to be flayed afterward like a wine-skin <note>original: "ἀσκὸς" (askos). This refers to a leather bag or wine-skin; the image of being flayed to make a bag was a common ancient metaphor for a brutal execution.</note>, and to have his family wiped out.*

Solon composed these lines to represent what the many and the common people were saying about him. However, although he rejected the tyranny, he did not handle matters in a weak or submissive manner toward the powerful, nor did he make the laws simply to please those who had chosen him. Instead, where things were already at their best, he did not apply "surgery" <term>surgery: 'ἰατρείαν' (iatreian), used here metaphorically to mean radical or invasive political reform</term> or innovation. He feared that if he threw the city into total confusion and disorder, he would be too weak to restore it and fit it together again toward what is best. 

But those things which he could hope to achieve through persuasion, or by applying necessity to those who resisted, these things he did, as he himself says, "fitting together force and justice" <note>original: "ὁμοῦ βίην τε καὶ δίκην συναρμόσας". This is one of Solon's most famous philosophical statements on the nature of law.</note>. Wherefore, when he was later asked if he had written the best laws for the Athenians, he replied: "The best they would accept." 

As for what more recent writers say about the Athenians—that they cleverly disguise unpleasant things with kind and humane names <note>This refers to the Greek rhetorical practice of "hypocorism" or using euphemisms to make harsh realities sound more pleasant.</note>—they call prostitutes "companions" <term>companions: 'ἑταίρας' (hetairas), a more refined term for high-status courtesans</term>; they call taxes "contributions" <term>contributions: 'συντάξεις' (syntaxeis), implying a voluntary or organized sharing of costs rather than a forced levy</term>; and gar...

<summary>Solon defends his refusal to become a tyrant despite public mockery, explaining his moderate approach to lawmaking as "fitting together force and justice" to create the best laws the people would actually tolerate.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Plutarch’s biography of Solon, specifically following the discussion of how Solon used gentle language to describe harsh realities. It recounts the enactment of the Seisachtheia (the cancellation of debts) and the economic reforms involving currency and measures.</meta>

...and calling the city garrisons "guards." Calling the prison an "apartment" <note>original: "οἴκημα" (oikēma). This was a linguistic "device" or "sophism" of Solon—using a mild word for a harsh institution to make the law more palatable to the public.</note> was first a clever device of Solon, as was the establishment of the cancellation of debts, which he named the "shaking-off of burdens" <term>shaking-off of burdens: 'Seisachtheia' (σεισάχθεια). This was Solon's landmark reform to address the debt crisis that had led many Athenians into slavery.</term>.

For this was his first political act: he decreed that existing debts be released and that, for the future, no one should lend money on the security of the debtor's body <note>This law ended the practice of "debt-slavery," where a person who could not pay their debts was forced to become the slave of their creditor.</note>. Yet some writers, among whom is Androtion <note>Androtion was an Athenian historian and politician (c. 410–340 BCE) who provided an alternative, more conservative interpretation of Solon's reforms.</note>, have written that the poor were relieved not by a cancellation of debts, but by a reduction of interest, and that they welcomed this act of humanity and called it the "shaking-off of burdens." This was accompanied by an increase in the size of measures and the value of the currency.

For he made the mina <term>mina: 'μνᾶν' (mnān), a unit of weight and currency.</term> consist of one hundred drachmas, whereas it had previously been seventy-three. Thus, while the debtors paid back the same numerical amount, the actual value was less; in this way, those paying back large sums were greatly benefited, while those receiving them suffered no loss.

But most writers agree that the "shaking-off of burdens" was an actual abolition of contracts, and Solon’s own poems harmonize better with this view. For in these poems, Solon boasts that he removed the "boundary markers" <term>boundary markers: 'ὅρους' (horous), pillars or stones placed on land to indicate that the property was mortgaged or that the owner owed a portion of their produce to a creditor.</term> which were fixed in many places upon the pledged land; the land which was previously enslaved is now free. And of those citizens who had been sold for money, some having spent so long in foreign lands that they no longer spoke the Attic tongue <note>Attic tongue: the dialect of Greek spoken in Athens.</note> because of their many wanderings, and others here at home enduring shameful servitude, he says he made them free.

However, it is said that a most distressing thing happened to him as a result of that action. When he was setting out to release the debts and was seeking appropriate arguments and a suitable beginning, he confided in those friends whom he most trusted and frequently consulted—men such as Conon, Cleinias, and Hipponicus. He told them that he did not intend to touch the land <note>This refers to the redistribution of land, a radical demand from the poor which Solon refused to implement, preferring a middle-ground reform.</note>, but that he had decided to perform the cancellation of debts. But these friends, taking advantage of him and anticipating the law, immediately borrowed a large sum...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Solon’s economic reforms in Athens, specifically addressing the scandal involving his friends—who took advantage of his plans—and comparing Solon’s moderate political approach with the more forceful methods of the Spartan lawgiver, Lycurgus.</meta>

...having received the money, they became wealthy and contracted great debts. When the movement <note>referring to the 'Seisachtheia' or cancellation of debts</note> was brought forth, they once again enjoyed the fruits of their scheme, but caused great trouble for those who had lent to them by not paying them back. This placed Solon under great blame and slander, as if he had been a secret accomplice, when in fact he had been a fellow victim of their injustice.

However, for Solon, this charge was immediately dissolved by the matter of five talents—some say three—for that was the amount he was found to have out on loan, and he was the first to cancel these debts in accordance with the law. Some, including Polyzelus the Rhodian, say it was actually fifteen talents. 

His friends, however, continued to be called "debt-cutters" <note>original: "χρεωκοπίδαι" (chreokopidai). This was a derogatory nickname for Solon's friends—Conon, Cleinias, and Hipponicus—who used their inside knowledge of the upcoming debt cancellation to buy land with borrowed money they never intended to repay.</note> ever after. Solon had annoyed the wealthy by abolishing their contracts, and he annoyed the poor even more because he did not perform a redistribution of land as they had hoped. Nor did he, like Lycurgus, make the citizens entirely uniform and equal in their way of life.

But Lycurgus, being the tenth in descent from Hercules <note>original: "Ἡρακλέους" (Herakleous). The Spartan kings claimed divine ancestry from the hero Hercules.</note> and having reigned for many years over Lacedaemon <note>Sparta</note>, possessed great prestige, friends, and powers that served his constitution. He used force more than persuasion—to the point that his own eye was even knocked out <note>This refers to an incident where a young man named Alcander, enraged by Lycurgus’s harsh laws, struck him with a staff and blinded him in one eye.</note>. Thus, he cast himself into danger for the sake of the city’s safety and harmony, seeking to ensure that no citizen should be insecure.

Solon, however, did not achieve this level of control in his constitution, as he was a man of the people and of the middle class. Within the limits of his existing power, he did nothing by force, setting out only with his own will and the trust the citizens placed in him. Because he offended the majority by disappointing their expectations, he himself spoke about his situation:

> "But now they are angry with me, and they all look at me askance with their eyes, as if I were an enemy." <margin>⑩</margin>

Yet he says that if anyone else had held the same power, they would not have restrained the people, nor would they have stopped until they had stirred everything up and "taken the cream from the milk" <note>original: "πᾶρ ἐξέλῃ γάλα" (par exēlē gala). A proverbial expression meaning to strip away the best part or to exhaust the source of wealth entirely through greed.</note>. Soon, however, the benefit...

<summary>This page recounts the scandal of Solon's friends exploiting his debt reforms for profit and compares Solon's moderate, trust-based leadership to the more forceful, royal authority of the Spartan lawgiver Lycurgus.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Lycurgus, debt cancellation, Chreokopidai, land redistribution, Athens, Sparta, political reform, Hercules, Polyzelus the Rhodian</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues from the discussion of Solon’s moderate approach compared to the forceful methods of Lycurgus. It describes the Athenian public's eventual acceptance of Solon’s reforms and his restructuring of the Athenian social and legal order.</meta>

...perceiving the benefit and setting aside their private complaints, they offered a sacrifice in common, naming the sacrifice the *Seisachtheia* <note>original: "σεισάχθεια" (seisachtheia), meaning "the shaking off of burdens," referring to the cancellation of debts</note>, and they appointed Solon as the reformer of the constitution and their lawgiver. 

They did not grant him power over some things while withholding it from others; rather, they entrusted everything to him equally: the magistracies, the assemblies, the law-courts, and the councils. He was given the power to define the property qualification, the number, and the timing for each of these, dissolving or maintaining existing institutions and establishing new ones as he saw fit. 

First, then, he repealed all the laws of Draco <note>Draco was an earlier Athenian legislator (c. 621 BCE) whose legal code was famously harsh; the word "draconian" survives in English today to describe such severity</note> except for those concerning homicide, because of their severity and the magnitude of the penalties. For almost all offenders, a single penalty was decreed: death. Consequently, even those convicted of idleness were put to death, and those who stole vegetables or fruit were punished in the same way as temple-robbers and murderers. 

Because of this, Demades <note>an Athenian orator of the 4th century BCE</note> later became famous for saying that Draco wrote his laws in blood and not in ink. Draco himself, as they say, when asked why he set death as the penalty for most offenses, replied that he considered the small ones to be worthy of it, and for the great ones, he had no greater penalty available.

Second, wishing to leave all the high offices in the hands of the wealthy as they had been, but to involve the rest of the citizenry in the government—from which the common people had previously been excluded—Solon took a census of the citizens' property. 

Those who produced five hundred measures of dry and liquid goods combined, he placed in the first rank and called them <term>Pentakosiomedimnoi</term> <note>original: "πεντακοσιομέδιμνοι"; literally "five-hundred-bushel men," the highest economic class in Athens</note>. 

In the second rank, he placed those who were able to maintain a horse or produce three hundred measures; these they called the <term>Hippyda telountas</term> <note>original: "ἱππάδα τελοῦντες"; those belonging to the Knight or Cavalry class</note>. 

The <term>Zeugitai</term> <note>original: "ζευγῖται"; literally "yoke-men," likely referring to those who owned a yoke of oxen for plowing</note> were the names given to those of the third census, whose production was two hundred measures of both kinds combined. 

All the rest were called <term>Thetes</term> <note>original: "θῆτες"; the laboring class or landless peasants</note>. To these, he gave no right to hold any office, but allowed them only to participate in the assembly and serve as jurors in the courts.

<summary>Solon is officially appointed as the sole legislator of Athens, repealing Draco’s infamously harsh laws and establishing a new social hierarchy based on agricultural production rather than birthright.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Draco, Seisachtheia, Pentakosiomedimnoi, Zeugitai, Thetes, Athenian Constitution, property census, law reform</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of the lawgiver Solon, specifically moving from his general appointment as a reformer to the specific legal mechanisms he established to balance power between the wealthy elite and the common people.</meta>

->+<-

Moreover, within his constitution, there was a feature that—while most excellent—did not appear to be of great significance when it was first devised. He directed the majority of disputes to the jurors. Indeed, he ordained that for cases judged by the magistrates, he granted the right of appeal to the law-court <note>The "Heliaia," the supreme court of ancient Athens, where citizens acted as jurors</note> for those who wished it. It is also said that he wrote the laws somewhat unclearly, containing many potential interpretations <note>original: "κλήσεις" (klēseis); here referring to various legal claims or invitations for interpretation</note>, in order to increase the power of the courts. For since no part of their disputes could be settled definitively by the laws, it happened that the citizens always had to go to the jurors, and every dispute was submitted to them, making the jurors, in a way, the masters of the laws. He himself testifies to this in his own verses, where he says:

> For I gave the people as much power as is sufficient for their honor,
> neither taking away from it, nor reaching for more.
> As for those who had power and were admired for their wealth,
> I saw to it that they suffered nothing unseemly.
> I stood casting a strong shield over both sides,
> and I did not allow either to triumph unjustly. Ⓐ Still, again, thinking it

necessary to help the weakness of the common people, he gave everyone the right to seek justice on behalf of someone who had been wronged. For if a person was struck, harmed, or violated, it was permitted for anyone who was able and willing to indict the wrongdoer and prosecute him. He acted rightly in legislating this for the citizens, as if they were all members of one body <note>This "body politic" metaphor suggests that an injury to one citizen is an injury to the entire community</note> and should become accustomed to feeling each other's pain. They remember a saying of his that agrees with this law; for when he was asked, it seems, which city is best governed, he replied: "The one in which those who are not wronged come forward and punish the wrongdoers no less than those who are wronged."

Having established the Council of the Areopagus <note>original: "Ἀρείῳ πάγῳ" (Areios Pagos), the "Hill of Ares," where a council of former high magistrates met to oversee the laws</note> from the yearly <term>archons</term> <gloss>magistrates</gloss>—a body of which he himself was a member—and seeing the people still swelling with pride and becoming bold
<margin>hm</margin>
due to the cancellation of their debts, he additionally assigned a second council. This was composed of one hundred men from each of the four tribes. Ⓐ He ordered these men to deliberate beforehand <note>original: "προβουλεύειν" (probouleuein); the Council of Four Hundred acted as a steering committee to ensure the public assembly didn't act impulsively</note> on behalf of the people, and to allow nothing to [be brought before the assembly without prior consideration].

<meta>This page continues the biography of Solon, the Athenian lawgiver. On the previous page, the author discussed Solon’s constitutional reforms, specifically his empowerment of the popular law courts as a check against the magistrates. This page explores the establishment of the Council of the Areopagus and some of Solon's more unusual social laws.</meta>

...having unexpectedly jumped into Phereky<unclear>...y</unclear><insert>1</insert> <note>original: "Φερέκυ" (Phereky); this appears to be a scribal error or corruption in the manuscript for "the affairs of state" (τα πράγματα)</note>. He established the **upper council** <note>The Council of the Areopagus, composed of former high-ranking magistrates</note> as the overseer of all things and the guardian of the laws. He believed that if the city were moored to two councils as if to two anchors, it would be less tossed by storms and would keep the people in a state of greater calm.

Most people say, as has been mentioned, that Solon was the one who established the Council of the Areopagus. This view seems to be supported primarily by the fact that **Draco** <note>The lawgiver who preceded Solon, known for his harsh penalties</note> nowhere mentions or names the Areopagites, but instead always addresses the <term>Ephetae</term> <note>A group of 51 judges who presided over homicide cases before Solon's reforms</note> concerning matters of homicide. However, the **thirteenth revolving tablet** <note>original: "ἄξων" (axon); Solon’s laws were inscribed on four-sided wooden pillars that could be turned for reading</note> of Solon contains the eighth law, which is written in these exact words:

> "Of the disenfranchised, all those who were without civic rights before Solon became Archon shall have their rights restored. This excludes those who, having been convicted by the Council of the Areopagus, or the <term>Ephetae</term>, or the <term>Prytaneum</term> <note>A council of executive leaders</note> by the Kings, fled on charges of murder, slaughter, or seeking tyranny, at the time this law was published."

These words show, conversely, that the Council of the Areopagus existed before Solon’s archonship and his legislation. For who could have been convicted by the Areopagus before Solon, if Solon was the first to give the Council of the Areopagus the power to judge? Unless, by Zeus, there is some obscurity in the text or a missing piece of information. Perhaps the law means that those convicted on charges usually judged by the Areopagites, <term>Ephetae</term>, and <term>Prytaneis</term> at the time the law was published should remain disenfranchised, while all others are restored to their rights. You should consider this matter for yourself.

Of his remaining laws, the most peculiar and surprising is the one ordering that any man who does not join one side or the other during <term>stasis</term> <note>original: "στάσει"; civil strife or internal factional conflict</note> shall be disenfranchised <note>original: "ἄτιμον" (atimon); stripped of the rights of citizenship</note>. It seems he intended that a person should not be indifferent or insensitive to the public good by securing his own private affairs and priding himself on not sharing in the suffering or the "sickness" of his fatherland. Instead, he should immediately join those who are acting for the better and more just cause, sharing in the danger and providing aid, rather than waiting safely to see which side prevails. 

Also strange and ridiculous seems the law concerning the...

<meta>This page continues Plutarch’s biography of Solon. The previous page ended with a discussion of Solon’s amnesty laws and his establishment of the Council of the Areopagus. This page moves into Solon’s social and domestic legislation, specifically concerning the rights of heiresses and the regulation of dowries to ensure marriages were based on affection and procreation rather than financial gain.</meta>

->4<-

...assigning the inheritance, having become master and lord according to the law; but so that the second might follow the first to <unclear>undergo</unclear> be married. And some say this is a wise provision regarding those who are not able to perform their marital duties, but who take <term>heiresses</term> <note>original: "ἐπίκληρος" (epikleros); in Athenian law, this was a daughter with no brothers who inherited her father’s estate. She was required to marry her nearest male relative to keep the property and family line intact.</note> for the sake of their wealth, and who use the law to violate nature. For when they see that the heiress is consorting with whomever she wishes, they will either dissolve the marriage, or they will keep her in shame, paying the penalty for their greed and insolence. It is also well-conceived that it is not permitted for her to join with just anyone, but only with the relatives of her husband, so that the child being born might be of the same house and share in the family lineage. 

To this end also contributes the practice of being shut in with the bride after she has eaten a <term>quince</term> <note>original: "μήλον κυδώνιον" (melon kydonion). In Greek tradition, the bride ate a quince—a fruit associated with Aphrodite—to symbolize that her words and breath would be sweet to her husband.</note> together with the groom. And there is the law that he who takes the heiress must meet with her at least three times every month. For even if no children are born, this is a certain honor from a husband to a virtuous wife, and a sign of affection. It removes many of the irritations that gather from time to time, and does not allow their differences to turn them completely away from each other. 

Regarding other marriages, he abolished <term>dowries</term> <note>original: "φερνάς" (phernas)</note>. He commanded that the bride should bring nothing except three garments and some furniture of small value, and nothing else should be brought by the woman being married. For he did not want marriage to be "for hire" or "for sale," but for the union of man and woman to exist for procreation, grace, and love. 

For instance, when the mother of **Dionysius** <note>Dionysius the Elder, the tyrant of Syracuse (c. 432–367 BCE)</note> asked him to give her in marriage to one of the citizens, he replied that while he had broken the laws of the city as a tyrant, he was not able to violate the laws of nature by acting as a matchmaker for those out of season <note>i.e., arranging a marriage for an older woman that was biologically "untimely."</note>. In well-ordered cities, however, this disorder should neither be necessary nor overlooked—these untimely and ungraceful couplings which have no matrimonial function or end. But for an old man marrying a young girl...

<meta>This page continues Plutarch’s biography of Solon. The previous page discussed the legal obligations regarding the marriage of heiresses (epikleroi) to ensure the continuity of the family estate. This page continues that theme before moving into Solon's laws regarding slander, wills, and the behavior of women in public.</meta>

-> + <-

A ruler or lawgiver would appear harmonious if he arranged matters so that marriage with an <term>heiress</term> <note>original: "ἀποκλήτω" (apokleto), variant of "ἐπίκληρος" (epikleros). This refers to a daughter who inherited her father's estate in the absence of male heirs.</note> was conducted with decency. In the case of a young man who marries a wealthy old woman, like "partridges" <note>original: "ὥσπερ οἱ πέρδικες" (hosper hoi perdikes). Plutarch uses a folk belief about the lustful nature of partridges to mock young men who marry older, wealthy women for money rather than procreation, suggesting they "fatten" themselves on the estate.</note> who grow fat through indulgence, he should be found out and moved to marry a maiden who is in need of a husband.

And so much for these matters.

Solon is also praised for the law that forbids **speaking ill of the dead**. For it is a pious act to consider those who have passed away as sacred; it is just to refrain from attacking those who are no longer with us; and it is statesmanlike to remove the eternity of hatred from society. 

He also forbade speaking ill of the **living** in temples, law courts, public offices, or during the viewing of games. If someone violated this, Solon ordained that they must pay three <term>drachmas</term> <note>A drachma was a standard silver coin in ancient Greece; three drachmas represented several days' wages for a skilled laborer.</note> to the injured individual and two more to the public treasury. For never to control one's anger is a sign of a lack of education and restraint, yet to do so at all times is difficult, and for some, impossible. A law must be written with a view to what is possible, if the legislator wishes to punish a few people usefully, rather than many uselessly.

He also gained a high reputation for his law concerning **wills**. Previously, it was not permitted to make a will; instead, the wealth and the household had to remain within the family line of the deceased. But Solon permitted anyone who wished—provided they had no children—to give their property to whomever they liked. In doing so, he honored friendship more than kinship, and personal favor more than legal necessity, making a man's wealth his own true possession. 

However, he did not allow such gifts to be made unconditionally. He required that the giver not be constrained by:
*   Sickness
*   Drugs or potions
*   Imprisonment
*   External necessity
*   The undue persuasion of a wife

This was a very wise provision, for Solon considered being "persuaded" against one’s best interests to be no different from being forced. He placed necessity on the same level as deceit, and pleasure on the same level as pain, believing that both are equally capable of unseating a man's reason.

He also set regulations for the **public appearances of women**, as well as their mourning and their festivals, with a law forbidding disorder and lack of restraint. To go out...

<meta>This page continues Plutarch’s biography of Solon. The previous page ended while discussing laws regarding public conduct and speech. This page details Solon's sumptuary laws—specifically those governing the behavior and mourning rituals of women—and his economic reforms aimed at encouraging trades and crafts in Athens, contrasting his approach with that of the Spartan lawgiver, Lycurgus.</meta>

->+<-

...ordering that a woman should travel with no more than three garments, and carry no more than an <term>obol's</term> <note>An obol was a small silver coin, the sixth part of a drachma; this law restricted the amount of food or drink a woman could bring on a journey to a very meager amount.</note> worth of food and drink, nor a basket larger than one <term>cubit</term> <note>A unit of length based on the forearm, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters.</note>. He also forbade women from traveling by night unless they were carried in a wagon with a lamp shining in front of them. He abolished the practice of mourners scratching their flesh <note>original: "ἀμυχὰς" (amychas). It was a common ancient custom for grieving women to scratch their cheeks until they bled.</note>, the use of prepared dirges, and the wailing for one person at the funeral of another. He did not allow the sacrifice of an ox at a grave, nor the burying of more than three garments with the deceased. Furthermore, he forbade people from visiting the tombs of others, except during the funeral procession itself. Most of these practices are also forbidden by our own current laws. However, our laws add a penalty for those who commit such acts, to be imposed by the <term>Women-wardens</term> <note>original: "Γυναικονόμων" (Gynaikonomoi). These were public officials in certain Greek cities charged with regulating the conduct and luxury of women.</note>, punishing them as unmanly and effeminate for being caught up in such excessive passions and errors.

Seeing that the city was filling with a multitude of people who were constantly flocking from all over into Attica for security, and recognizing that most of the land was poor and meager, and that the merchants who brought grain by sea were unwilling to provide it to those who had nothing to give in exchange, he turned the citizens toward the crafts. He wrote a law stating that a son was not under any legal obligation to support his father if that father had not taught him a craft. 

Perhaps for Lycurgus—who lived in a "pure" city <note>Plutarch refers to Sparta's isolation and ethnic homogeneity compared to the cosmopolitan mixture of Athens.</note> and did not have a land filled with a mixture of many foreigners—it was well, as Euripides says, to have "more of them." 

**A**nd the most important thing for an oligarchic multitude, so that the best men might not be overtaken, but instead always labor toward harmony, was that they were humble and surrounded by Lacedaemon <note>The region around Sparta.</note>; it was appropriate for them to release the citizens from the distraction of labor and <term>mechanical work</term> <note>original: "βάναυσον" (banauson). This term refers to manual labor or trades, which were often considered beneath the dignity of a full Spartan citizen (Spartiate).</note>, keeping them focused on arms, learning and practicing this one craft alone. 

**S**olon, however, adapted his laws to suit the circumstances rather than forcing the circumstances to fit the laws. Knowing that the soil of Attica was weak and only sufficient for those who farmed it, and was unable to support an idle and leisurely mob, he bestowed dignity upon the crafts. He also charged the Council of the **Areopagus** <note>The ancient "Hill of Ares," the high court of Athens, which Solon empowered to oversee the moral and legal conduct of the citizens.</note> with...

<summary>Solon establishes sumptuary laws to curb excessive mourning and luxury among women, and mandates that fathers teach their sons a trade to ensure economic stability in resource-poor Athens.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, sumptuary laws, funeral customs, Women-wardens, Gynaikonomoi, crafts, manual labor, Lycurgus, Sparta, Areopagus</keywords>

...ordered the Areopagus to oversee how each man obtains his necessities and to punish those who are idle. But more useful still was the law that those born of a courtesan <note>original: "ἑταίρας" (hetairas) - a professional female companion in ancient Greece, often highly educated but without the legal status of a wife.</note> were not under compulsion to support their fathers, as Heraclides of Pontus has recorded. For a man who disregards what is honorable in marriage clearly does not take a woman for the sake of children, but rather for the sake of pleasure; he receives his reward, and leaves himself no right to speak freely <note>original: "παρρησίαν" (parrhēsian) - the ancient Greek concept of frank, uninhibited speech, often viewed as a civic right and moral standing.</note> toward those born to him, for whom he has made their very birth a reproach.

In general, Solon's laws concerning women seem very numerous. For he allowed a man to kill an adulterer <note>original: "μοιχὸν" (moichon)</note> if caught in the act. But if someone kidnaps a free woman and rapes her <note>original: "βιάσηται" (biasētai) - to use force or violate.</note>, he set the fine at one hundred <term>drachmas</term> <note>A silver coin that was the standard unit of currency; at this time, 100 drachmas was a very substantial sum of money.</note>; and if he acts as a procurer <note>A pimp or someone who facilitates illicit sexual encounters.</note>, the fine is twenty drachmas. The exception is those women who openly sell themselves, by which I mean courtesans; for these women give themselves to those who pay in plain sight. Furthermore, he does not allow a man to sell his daughters or sisters, unless he should catch a maiden having had relations with a man. 

It is irrational to punish the same act sometimes harshly and relentlessly, and at other times lightly and in jest by fixing a small fine as a penalty—unless, perhaps, silver was so scarce in the city at that time that the difficulty of providing it made these monetary fines quite large. In the assessments for sacrifices, for instance, he reckons a sheep and a drachma as the equivalent of a <term>medimnos</term> <note>A unit of dry measure used for grain, roughly equivalent to 52 liters.</note>. To a victor at the <term>Isthmian Games</term> <note>One of the four great Panhellenic athletic festivals, held near Corinth.</note>, he ordered the city to give one hundred drachmas; to a victor at the <term>Olympic Games</term>, five hundred. To one who brought in a wolf, he gave five drachmas; and for a wolf-cub, one drachma. <margin>61</margin> As Demetrius of Phalerum says, the former was the price of an ox, and the latter the price of a sheep. For the prices he defines on the twelfth <note>original: "δεκάτῃ καὶ δευτέρῳ" (dekatē kai deuterō). These laws were originally written on revolving wooden tablets called *axones*.</note> tablet for chosen sacrificial victims are likely many times higher than these, yet even those are cheap compared to current prices. <meta>This page continues the discussion of Solon’s legislative reforms in Athens. While the previous page addressed domestic issues like marriage and mourning, this section shifts to the formal classification of the citizenry and practical agricultural regulations necessitated by the dry Attic climate.</meta>

...not entirely effective in every respect. The ways of life were first named according to the classes into which the people were divided. To the warrior class, the name **Hoplites** <note>original: "ὁπλῖτας" (hoplitas) — heavy-armed infantrymen who provided their own armor.</note> was given. To the laborers, the name **Ergadeis** <note>original: "ἐργάδεις" (ergadeis) — the artisan or craftsman class.</note>. Of the remaining two, the **Geleontes** <note>original: "Γελέοντες" (Geleontes)</note> were the farmers, while the **Aigikoreis** <note>original: "αἰγικορεῖς" (aigikoreis) — literally 'goat-herds'.</note> were those who spent their time in the pastures and with the flocks.

Since the land was not well-supplied with water, having neither ever-flowing rivers nor lakes nor abundant springs, but most people instead used man-made wells, Solon wrote a law: where there was a public well within an *ippikon* <note>original: "ἱππικοῦ" (hippikou) — a distance associated with a horse-race course.</note>, that well was to be used. The *ippikon* distance was defined as four **stadia** <note>original: "στάδια" (stadia) — approximately 740 meters or nearly half a mile.</note>. But where the distance was greater, people were to seek their own water. If, however, they dug to a depth of ten **fathoms** <note>original: "ὀργυιῶν" (orgyiōn) — an 'orguia' is the span of a man's outstretched arms, roughly six feet.</note> on their own property and did not find any, then they could take water from a neighbor, filling a six-gallon jar twice each day. He felt it necessary to assist those in need, but not to encourage laziness.

He also determined the measurements for planting with great expertise. For those planting in a field, he ordered them to stay five feet away from the neighbor’s property; but for those planting a **fig tree** <note>original: "συκῆν" (sykēn)</note> or an **olive tree** <note>original: "ἐλαίαν" (elaian)</note>, they must stay nine feet away. For these trees spread their roots further and are not harmless neighbors to all plants; they take away nourishment and even damage some by releasing certain discharges. He ordered that anyone wishing to dig a pit or a trench could do so at whatever depth they chose, provided it was at a distance from the neighbor's property equal to its own depth. And when setting up hives of **bees** <note>original: "μελιττῶν" (melittōn)</note>, one must keep them three hundred feet away from those previously established by another.

Of the products of the land, he allowed only **olive oil** <note>original: "ἐλαίου" (elaiou)</note> to be sold to foreigners, while forbidding the export of anything else. He ordered the Archon <note>The chief magistrate of Athens.</note> to pronounce curses against those who exported such items, or else pay one hundred **drachmas** <note>original: "δραχμὰς" (drachmas)</note> into the public treasury. This law is on the first **axon** <note>original: "ἄξων" (axōn) — the revolving wooden tablets on which Solon's laws were inscribed.</note>. This is why one would not find it completely unbelievable when people say that the export of **figs** <note>original: "σύκων" (sykōn)</note> was also forbidden in ancient times. And they say that the act of denouncing those who exported them led to such informers being called **sycophants** <note>original: "συκοφάντην" (sykophantēn) — literally 'one who shows the fig'. In modern English, this means a flatterer, but in ancient Athens, it referred to a professional informer or malicious accuser who 'revealed' illegal exports.</note>. He also wrote...

<meta>This page continues Plutarch’s account of Solon’s legislation. Having previously discussed agricultural water rights and land use, the text now transitions into laws governing civil damages, requirements for naturalized citizenship, and the physical preservation of the laws themselves on wooden structures. It concludes with the beginning of Solon’s calendar reforms.</meta>

->+<-

He also wrote a law regarding damages, in which he commands that if a dog should bite someone, the owner is to hand it over bound to a wooden collar <note>original: "κλοιῷ" (kloiō) — a heavy wooden block or clog worn around the neck to restrain the animal.</note> three cubits <note>Approximately 4.5 feet or 1.3 meters.</note> long. This was a clever device intended for public safety. However, the law regarding naturalized citizens <note>original: "δημοποιήτων" (demopoiētōn) — literally "those made into the people."</note> presents some difficulty. It does not allow anyone to become a citizen except those who are in perpetual exile from their own land or those who move to Athens with their entire households to practice a craft <note>original: "ἐπὶ τέχνῃ" (epi technē). Solon aimed to boost the Athenian economy by attracting skilled laborers.</note>. They say he did this not so much to drive away others, but to invite these specific groups to Athens with the certain promise of participating in the citizenship. He believed that those who had lost their own country by necessity, or had left it by choice, would be loyal citizens.

Unique to Solon also was the regulation concerning public dining, which he ordained should be provided at the **Prytaneion** <note>The seat of government in ancient Athens where the "sacred fire" of the city was kept and where distinguished citizens and guests were fed at public expense.</note>. He did not permit the same person to eat there often, but if one whose duty it was refused to attend, he would be punished. He considered the former to be an act of greed, and the latter a disdain for public affairs. He gave validity to all his laws for one hundred years. They were written on wooden "axles" <note>original: "ἄξονας" (axonas) — four-sided wooden blocks that could be rotated to read different sections of the law.</note> held within frames that allowed them to turn. Small remains of these were still preserved in the **Prytaneion** in our time. As Aristotle says, they were also called **Kyrbeis** <note>original: "κύρβεις" — another term for these law-tablets, sometimes specifically associated with religious or sacred law.</note>. Indeed, the comic poet Cratinus says somewhere regarding Solon and Draco: "By whose **kyrbeis** they now roast their parched barley." <note>Cratinus was joking that the wooden law-tablets had become so old and obsolete that people were using them as firewood for cooking.</note>

Some say that specifically those tablets containing sacred rites and sacrifices were called **kyrbeis**, while the others were named **axones**. Now, the Council swore a common oath to confirm the laws of Solon. Additionally, each of the **Thesmothetai** <note>The six "law-givers" or junior archons of Athens.</note> made an individual vow in the **Agora** <note>The central marketplace and civic heart of Athens.</note> at the [Royal] Stone, declaring that if he should transgress any of the statutes, he would dedicate a golden statue of equal size to himself at Delphi.

Observing the irregularity of the month and that the motion of the moon coincided neither with the setting nor the rising of the sun, but often on the same day the moon both caught up with and passed the sun, he ordained that very day... <note>Solon was attempting to align the lunar and solar calendars. The text here refers to the final day of the month, which he named "The Old and the New" (henē kai nea), because it shared time with both the outgoing and incoming moon.</note>

<meta>This page continues Plutarch’s biography of the Athenian lawgiver Solon. Having previously discussed the social and civil laws, the text now details Solon’s reform of the calendar and his subsequent voluntary exile from Athens to avoid constant petitions for legal amendments. He begins his famous travels to Egypt and Cyprus.</meta>

...that day should also be called by another name <note>original: "ἑτέραν κἀκεῖναν καλεῖσθαι" (heteran kakeinan kaleisthai). Plutarch refers to the last day of the month, which Solon named the "Old and New" day.</note>. The portion of the day before the moon’s conjunction with the sun he called "evening," while the remainder he called the beginning of the conjunction. He seems to have been the first to understand correctly and gently, without complex arguments <margin>>></margin>, that a month consists of both a "waning" part and a "waxing" part <note>original: "τὸ μὲν φθίνοντος μηνὸς, τὸ δὲ ἱσταμένοιο" (to men phthinontos mēnos, to de histamenoio). These were the traditional Greek divisions of the lunar month.</note>. The following day he named the **New Moon** <term>noumēnía: the first day of the lunar month</term>. Regarding the days after the twentieth, he did not add to the count, but rather counted backwards and subtracted from the total light of the moon, numbering them down to the thirtieth.

After his laws were enacted, some citizens would approach him every day, either praising or criticizing them, or advising him to insert whatever occurred to them into the written text, or to take something away. Most numerous, however, were those who questioned and cross-examined him, demanding that he explain the exact meaning and intent of every specific provision. Solon saw that to refuse these requests would be seen as strange, but to grant them would invite resentment <note>original: "ἐπίφθονον" (epiphthonon) — implying that by explaining his laws, he would be forced into arguments that would make him enemies.</note>. In short, wishing to escape these difficulties and to flee the dissatisfaction and fault-finding of his fellow citizens—for, as he himself said, **"In great deeds, it is hard to please everyone"**—he used his status as a ship-owner as a pretext for travel and set sail. He requested a ten-year leave of absence from the Athenians, hoping that during this time, they would become accustomed to his laws as a matter of habit.

First, therefore, he arrived in Egypt and stayed there, as he himself says in his poems:
<margin>></margin> "At the mouths of the Nile, near the Canopic shore."
<margin>></margin> For some time, he studied philosophy alongside **Psenophis of Heliopolis** and **Sonchis of Sais**, who were the most learned among the Egyptian priests. From them, as Plato tells us, he heard the story of **Atlantis** <note>This refers to the legend Solon allegedly brought back from Egypt, which later served as the basis for Plato's dialogues *Timaeus* and *Critias*.</note>, and he attempted to introduce this account to the Greeks through the medium of a poem.

Later, he sailed to Cyprus and was especially loved by one of the kings there named **Philocyprus**. This king possessed a small city founded by **Demophon**, the son of Theseus <note>A legendary Athenian hero, suggesting a deep ancestral link between Athens and Cyprus.</note>...

<summary>Solon reforms the Athenian calendar and then departs on a ten-year journey to Egypt and Cyprus to allow his laws time to take root without further interference. During his travels, he studies with Egyptian priests and learns the legend of Atlantis.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Plutarch’s account of Solon’s travels after leaving Athens. Following his time in Egypt, Solon has arrived in Cyprus, where he becomes a close advisor to the local king, Philocypros.</meta>

...around the Clarius river. The city was situated in a location that was naturally fortified, but otherwise difficult and poor. Solon persuaded the king <note>Philocypros, the king of Soloi in Cyprus.</note> to relocate the city to the beautiful plain lying below, and to establish a greater power there. Being present himself, he oversaw the settlement. He helped organize it in the best possible way for both a pleasant lifestyle and for safety, so that many settlers soon flocked to the site. This caused other kings to look upon him with envy. Therefore, to pay honor to Solon, the king—who had previously called the city Aipeia <note>original: "αἰπεῖαν" (aipeian), meaning "high" or "steep."</note>—renamed it **Soloi** after him. Solon himself mentions this settlement, addressing Philocypros in his elegies <note>Solon was a renowned poet as well as a lawmaker; his verses often served as political and historical records.</note> as follows:

> May you rule the Solians for a long time here,
> inhabiting this city, and your lineage after you.
> But as for me, may violet-crowned Cypris <note>original: "κύπρις ἰοστέφανος" (kypris iostephanos). Cypris is a title for the goddess Aphrodite, whose legendary birthplace was Cyprus.</note>
> send me safely from this famous island in a swift ship.
> Upon this settlement, may she bestow grace and noble glory,
> and a good return to my own fatherland. +

Regarding his meeting with Croesus <note>Croesus was the King of Lydia, legendary for his immense wealth.</note>, some think they can prove it is a fiction based on chronology. However, for a story so famous and supported by so many witnesses—and what is even more important, one so fitting to Solon's character and worthy of his high-mindedness and wisdom—I do not think I should reject it for the sake of certain so-called "chronological canons." These are rules which thousands of people are still trying to correct to this day, yet they have never been able to reconcile the contradictions among themselves. 

And so, they say that when Solon arrived in **Sardis** <term>Sardis: the capital city of the Lydian Empire</term> to visit Croesus, he experienced something similar to a man from the inland visiting the sea for the first time. For just as such a man, seeing a large river coming from a mountain, might think it is the ocean, so too did Solon. As he walked through the court and saw many of the royal officials dressed magnificently in...
