<meta>This page continues Plutarch’s "Life of Solon," specifically the famous encounter between the Athenian lawmaker and Croesus, the fabulously wealthy King of Lydia. On the previous page, the narrator defended the historical authenticity of this meeting despite chronological discrepancies, arguing it fits Solon’s character perfectly.</meta>

And strutting about with feasts, processions, and bodyguards, Croesus seemed to be a man of great renown; until Solon came to him. Every precious stone, every richly dyed garment, and all the skillfully crafted gold ornamentation—all that might be envied or admired—was displayed so that he might appear as a solemn and most magnificent spectacle. 

This display was presented in its full brilliance, yet Solon did not react as Croesus expected <note>original: "ἀκροίσας" (akroisas), likely a misspelling of "heard" or "attended to."</note>. Instead, it was clear to those of sound mind that Solon looked down upon such lack of taste and petty-mindedness. Croesus then ordered his treasuries to be opened for him, leading him on a tour to display the arrangement and extravagance of his wealth <note>original: "δεσμῶν" (desmon). The scribe likely meant "δεσμῶν" as 'hoards' or 'stores', though the reading is slightly unclear.</note>. It was enough for him to provide Solon with a full understanding of his status. When Solon had seen everything, Croesus asked him if he knew of any man more blessed <note>term: "εὐδαιμονία" (eudaimonia). This refers to a deep, flourishing happiness or "blessedness" granted by the gods, rather than just momentary joy.</note> than himself. 

Solon declared that Tellus <note>Tellus was an Athenian citizen, used by Solon to illustrate that true happiness requires a noble life and a glorious death.</note> was far more distinguished <unclear>than he</unclear>, because Tellus had been a good man, had left behind reputable children, and a life lacking in nothing, and had died gloriously while fighting bravely for his fatherland. 

Clearly, Solon seemed strange and uncouth to Croesus, because he did not measure happiness by a great quantity of silver or gold, but rather valued the life and solitary death of a common citizen more than all of Croesus's vast empire. Nevertheless, he asked him again whether, after Tellus, he knew of anyone more fortunate. 

Solon replied again, naming Cleobis and Biton <note>Two brothers from Argos famous for their physical strength and devotion to their mother, a priestess of Hera.</note>, men distinguished for their brotherly love and devotion to their mother. When their oxen were slow <insert>to arrive</insert>, they themselves got under the yoke and pulled the entire wagon to the temple of Hera, while the citizens rejoiced and called them blessed. And having sacrificed and feasted, they no longer—

<summary>Solon visits King Croesus of Lydia and remains unimpressed by his vast wealth. He famously argues that a common Athenian named Tellus and the devoted brothers Cleobis and Biton were truly happier because they lived virtuously and died honorably.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Solon’s visit to the court of King Croesus of Lydia. Having been shown the king's immense wealth, Solon is now asked to judge who is the happiest of men. This follows the previous page where Solon discussed Tellus the Athenian as a model of true happiness.</meta>

->+<-

...he stood up after a day. The Greek man <note>referring to Solon</note> had displayed such a view of death. But what is it to us if Croesus, being moved to anger, considered the man of no account among gods or men? Solon, neither wishing to flatter him nor to provoke him further, said: "O King of the Lydians <note>original: "χυδάν" (chudan), likely a scribal error for "Λυδῶν" (Lydon), meaning Lydians.</note>, God has given to us Greeks a certain modest wisdom, it seems, one that is bold and of the common people, not a kingly or brilliant one. 

This wisdom, seeing that life is always subject to all sorts of fortunes, does not allow us to be prideful because of our present goods, nor to admire a man's success <term>εὐτυχία (eutychia): "good luck" or external success, which Solon contrasts with internal "blessedness"</term> while it is still subject to the changes of time. For the future comes on in varied ways, hidden from each man. We consider that man alone to be happy whose life the divinity has brought to a successful end. 

To pronounce a man blessed <term>μακαρισμός (makarismos): a formal declaration of someone's ultimate happiness or blessed state</term> while he is still living and risking the hazards of life is like the proclamation and crowning of an athlete while the contest is still going on: it is uncertain and lacks authority."

Having said these things, Solon departed, having vexed Croesus but not having corrected him. Now Aesop <note>The famous author of fables, who was traditionally contemporary with Solon</note> happened to be in Sardis at the time, having been summoned by Croesus and held in honor. He was annoyed that Solon had received no kind treatment, and advised him, saying: "O Solon, one must speak to kings either as little as possible, or as pleasantly as possible." And Solon replied: "By Zeus, no! Either as little as possible, or as best as possible." At that time, then, Croesus held Solon in such contempt. 

But later, when he engaged with Cyrus <note>Cyrus the Great, founder of the Persian Empire</note>, he was defeated in battle and lost his city. He himself was taken alive and was about to be burned. When the pyre had been built, he was bound and placed upon it, while all the Persians looked on and Cyrus was present. He cried out as loud as he could, shouting three times: "O Solon, Solon!" Cyrus, being amazed, sent men to ask who among men or gods this was, who—

<meta>catchword: who—</meta>

<summary>Solon explains to Croesus that no man can be called truly happy until he has died well, leading to his dismissal. The narrative then shifts to Croesus’s later defeat by Cyrus, where he realizes Solon's wisdom while facing execution on a pyre.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Croesus, Cyrus the Great, Aesop, Sardis, Lydians, Persians, happiness, fortune, eudaimonia, philosophy of death</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Solon. The previous page ended with Aesop observing Solon’s frosty reception at the court of King Croesus of Lydia. This page describes how Solon’s warnings were eventually vindicated when Croesus was captured by Cyrus the Great, and then details Solon's return to an Athens torn by factional politics.</meta>

-> + <-

...Solon <note>original: "Σόλων" (Solōn)</note> [said] that only in the midst of unforeseen successes should one be called back [to reality]. And Croesus, hiding nothing, said: "This man is one of the wise men among the Greeks; I sent for him, not because I desired to hear or learn something I lacked, but so that he might be a spectator and witness to that happiness <note>original: "εὐδαιμονίας" (eudaimonias)</note>, the loss of which has turned out to be a greater evil than the possession of it was a good. For while it was present, it was merely a matter of words and reputation; but the changes of it have brought me to terrible sufferings and incurable calamities where it ended. And that man, judging these things then from his travels, commanded me to look to the end of life, and not to be arrogant or prideful based on unstable expectations." 

When these words were reported to Cyrus <note>Cyrus the Great, founder of the Persian Empire, who had just defeated Croesus</note>, Solon was shown to be wiser than Croesus; and Cyrus, seeing the power of Solon's word in this living example, released Croesus. Furthermore, he continued to treat him with honor for as long as he lived. Thus, Solon gained the reputation of having, with a single word, saved one king and educated another. 

But back in the city [of Athens], everyone was in a state of civil strife <note>original: "ἐστασίαζον" (estasiazon), referring to stasis or political factionalism</note> while Solon was away. Lycurgus was the leader of the <term>Pedieis (Men of the Plain)</term> <note>The aristocratic faction who owned the fertile inland territory</note>; Megacles, the son of Alcmaeon, led the <term>Paralioi (Men of the Coast)</term> <note>The merchant and fishing faction</note>. Pisistratus led the <term>Diakrioi (Men of the Highlands)</term> <note>The faction from the poorer, mountainous regions</note>, among whom was the <term>thetic crowd</term> <note>original: "θητικὸς ὄχλος" (thētikos ochlos), the 'thetes' were the lowest social class of manual laborers</note>, who were especially resentful of the wealthy. Consequently, although the city still followed the laws, everyone expected a revolution. All longed for a new state of affairs, not hoping for equality, but expecting to gain an advantage in the change and to completely overpower their rivals. 

While matters were in this state, Solon arrived back in Athens. He was held in reverence and honor by all. However, in public speaking and political action, he was no longer as capable or energetic due to his old age. Nevertheless, meeting privately with the men leading the factions, he tried to reconcile them and bring them into harmony. It seemed that Pisistratus especially...

<meta>This page continues Plutarch’s "Life of Solon." The previous page described the factions within Athens—the Men of the Plain, the Men of the Coast, and the Men of the Highlands—led by Lycurgus, Megacles, and Peisistratos respectively. This section focuses on the character of Peisistratos and Solon's reaction to the birth of Greek tragedy.</meta>

->+<-

...to be recognized. For he was also charming and friendly in his speech and dealings. He was helpful to the poor and, in his enmities, he was reasonable and moderate. Even the qualities he did not naturally possess, he would imitate, and he was believed above all others because of his speech, appearing as a cautious and orderly man. This was especially true of him: he pretended to be displeased with those who sought to overturn the present state of affairs. For through such deceptions, he misled many. But Solon <note>original: "Σόλων" (Solōn)</note> detected his true character and his underlying plots. He did not, however, hate him, but tried to soften him and advise him. Solon would approach him and say that if one could only remove his **ambition** <note>original: "φιλόπρωτον" (philoprōton), literally 'the desire to be first'</note> from his soul and his desire for **tyranny** <note>A "tyrant" in this context refers to an absolute ruler who seized power unconstitutionally, not necessarily a cruel one.</note>, there was no other man more naturally gifted for virtue, nor a better citizen.

When **Thespis** <note>Thespis of Icaria (6th century BCE), traditionally considered the first person to appear on stage as an actor playing a character, rather than just speaking as himself.</note> and his followers were beginning to move **tragedy** <note>original: "τραγῳδίαν" (tragōdian)</note> forward—a matter that attracted many because of its novelty, though it had not yet been brought into a formal competitive contest—Solon, who was by nature fond of listening and learning, and even more so in his old age, devoted himself to leisure and play, and by Zeus, to wine and music. He went to watch Thespis himself performing, as was the custom of the ancients. 

After the performance, Solon addressed him and asked if he was not ashamed to tell such great lies <note>original: "ψευδόμενος" (pseudomenos)</note> in front of so many people. When Thespis replied that there was no harm in saying and doing such things in "play," Solon struck the ground hard with his staff and said:

"If we praise and honor this 'play' in this way, we shall soon find it appearing in our business contracts."

Later, when **Peisistratos** <note>A populist leader and distant relative of Solon who eventually became the tyrant of Athens.</note> had wounded himself and came into the marketplace being carried on a chariot, he incited the common people, claiming that he had been plotted against by his enemies because of his political stance. He had many people surrounding him, acting with indignation and shouting. Solon, coming near and standing by him, said: "O son of Hippokrates <note>Hippokrates was the father of Peisistratos.</note>, you do not perform your part well..."

<summary>Solon identifies the deceptive ambition of the populist Peisistratos and expresses deep skepticism toward the birth of theater, fearing that the "lies" of actors will eventually infect public truth and legal contracts.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Peisistratos, Thespis, tragedy, Athenian politics, deception, theater, Hippokrates, tyranny</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues Plutarch’s "Life of Solon." The previous page described how Solon warned against the deceptive nature of Peisistratos, who was using the newly invented art of tragedy to manipulate public emotion. This page details Peisistratos's famous trick to seize power and Solon's futile resistance.</meta>

-> + <-

...he might play the part of the **Homeric Odysseus** <note>Solon is comparing Peisistratos to Odysseus, who in Homer's *Odyssey* famously wounded himself and wore rags to enter Troy undetected as a spy.</note>. For he was deceiving the citizens with those same tricks by which that hero deceived his enemies, having wounded himself. As a result of this, the multitude was ready to fight on behalf of Peisistratos, and the people gathered in an assembly. When **Ariston** <note>An Athenian politician who proposed the decree to grant Peisistratos a guard.</note> proposed that a bodyguard of fifty **club-bearers** <term>original: "κορυνηφόροι" (korynephoroi); these were guards armed with wooden clubs rather than the traditional metal spears of soldiers, making them appear less like a military coup and more like a personal security detail.</term> be given to Peisistratos, Solon stood up and spoke against it, going through many points similar to those he wrote in his poems: "For you look only to the tongue and the words of a wily man. Each one of you individually walks with the steps of a fox; but in all of you together there is a **hollow mind** <note>original: "χαῦνος νόος" (chaunos noos); Solon is rebuking the Athenians for being collectively foolish despite their individual cleverness.</note>." Seeing the poor rushing to please Peisistratos and making an uproar, while the rich were slipping away and behaving like cowards, he left, saying that he was wiser than some and braver than others: wiser than those who did not understand what was happening, and braver than those who understood but were afraid to oppose the **tyranny** <note>A "tyrant" in this historical context was a leader who took power by force or extra-legal means, often with the support of the common people against the aristocracy.</note>.

The people, having ratified the decree, no longer disputed with Peisistratos about the number of club-bearers he kept, but openly overlooked it until he had seized as many as he wished, until he finally occupied the **Acropolis** <note>The high, fortified citadel of Athens; seizing it was the definitive act of taking control of the city.</note>. When this happened and the city was thrown into confusion, **Megacles** <note>The leader of the coastal faction and a rival to Peisistratos.</note> immediately fled with the other **Alcmaeonids** <note>A powerful and wealthy noble family of Athens that was often in conflict with the city's tyrants.</note>. Solon, though he was already very old and had no one to help him, nevertheless went out into the **marketplace** <note>The Agora; the heart of Athenian public and political life.</note> and spoke to the citizens, reproaching them for their lack of counsel and weakness, and further inciting and encouraging them not to surrender their liberty. At that time he also said the memorable thing: that previously it would have been easier for them to prevent the tyranny while it was forming, but now it is a greater and more glorious task to cut it out and destroy it now that it has already been established and has taken 
-> root. <-
-> [Catchword: for] <-

<summary>Solon unsuccessfully warns the Athenians against granting Peisistratos a bodyguard of club-bearers, which leads to Peisistratos seizing the Acropolis and establishing a tyranny.</summary>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous account where Solon unsuccessfully warned the Athenian assembly about Peisistratos’s intentions to seize power. Having failed to convince the people, Solon now takes a symbolic final stand before the new regime takes hold.</meta>

-> + <-

But since no one paid any attention to him because of their fear, Solon went away to his own home. He took up his weapons and placed them in the narrow street before his doors, saying: "For my part, I have aided my fatherland and the laws as far as my power allowed." After this, he remained quiet. Although his friends urged him to flee, he did not listen to them. Instead, he wrote poems in which he reproached the **Athenians** <note>original: "Ἀθηναίοις" (Athenians); Solon frequently used poetry as a medium for political commentary and public education.</note>:

<margin>></margin> If you have suffered piteously because of your own cowardice,
<margin>></margin> Do not attribute any portion of this fate to the gods.
<margin>></margin> For you yourselves increased these men’s power by giving them guards,
<margin>></margin> And because of this, you have gained the evil of **slavery** <note>original: "δουλοσύνην" (doulosynēn); in this context, Solon refers to political subjugation under a tyrant rather than chattel slavery.</note>.

When many warned him that he would be put to death by the **tyrant** <term>original: "τύραννος" (tyrannos); a ruler who seized power unconstitutionally, though at this time the word did not yet carry the purely negative connotations of modern "tyranny."</term> and asked what he was relying on to act so recklessly, he replied: "On my old age." Nevertheless, once **Peisistratos** had become master of the city's affairs, he treated Solon with such great care—honoring him, showing him affection, and summoning him to court—that Solon eventually became his advisor and approved of many of his actions. For Peisistratos maintained most of Solon’s laws, adhering to them first himself and compelling his friends to do the same. Indeed, even when he was already ruling as a tyrant, Peisistratos was summoned to the **Areopagus** <term>original: "Ἄρειος πάγος" (Areios Pagos); the "Hill of Ares," which served as the high court of Athens for homicide cases.</term> on a charge of murder, and he appeared there in a decorous manner to make his defense; however, his accuser did not show up. 

Peisistratos also enacted other laws himself, one of which commands that those who have been **maimed in war** <note>This is one of the earliest recorded instances of a state-sponsored disability pension for veterans.</note> should be maintained at the public expense. **Heracleides** <note>Heracleides of Pontus, a philosopher and student of Plato and Aristotle.</note> says that Peisistratos was following the example of Solon, who had already passed a decree regarding a man named **Hieronymus** who had been maimed. Furthermore, as **Theophrastus** <note>A successor to Aristotle in the Peripatetic school.</note> has recorded, it was not Solon but Peisistratos who established the **law against idleness** <note>original: "ἀργίας νόμον" (argias nomon); a law requiring citizens to prove they had a means of support, intended to prevent civil unrest.</note>, by which he made the countryside more productive and the city more peaceful. But Solon, having already handled great matters such as the leadership concerning the **Salaminian Gulf** <note>A reference to the war between Athens and Megara over the island of Salamis, which Solon helped Athens win.</note>, did not follow through on...

<summary>Solon makes a final symbolic protest against Peisistratos’s tyranny before retiring to write poetry. Surprisingly, Peisistratos maintains Solon's laws and the two achieve a level of mutual respect, with Peisistratos even appearing before the high court.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the "Life of Solon" by Plutarch. It follows the account of Solon’s reluctant reconciliation with the tyrant Peisistratos and transitions into the story of Solon’s unfinished literary project regarding Atlantis, which Plato would later attempt to complete. This section provides a famous comparison between an unfinished building and an unfinished book.</meta>

...brought his history to the **Athenians** <note>original: "Ἀθηναίοις"; here referring to the account of the lost continent of Atlantis, which Solon reportedly heard from Egyptian priests.</note>, but he grew weary. It was not, as **Plato** <note>The philosopher Plato was a descendant of Solon and used Solon’s unfinished notes as the basis for his dialogues *Timaeus* and *Critias*.</note> says, a lack of a starting point, but rather because he was hindered by old age and frightened by the sheer magnitude of the writing. In his leisure, these famous verses remained his guiding words:

> I grow old ever learning many things.

And:

> And now the works of the **Cyprus-born goddess** <note>original: "Κυπρογενοῦς" (Kyprogenous); an epithet for Aphrodite, the goddess of love, who was said to have risen from the sea near Cyprus.</note> are dear to me,
> and those of **Dionysus** <note>The god of wine and theater.</note> and the **Muses** <note>The nine goddesses of artistic and intellectual inspiration.</note>,
> which bring true cheer to men.

As if it were the vacant soil of a beautiful estate, **Plato** <note>original: "Πλάτων"</note> somehow felt it was fitting to elaborate upon and adorn the **Atlantic story** <term>original: "Ἀτλαντικὴν ὑπόθεσιν" (Atlantikēn hypothesin); the legendary account of the island of Atlantis.</term>. He bestowed upon it great porches, enclosures, and courtyards, such as no other story, myth, or poem ever possessed.

<note>A faint, decorative watermark or sketch depicts an ornamental finial or crest in the right margin, likely added by a later scribe or owner of the manuscript.</note>

But because he began the work late in his life, his life came to an end before the task was finished. The more the written parts delight us, the more we are pained by what was left incomplete. For just as the city of the **Athenians** left the **Temple of Olympian Zeus** <term>original: "Ὀλυμπιεῖον" (Olympieion); a massive temple in Athens that remained unfinished for centuries, finally completed by the Emperor Hadrian.</term> incomplete, so the wisdom of Plato left the Atlantic story as his only unfinished work among many beautiful ones.

As for Solon’s life, he lived for a significant time after **Peisistratos** <note>The tyrant who seized power in Athens in 561 BCE.</note> began his rule—though not as long as **Heraclides of Pontus** <note>A philosopher and historian who often recorded more legendary or exaggerated accounts.</note> claims. According to **Phanias of Eresus** <note>A pupil of Aristotle and a historian.</note>, he lived less than two years longer. For Peisistratos began his tyranny during the archonship of **Comias** <note>original: "Κωμίου"; the Archon Eponymous of 561/0 BCE.</note>, and Phanias says Solon died during the archonship of **Hegestratus** <note>original: "Ἡγεστράτου"; the Archon of 560/59 BCE.</note>, who succeeded Comias.

The story that his body was burned and his **ashes** <note>original: "τέφρας" (tephras)</note> were scattered around the island of **Salamis** <note>The island where Solon won his greatest military and political fame.</note> is entirely incredible and legendary; yet it has been recorded by other noteworthy men, and even by **Aristotle the philosopher**. + +

+      +      +      +      +      +      +      +

+

Publicola

<note>A vertical red ink ornament with symmetrical scrollwork and floral motifs is situated at the beginning of the text block.</note> Such a man [Solon] having been, it seems he is compared to **Publicola** <note>original: "ποπλικολαν" (poplikolan); a title meaning "Friend of the People," derived from the Latin *populus* (people) and *colere* (to cherish or cultivate).</note>. This was the name the Roman people later devised for him in his honor; before that, he was called **Publius Valerius**. He was thought to be a descendant of that ancient **Valerius** who was the cause of the reconciliation between the **Romans** and the **Sabines** <note>The Sabines were a neighboring tribe; according to legend, Valerius helped negotiate a peaceful merger after the Romans had abducted Sabine women.</note> when they were at war. For it was he who most effectively persuaded the kings to meet together and brought them to a mutual agreement.

**Valerius** <note>original: "οὐαλέριος"</note>, belonging to this lineage, was already—as they say—distinguished by his eloquence and wealth while **Rome** was still under the rule of kings. He possessed a unique character, always using his influence rightly and with great boldness of speech <note>original: "παρρησίας" (parrhesias); a term for frank, honest speech often associated with democratic citizens.</note> in defense of what was just. Because he was generous and philanthropic in assisting those in need, it was immediately clear that if a **democracy** <term>original: "δημοκρατία" (dēmokratia); here Plutarch uses the Greek term for democracy to describe the early Roman Republic.</term> were ever established, he would be a leading figure.

When **Tarquinius Superbus** <note>original: "ταρκύνιος οὐπέρβος" (Tarquinios Ouperbos); Tarquin the Proud, the legendary seventh and final king of Rome.</note> took power—which he did neither honorably, but rather impiously and unlawfully, nor did he exercise it like a king, but with insolence and tyranny—the people hated and were burdened by him. They found the occasion for revolt through the tragedy of **Lucretia** <note>A noblewoman whose rape by the king’s son and subsequent suicide became the catalyst for the Roman revolution.</note>, who took her own life after being violated. **Lucius Brutus** <note>original: "λεύκιος βροῦτος"; the leader of the revolution and founder of the Roman Republic.</note>, taking charge of the changing political situation, went first to **Valerius**. Finding him most eager to help, he joined him in expelling the kings. 

So long as the people were expected to choose one general to lead instead of a king, **Valerius** remained quiet, feeling that the leadership belonged more to **Brutus**, the primary leader of the...

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>Continuity check: This page follows the discussion of Publius Valerius's background and his role during the transition from the Roman monarchy to the early Republic, picking up as the new government structure is being debated.</meta>

...**democracy** <term>original: "δημοκρατία" (dēmokratia); Plutarch uses the Greek term for democracy to describe the newly formed Roman Republic.</term> having been established. But because the name of monarchy was held in such hatred, and because it seemed the people would be less burdened if the supreme authority were divided, Brutus proposed and established two rulers. Valerius hoped that he would be elected to serve as **consul** <note>original: "συνυπατεύσειν" (synhypateusein); to serve as a colleague in the consulship. The consuls were the two highest-ranking magistrates of the Roman Republic.</note> alongside Brutus, but he was disappointed in this hope. For, against the secret wishes of Brutus, **Tarquinius Collatinus** was elected as his colleague instead of Valerius. This Collatinus was the husband of **Lucretia** <note>Lucretia was a noblewoman whose rape by the king's son sparked the revolution that ended the Roman monarchy.</note>; he was not superior to Valerius in virtue, but the powerful men, fearing that the exiled kings were still making many attempts from the outside to join battle, wanted a leader who was a "friendly enemy"—someone who, because of his personal grievances against the tyrants, would never yield to them.

**Valerius**, feeling indignant that he was not trusted to do everything for the sake of his fatherland—especially since he had suffered no personal harm from the tyrants—withdrew from the **Senate** <note>original: "βουλῆς" (boulēs); literally "the council."</note>, renounced his seat in the assemblies, and completely abandoned public affairs. This gave the common people much cause for talk and concern; they feared that in his anger he might join the kings and overturn the state of things, leaving the city in a precarious position. 

When **Brutus**, having grown suspicious of certain others as well, desired to have the Senate take a solemn oath over the sacrificial victims, he called them to the **Forum** <note>original: "ἀγοράν" (agoran); the Greek term for the public marketplace and center of civic life.</note> on the appointed day. Valerius went there with a very cheerful countenance. He was the first to swear that he would never yield nor give way to **Tarquin**, but would fight with all his might for **liberty** <note>original: "ἐλευθερίας" (eleutherias).</note>. This act provided great pleasure to the Senate and gave courage to the magistrates. 

Soon after, his deeds confirmed his oath. For ambassadors arrived from Tarquin bringing letters designed to seduce the people, and speaking with fair words. They thought they could most easily corrupt the masses by speaking on behalf of a king who seemed to have lost his pride and appeared to be asking for only moderate terms. As they were considering whether these men should be brought before the people—

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the early Roman Republic. This page details his return to the political sphere and the subsequent diplomatic maneuvering by the exiled King Tarquinius.</meta>

...to act as **consul** <note>original: "ὑπάτ. πράξειν" (hypat. praxein). The abbreviated "ὑπάτ." refers to the consulship, the highest elected office in the Roman Republic.</note>. Valerius, however, did not allow this, but stood firm and prevented it, fearing that for men who were poor and burdened by the war against the tyranny, such things might provide the origins and excuses for a revolution. After this, other ambassadors arrived, stating that **Tarquinius** <note>Tarquinius Superbus, the seventh and final King of Rome, who was overthrown in 509 BCE.</note> would renounce his kingship and cease making war, but requested his money and possessions, as well as those of his friends and family, from which they might live while in exile.

While many were leaning toward this request—and especially **Collatinus** <note>Tarquinius Collatinus, the co-consul, who was himself a relative of the exiled king.</note> who spoke in favor of it—**Brutus** <note>Lucius Junius Brutus, the primary leader of the revolution against the monarchy.</note>, being a rigid and harsh man, rushed out into the **agora** <note>original: "ἀγοράν" (agoran). While Plutarch uses the Greek term for a marketplace, in a Roman context, this refers to the Forum.</note>, calling his colleague a traitor for granting the resources for war and tyranny to those to whom it was perhaps thought terrible to even vote for travel expenses for their exile.

When the citizens had assembled, the first private citizen to speak was the courageous **Gaius Minucius**. He encouraged Brutus and advised the Romans to see to it that the money should fight with them against the tyrants, rather than with the tyrants against them. Nevertheless, it seemed best to the Romans, who now held the freedom for which they had been fighting, not to sacrifice peace for the sake of money, but rather to cast out the wealth along with the tyrants.

In truth, the money was the least of Tarquin’s concerns; the demand was simultaneously a test of the people and a preparation for a betrayal. The ambassadors managed this while lingering under the pretext of the money, saying that they were selling some items, keeping others, and sending others away, until they had corrupted two of the houses considered **noble and good** <note>original: "καλῶν καὶ ἀγαθῶν" (kalon kai agathon). A standard Greek phrase referring to the aristocratic class.</note> in the city: one containing three senators, and the other two of **proconsular** <note>original: "ἀνθυπατικῶν" (anthypatikon); referring to those who had held the rank of consul.</note> rank. All of these were brothers on their mother's side... and were relatives of the consul Collatinus. Separately, the **Vitellii** <note>A noble Roman family; members of this family and the Aquillii were central to the conspiracy to restore the monarchy.</note> had another connection to Brutus; for his sister...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the transition from the Roman monarchy to the Republic. The catchword "πὸν γορεῖν" likely completes the verb "to speak in public" used to describe Minucius’s speech on the preceding page.</meta>

**Brutus** <note>Lucius Junius Brutus, the founder of the Republic.</note> had a wife, and several children by her. Two of these, who had reached manhood and were relatives and close companions of the **Aquillii** <note>The Aquillii and the Vitellii were noble families who conspired to bring the exiled King Tarquinius back to power.</note>, were drawn in by them. They persuaded the youths to join in the treason and to ally themselves with the faction of the **Tarquins** <note>The family of the deposed King Tarquinius Superbus.</note>. They offered them royal hopes and deliverance from their father's <term>insensibility</term> <note>original: "ἀσελγείας" (aselgeias). While often meaning "insolence" or "excess," here it refers to the feigned stupidity that Brutus adopted to survive the King's reign.</note> and harshness. For by "harshness," they meant his inexorable attitude toward the wicked. 

As for his "insensibility," he had used it as a pretense and a veil, it seems, for a long time for the sake of safety against the tyrants; he protected himself with nothing other than this <term>surname</term> <note>original: "ἐπωνυμίαν" (eponymian). The name "Brutus" in Latin literally means "dullard" or "stupid."</note>. When the young men were thus persuaded and entered into discussions with the Aquillii, it was decided that they should all swear a great and terrible oath, pouring a libation of blood from a slaughtered man and touching the entrails. For this purpose, they gathered at the house of the Aquillii. 

The room in which they intended to do these things was, as one might expect, deserted and dark. Therefore, a slave named **Vindicius** <note>original: "βίνδικος" (Vindikos). History remembers him as the man whose discovery of the plot led to the legal process of "Vindicta," or the formal freeing of a slave.</note> went unnoticed by them, having hidden himself inside. He was not there because of any plan or prior inkling of what was to happen; he simply happened to be inside, and when he saw them approach in great haste, he hid himself out of fear of being seen. He used a chest that was lying in front of him as cover, so that he was able to witness what was being done and overhear their plans. 

They decided to assassinate the **consuls** <note>original: "ὑπάτους" (hypatous). The two chief magistrates of the Republic; at this time, Brutus and Collatinus.</note>. Having written letters to Tarquinius stating these things, they gave them to the ambassadors. For these ambassadors had become guests of the Aquillii and were present at the meeting to help arrange the conspiracy. 

When they had finished these deeds and departed, Vindicius came out. He did not know how to handle what had befallen him in secret, but was in a state of perplexity. He thought it a terrible thing—as indeed it was—to denounce sons to their father Brutus, or to... <note>The sentence breaks off mid-word as the page ends.</note>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the conspiracy to restore King Tarquinius. On the previous page, a slave (Vindicius) had hidden in the room where the conspirators were swearing a blood oath and writing letters to the exiled king.</meta>

...the letter addressed to his nephew **Collatinus** <note>Lucius Tarquinius Collatinus, one of the first two consuls of Rome alongside Brutus.</note>. But privately, thinking that he had no Roman friend of sufficient standing to trust with such secrets, and having no hope that informing them would achieve anything, he was driven by his knowledge of the matter. He went first to **Valerius** <note>Publius Valerius Publicola, a prominent Roman aristocrat known for his support of the common people.</note>, being especially drawn by the man’s reputation for being public-spirited and humanitarian; for he was accessible to everyone, and especially to those in need. He always kept his house open, and never rejected the speech or simple request of the humble. 

When **Vindicius** <note>The slave who witnessed the conspiracy.</note> went up to him and told him everything, with only Valerius's brother Marcus and his wife present, Valerius was shocked and terrified. He did not let the man go, but instead locked him in a room and placed a guard on the courtyard, setting his wife to watch the doors. He ordered his brother to surround the royal residence, seize the <term>letters</term> <note>original: "γράμματα" (grammata). These were the written proofs of the conspiracy between the Roman nobles and the exiled king.</note> if he could, and keep watch over the servants. 

Valerius himself, accompanied by many worthy friends and a large retinue, walked to the house of the **Aquillii** <note>The conspirators mentioned on the previous page.</note> while they were not at home. Therefore, with no one expecting him, and having arranged everything as the law required, he found the letters lying in the place where the ambassadors were staying. While he was doing these things, the Aquillii came running up. Encountering him at the doors, they sought to snatch away the letters. But Valerius and his men fought them off. They threw their cloaks around the conspirators' necks and, through force and with much pushing and being pushed through the narrow streets, they dragged them into the <term>Forum</term> <note>original: "ἀγορὰν" (agoran). The central public space of Rome.</note>. At the same time, the same struggle occurred at the royal residence, where Marcus seized letters being carried in other vessels and dragged as many of the royal servants as he could into the Forum. When they had finally calmed the uproar...

<summary>The slave Vindicius reveals the conspiracy to Valerius, who takes swift action by seizing incriminating letters and arresting the conspirators at the Aquillii house and the royal residence.</summary>
<keywords>Valerius Publicola, Vindicius, Aquillii, conspiracy, Roman Republic, Tarquin letters, Roman Forum</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues from the discovery of the conspiracy to restore the Tarquin kings. The previous page ended with Valerius finding the incriminating letters in the house of the Aquillii. This page describes the trial and the famously stoic, yet horrific, execution of Brutus's sons by his own order.</meta>

-> + <-

When they had departed, and although he had commanded that they should not be spared, he went out from the house, not even in a legal manner, and when the formal accusation was made, the <term>letters</term> <note>original: "γράμματα" (grammata). These were the written proofs of the conspiracy between the Roman nobles and the exiled king.</note> were read aloud. The men did not even dare to speak against the evidence. The sight was noble, yet there was a heavy silence. A few, wishing to curry favor with **Brutus** <note>Lucius Junius Brutus, the first consul of Rome, who had led the revolution to overthrow the monarchy.</note>, suggested that exile should be the punishment.

Even **Collatinus** <note>Brutus’s co-consul, who was himself related to the exiled royal family and was more inclined toward mercy.</note>, moved to tears, gave in to them through his own natural kindness. And even **Aquillius** <note>One of the leaders of the conspiracy.</note> was not silent. But Brutus called each of his sons by name. "Titus," <note>original: "Ἄκυλλε" (Akylle). The scribe appears to have written "Aquillius" here, but the historical context and Plutarch’s standard text indicate Brutus is addressing his son Titus.</note> he said, "and you, why do you not defend yourselves against the accusation?" When they did not offer any answer, he turned his face toward the <term>assistants</term> <note>original: "ὑπηρέτας" (hyperetas). These were the lictors, the official bodyguards and executioners who carried the bundles of rods and axes (fasces) that symbolized Roman authority.</note> and said, "The rest of the task is now yours."

They immediately seized the young men and began to tear off their clothes. They bound their hands behind their backs and beat their bodies with rods until they were bloodied. While the other spectators were unable to look on or endure the sight, they say that Brutus neither turned his eyes away, nor allowed his expression of sternness and gravity to be softened by any pity. Instead, he looked with a terrible gaze upon his children as they were being punished, until the lictors stretched them out on the ground and cut off their heads with an axe. 

Having thus made them an example to sober the others, he rose and departed. He had performed a deed that is neither possible to praise adequately for those who wish to do so, nor easy to blame. For either the height of his <term>virtue</term> <note>original: "ἀρετῆς" (aretēs). Plutarch is weighing whether Brutus was a man of superhuman principle or someone who had become emotionally deadened.</note> drove his soul into a state of passionless detachment, or the magnitude of his suffering drove it into a state of numbness. Neither of these is a small thing, nor is it merely human; it is either divine or bestial. 

It is more just that our judgment follow the man's great reputation, rather than allow his virtue to be disbelieved because of the weakness of the person judging it. For the Romans do not consider the founding of the city by **Romulus** to be as great a feat as Brutus's establishment and stabilization of the Republic. So when he had departed...

<summary>Brutus presides over the trial of his own sons for treason, watching their public scourging and execution with a legendary, terrifying stoicism. Plutarch reflects on whether this act was one of supreme virtue or inhuman numbness.</summary>
<keywords>Brutus, Collatinus, execution, Titus, lictors, Roman Republic, stoicism, virtue vs. numbness, treason, capital punishment</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative following the execution of the sons of Brutus. Having witnessed the consul's terrifying impartiality in putting his own children to death, the assembly now turns to the remaining conspirators and the growing tension between the two consuls, Brutus and Collatinus.</meta>

-> + <-

Regarding **Horatius** <note>original: "Γορατίου" (Horatiou). While the scribe writes 'Horatius' here, the historical context in Plutarch's *Life of Publicola* suggests this passage follows the actions of Brutus and Valerius. Horatius Cocles appears later in the biography; the scribe may have misidentified the subject or be referencing a specific source tradition.</note>, for a long time astonishment and silence held everyone because of the deeds that had been done. But in response to the **softness** <note>original: "μαλακίαν" (malakian). This refers to Collatinus's perceived lack of resolve or "weakness" in punishing the conspirators compared to Brutus’s iron-fisted justice.</note> and delay of **Collatinus**, the Aquillii <note>The Aquillii were the nephews of Collatinus and key conspirators in the plot to return the Tarquin kings to power.</note> took courage. For a time, they demanded the right to offer a defense, and that the slave <note>This is Vindicius, the slave who overheard the conspiracy and reported it to Valerius.</note> be given up to them as their property and not remain in the hands of the accusers. Collatinus was willing to grant these requests and was even dismissing the assembly on these terms.

However, **Valerius** <note>Publius Valerius, later known as Publicola (Friend of the People), who was instrumental in the revolution.</note> was neither able to let the man go, as he was surrounded by the crowd, nor would he allow the people to let the traitors depart. Finally, laying hands on their bodies, he called out to **Brutus**. He shouted that **Collatinus** was acting outrageously, if, while imposing the necessity of <term>child-killing</term> <note>original: "παιδοφονίας" (paidophonias). Brutus had just presided over the execution of his own sons.</note> upon his fellow consul, he himself thought he could grant favors to the wives, the traitors, and the enemies of the fatherland. The consul being indignant and ordering **Vindicius** <note>The slave-informant whose freedom was later granted as a reward.</note> to be led away, the <term>assistants</term> <note>The lictors, officials who carried out the consuls' orders and corporal punishments.</note> pushed through the crowd and seized the man, striking those who tried to take him away.

But the friends of Valerius stood in front to defend him, and the people cried out, demanding that **Brutus** be present. He therefore returned, and when silence was once again established, he said: "As for my sons, I myself was a sufficient judge; but concerning the rest of the citizens, who are free, the decision belongs to the <term>vote</term>." <note>original: "ψῆφον" (psephon). Brutus here makes a landmark distinction between his patriarchal authority over his family and the legal rights of Roman citizens.</note> He wished to speak and persuade the people, and no longer was chance required; but when the vote was taken, all were condemned together.

**Collatinus**, however, was held in some suspicion, as it seems, because of his kinship with the kings, and he was hated. The people also detested his second name, **Tarquinius**. <note>Collatinus’s full name was Lucius Tarquinius Collatinus. After the expulsion of the kings, the very name 'Tarquin' became a symbol of tyranny that the Roman populace could not tolerate, regardless of the man's character.</note> When these things happened, having completely clashed with public sentiment, he voluntarily resigned his office and left the city.

<summary>This page details the conflict between the consuls Valerius and Collatinus over the fate of the remaining conspirators and the slave Vindicius. It concludes with Brutus deferring to the people's vote and Collatinus resigning due to his royal connections and hated name.</summary>
<keywords>Brutus, Collatinus, Valerius, Publicola, Vindicius, Aquillii, conspiracy, Roman Republic, lictors, Tarquinius</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page follows the departure of the consul Collatinus, who resigned under pressure from the Roman people and his colleague Brutus. The narrative now focuses on the consolidation of the new Republic and the fate of the exiled King Tarquinius's property.</meta>

-> + <-

...so he departed. Thus, when elections were held again, **Valerius** was brilliantly appointed consul. Believing that **Vindicius** <note>original: "ὐινδίκιον" (Vindicion). This was the slave who had exposed the conspiracy of the Aquillii to restore the monarchy.</note> should enjoy some reward for his zeal, the people first voted that he should become a freedman and a citizen of the Romans, and that he should cast his vote in whichever tribe he wished to be assigned. To the other freedmen, it was much later and after a long time that **Appius** <note>Appius Claudius Sabinus Regillensis, a later Roman official known for his legal reforms.</note> gave the right of a public vote. Even to this day, the perfect form of manumission <note>The formal process of freeing a slave.</note> is called a **vindicta** <note>original: "οὐινδίκτα". Plutarch suggests the legal term for freeing a slave by a rod (vindicta) was named after the slave Vindicius.</note>, as they say, because of that man Vindicius.

After these events, the Romans plundered the property of the kings and gave it to the people. They leveled the house and the villa to the ground. The **Field of Mars** <note>original: "ἄρειον πεδίον" (Areion pedion). This large public area in Rome was used for military exercises and assemblies.</note>, which **Tarquinius** had possessed, was consecrated to the god. It happened to have been harvested already, and since heaps of grain were still lying there, they did not think it right to use it because of the consecration. Instead, running together, they carried the wagons of grain into the river. Likewise, they cut down the trees and threw them in, leaving the place entirely fallow and fruitless for the god.

As many things were pushed upon one another and crowded together in the current, the mass did not drift far from the clear water but, having been brought together, it struck against solid ground and stayed there. Since the debris being carried down had no exit but became caught and entangled, the accumulation began to take root and grow, strengthened by the force of the current. For the alluvial flow brought much silt which settled there, providing both increase and a binding quality. The blows of the water did not cause the mass to shake, but pressing gently, drove everything into the same spot and molded it together. Because of its size and stability, it gained the strength to withstand the majority of the things carried down by the river. This is now the **Sacred Island** <note>original: "νῆσος... ἱερά". Known today as the Tiber Island (Isola Tiberina), located in a bend of the Tiber river in Rome.</note> in front of the city; it contains temples of the gods.

<summary>This page describes the reward given to the slave Vindicius for exposing a royalist plot and details the destruction of King Tarquinius's property, which led to the legendary formation of the Tiber Island.</summary>
<keywords>Valerius Publicola, Vindicius, manumission, vindicta, Campus Martius, Tarquinius, Tiber Island, Roman topography, religious consecration</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the Roman transition from monarchy to republic. Having described the disposal of King Tarquinius’s property into the Tiber, the text now explains the formation of the Tiber Island and the subsequent military conflict between the new Republic and the exiled king's allies.</meta>

->+<-

And concerning the stakes <note>original: "πάλους" (palous). These were wooden piles or stakes driven into the riverbed which trapped the debris thrown into the Tiber, eventually forming the Tiber Island.</note>. In the language of the Latins, it is called "Between Two Bridges" <note>original: "μέση δυοῖν γεφυρῶν". In Latin: *Inter duos pontes*. This is the *Insula Tiberina* (Tiber Island) in Rome.</note>. Some, however, do not wish this to be the case, but say that the field was consecrated when **Tarquinius** fell <note>The author refers to the fall of the king's power, not his death.</note>, but rather at a later time, when **Tarquinia**, a maiden, dedicated another field bordering that one. This **Tarquinia** was a virgin priestess, one of the **Vestals** <note>The Vestal Virgins were priestesses of Vesta, goddess of the hearth, who maintained the sacred fire of Rome.</note>. She received great honors for this act, among which was the right to give testimony alone among women <note>In Roman law, women were generally not allowed to serve as legal witnesses; this was a rare and prestigious exemption.</note>. The right to be a stewardess <note>original: "ταμείᾳ" (tameia). Most standard versions of Plutarch's text read "to be married" (γαμεῖσθαι), but the manuscript here suggests a role in financial or household administration.</note>, which was granted to her alone, she did not accept. These things the storytellers of the ancient tongues recount.

But when **Tarquinius** despaired of regaining his rule through gardens <note>original: "ἀπὸ Γοντίας... ἐκ κήπων". This likely refers to the "Gardens of Tarquinius" or the "Garden Conspiracy" mentioned in the previous page. Plutarch means that after the plot failed, Tarquin turned to open war.</note> and the recovery of his power through deception, the **Tyrrhenians** <note>The Etruscans, a powerful civilization in central Italy.</note> received him eagerly and led him back with a great force. The **consuls** <note>The two primary elected leaders of the Roman Republic.</note> stood against them on behalf of the **Romans**, and they drew up their ranks in sacred places, one of which they call the **Arsian Grove** <note>original: "οὐρσον ἄλσος" (ourson alsos). A forest on the border of Roman and Etruscan territory.</note>, and the other the **Aesian <gloss>ou</gloss> Meadow** <note>The gloss "ou" corrects the ending of "Aesiou" (Aesian). This was a plain near the grove.</note>. As they began to engage hand-to-hand, **Arruns**, the son of **Tarquinius**, and **Brutus**, the consul of the **Romans**, did not fall upon one another by chance, but out of hatred and rage. One charged as if against a tyrant and enemy of his fatherland, and the other seeking revenge for his exile; they rushed at one another with their horses. Joining battle with passion rather than reason, they were reckless with their lives and died together at each other's hands.

Although this opening struggle was terrible, the rest of the battle did not have a more moderate end. Instead, having done and suffered equal damage, the armies were separated by a storm. **Valerius** <note>Publius Valerius Publicola, the surviving consul.</note> was therefore in a state of helplessness, not knowing the outcome of the fight, for he saw his soldiers discouraged by their own dead, but encouraged by the dead of the enemy. So undecided and equal was the slaughter because of the great numbers. It happened to both sides that seeing only their own losses confirmed their defeat, while they could only guess at the losses of the enemy. When night came <gloss>on</gloss> and things had happened <gloss>non</gloss> <note>These interlinear glosses "on" and "non" are likely grammatical corrections for the Greek genitive absolute construction "γενομένων".</note>

---

$\cdot/\cdot$ as if they had fought so badly.

<summary>This page describes the legendary origins of the Tiber Island and the bloody Battle of Silva Arsia, where the consul Brutus and the prince Arruns killed each other in a mutual charge.</summary>
<keywords>Tiber Island, Tarquinia, Vestal Virgins, Tyrrhenians, Etruscans, Battle of Silva Arsia, Arruns, Brutus, Valerius Publicola, Roman Republic</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues Plutarch’s account of the Roman Republic's early days. Following the conflict over King Tarquinius’s property and the formation of the Tiber Island, the narrative now details a miraculous event during the war between the Romans and the Etruscan allies of the exiled king, leading to the first Roman triumph and the origin of funeral orations.</meta>

->+<-

In the silence of the camp, they say a voice issued from the sacred grove <note>original: "ἄλσους" (alsous). This refers to the Arsian Grove, where the battle took place. Tradition identifies the voice as belonging to the forest god Silvanus.</note>, a great voice that broke out declaring that the **Tyrrhenians** <note>The Tyrrhenians are the Etruscans, a powerful civilization in central Italy who were allies of the exiled King Tarquin.</note> had lost one more man in the battle than the Romans. The Romans were unaffected by this greater loss. This was a sign of divine inspiration. Immediately, because of this voice, the one side [the Romans] were inspired to raise a great and courageous battle-cry. But the **Tyrrhenians**, becoming terrified and thrown into confusion, fled from their camp, and most were scattered.

The Romans then fell upon those who remained, who were fewer than five thousand, and they captured all the tents. When the dead were counted, those of the enemy were found to be eleven thousand three hundred; the Romans were exactly one fewer <note>original: "παρ’ ἕνα" (par' ena). This miraculous "one-man difference" confirmed the divine voice's prophecy and the Roman victory.</note>. They say this battle occurred one day before the **Kalends** of March <note>The Kalends were the first day of the month; this date corresponds to February 28th or 29th.</note>. From this victory, **Valerius** celebrated a triumph <note>term: <term>Triumph</term> (thriambos) – A spectacular civil ceremony and religious rite in ancient Rome, held to publicly celebrate and sanctify the success of a military commander.</note>, riding out in a four-horse chariot—the first of the **consuls** <note>term: <term>Consul</term> (hypatos) – One of the two annually elected chief magistrates who jointly ruled the Roman Republic.</note> to do so.

The event provided a solemn and magnificent sight, and did not (as some say) cause envy or resentment in those watching. For if it had, the custom would not have been maintained for so long, nor pursued with such ambition, through so many years when such things would have been impossible to sustain. The people also welcomed the honors **Valerius** paid to his fallen colleague <note>This refers to Lucius Junius Brutus, who had died in the battle.</note>, with which he adorned the funeral. He delivered a **funeral oration** <note>term: <term>Funeral Oration</term> (epitaphios) – A formal speech delivered in praise of the deceased.</note> for him. This custom was so loved by the Romans and brought such grace that, from that time on, it became the practice for all good and great men to be praised by the most eminent citizens at their deaths.

It is said that this custom is even older than the Greek funeral orations, unless that too is an invention of **Solon** <note>Solon was a famous Athenian lawmaker and poet (c. 630–560 BCE).</note>, as the rhetorician **Anaximenes** has recorded. However, the people were only annoyed and offended by **Valerius** regarding one matter: that **Brutus**, whom the people considered the father of their liberty, did not think it right to rule alone, but first chose one colleague for himself and then a second. But this man [Valerius], they said, having gathered...

<summary>This page describes a divine voice confirming a Roman victory over the Etruscans, Valerius's inaugural triumph in a chariot, and the origin of the Roman custom of funeral orations for great men.</summary>
<keywords>Valerius Publicola, Brutus, Etruscans, Battle of Silva Arsia, Roman Triumph, funeral orations, Solon, Anaximenes, Kalends of March</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues Plutarch's *Life of Publicola*. The previous page described Valerius Publicola's victory over the Etruscans and the miraculous divine voice that confirmed the Roman win. Now, the narrative turns to Publicola's leadership in Rome and his response to the people's fears that he might become a tyrant.</meta>

->+<-

...everything was focused on him, as there was no longer any part of Brutus's legacy <note>Brutus, the co-founder of the Republic, had recently died in battle, leaving Valerius as the sole consul.</note> remaining to him, but only that of **Tarquin’s** tyranny. And yet, he was blamed—even while being praised alongside Brutus—for imitating Tarquin. He lived under the constant presence of all the rods and axes <note>term: <term>Fasces</term> – A bundle of wooden rods (rhabdoi) often including an axe (pelekis), symbolizing a magistrate's power to punish and execute.</note>, inhabiting a house so great in size that it surpassed even the king's house which he had helped destroy. For in truth, he lived in a rather grand and "tragic" <note>original: "τραγικώτερον" (tragikōteron). In this context, it suggests a house that was overly theatrical or pretentious, like a stage set for a king.</note> manner 
<margin>He lived in an unsuitable way</margin> on the hill called the **Velia** <note>original: "Οὐϊελλίαν" (Ouiellian). A ridge in ancient Rome near the Forum.</note>, his house hanging over the **Forum** and looking down upon all things from its height. It was difficult to approach and steep from the outside. So, when he descended from it, his appearance was lofty, and the grandeur of his procession was like a royal parade. 

But he showed that for one in power and in great affairs, it is a great good to possess frankness <note>original: "παρρησίαν" (parrhēsian). The Greek ideal of speaking truth to power or speaking freely without fear.</note> joined with truth, rather than flattery. For when he heard from his friends—who recounted what the common people felt were his faults—he did not become argumentative or resentful. Instead, quickly gathering many laborers while it was still night, he tore the house down and razed it entirely to the ground. 

As a result, when the Romans saw this at daybreak and gathered together, they admired and marveled at the man's magnanimity <note>original: "μεγαλοφροσύνην" (megalophrosynēn). The virtue of being "great-souled," or rising above petty concerns for the public good.</note>. However, they were also grieved for the house, missing its size and beauty, as if it were a person unjustly driven away by envy. For Valerius was now like a homeless man, living in the houses of others. Eventually, the people received him—not for his own benefit, but into a place the people gave to him—and they constructed a house more modest than the first. This is where the temple called **Vica Pota** <note>original: "Οὐϊκαππότας" (Ouikappotas). A Roman goddess associated with victory and "conquering and drinking in."</note> stands today. 

Wishing to make not only himself but also his office approachable and gentle instead of terrifying to the common people, he removed the axes from the rods. And these very rods, whenever he entered the assembly, he lowered and bowed to the people, shifting the state toward the form of a **democracy** <note>original: "δημοκρατίας" (dēmokratias). While Rome was technically a Republic, Plutarch uses the Greek term for "rule by the people" to describe these populist concessions.</note>.

<summary>Valerius Publicola faces criticism for his regal lifestyle and grand house on the Velia. To prove his commitment to the Republic, he tears down his own house in the middle of the night and introduces reforms to make the consulship less intimidating, such as removing axes from the fasces.</summary>
<keywords>Publicola, Velia, Tarquin, Vica Pota, Fasces, democracy, Rome, magnanimity</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Valerius Publicola's transition from a perceived tyrant back to a beloved leader of the Roman people. Following the previous page's description of his imposing house on the Velia, the text now details his specific political actions and the laws he enacted to secure the Republic's future.</meta>

-> + <-

And while these matters were being handled in this way, the leaders were acting. They appeared to the many not as caretakers of their own interests, but quite the opposite; Valerius shifted the envy directed at him by this demolition and curtailment of his own house. By adding to himself as much greatness of power as he seemed to take away from his authority, he winningly brought the people over to his side. They bore him willingly, so much so that they proclaimed him **Publicola**. This name signifies "one who cares for the people" <note>original: "δημοκηδῆ" (demokēdē). From *demos* (people) and *kedos* (care or concern). This title became the name by which Valerius is most famous in history.</note>. This became the most enduring of his ancient names, which we shall also use in recounting the rest of this man’s life.

For he gave power to those who wished it to seek office and provide for the public good. Before the establishment of the **tribunes** <note>term: <term>Tribunes</term> – Officials (tribuni plebis) elected to protect the rights and interests of the common people against the actions of the aristocracy.</note>, not knowing anything of rank, but rather believing he would be acting out of envy or ignorance if he did not, he divided the powers of the monarchy toward the finest forms of government.

First, he filled up the **Senate** <note>original: "βουλήν" (boulēn). The Roman council of elders and advisors.</note> which had become sparsely populated. For some had been put to death by **Tarquin** previously, and others had died in hand-to-hand combat in the battle. They say that those enrolled by him reached the number of one hundred and sixty-four. 

After these things, he wrote laws. Among these, he made those for the common people especially strong. One law benefited the people by allowing them to appeal a judgment from the **consuls** <note>term: <term>Consuls</term> – The two highest-ranking elected political officials in the Roman Republic.</note> to the judgment of the people. A second law forbade anyone from taking up an office without the people's consent, ordering the penalty of death for anyone who did so. 

A third law after these was one that helped the poor; he removed the taxes <note>original: "τέλη" (telē). Duties, tolls, or taxes paid to the state.</note> from the many, which made everyone apply themselves to their work more eagerly. The law written against those who were disobedient toward the consuls seemed no less popular, and even more so to the many. As it is written, he set the penalty for disobedience at the value of five oxen and two sheep. The price of a sheep was ten obols <note>original: "ὀβολοὶ" (oboloi). A unit of Greek currency. Plutarch uses Greek monetary terms to help his readers understand the ancient Roman values.</note>, and that of an ox was a hundred, as they were not yet using much coined money at that time...

<summary>Valerius earns the title 'Publicola' by dismantling his grand house and enacting laws that protect the common people, including the right to appeal and the removal of taxes.</summary>
<keywords>Publicola, Valerius, Rome, Senate, Tarquin, Consuls, taxation, Roman Law, Tribunes</keywords>

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues Plutarch's account of Publicola's reforms. Following his name change and the expansion of the Senate mentioned on the previous page, the text now details his economic legislation, his laws against tyranny, the establishment of the public treasury, and his respect for civic seniority.</meta>

-> + <-

...Romans; but they thrived on sheep and cattle breeding. For this reason, even to this day, they call their wealth *pecunia* <note>original: "πεκούνια" (pekounia). Derived from the Latin word *pecus*, meaning cattle or livestock, reflecting a time when wealth was measured by one's herd.</note> from their sheep. On their oldest coins, they used to stamp the image of an ox, a sheep, or a pig. They even gave their children names such as **Suillii**, **Bubulci**, **Caprarii**, and **Porcii** <note>These Roman family names are derived from animals: *Suillius* from *sus* (pig), *Bubulcus* from *bos* (ox), *Caprarius* from *capra* (goat), and *Porcius* from *porcus* (pig).</note>; for they called goats *caprae* and pigs *porci*.

Having become such a popular and moderate lawgiver in these matters, he mitigated the severity of punishments in more significant affairs. For he wrote a law which gave permission to kill, without a formal trial, anyone who attempted to set himself up as a tyrant. He declared the killer free from the guilt of murder <note>original: "Φόνου καθαρὸν" (phonou katharon) - ritually and legally clean of blood-guilt.</note>, provided they could produce evidence of the crime. For since it was impossible for someone attempting such a great undertaking to escape the notice of everyone, yet not impossible for him to grow too powerful to be judged after the fact, Publicola granted the power to anticipate the offender to anyone capable of doing so. 

He was also praised for his law regarding the treasury. When it became necessary for the citizens to contribute funds from their estates for the war, he would not handle the money himself, nor would he allow his friends to manage it, nor did he permit any public funds to enter a private home at all. Instead, he designated the **Temple of Saturn** <note>The *Aerarium Saturni*, located at the foot of the Capitoline Hill, which served as the primary treasury of the Roman Republic.</note> as the treasury, a purpose for which they continue to use it to this day. 

He allowed the people to appoint two of the younger men as treasurers <note>term: <term>Quaestors</term> – (from *quaero*, 'to seek' or 'inquire') These were junior officials responsible for overseeing the public treasury and financial records.</note>. The first ones to be appointed were Publius Veturius and Marcus Minucius. A great deal of money was collected; for one hundred thousand people were recorded in the census, excluding the sums contributed by orphans and widows. 

After arranging these matters, he appointed Lucretius, the father of the deceased Lucretia, as his colleague in office. To him, Publicola voluntarily yielded the senior authority, and he handed over the rods known as the **fasces** <note>term: <term>Fasces</term> – A bundle of wooden rods, sometimes including an axe, carried by attendants (lictors) as a symbol of a magistrate's power and jurisdiction.</note>. This mark of honor to seniority has remained with us to this day; from that time on, the privilege was granted to the elders...

<summary>Publicola establishes laws defining Roman wealth through livestock, creates a legal basis for killing aspiring tyrants, and founds the public treasury in the Temple of Saturn. He also demonstrates civic humility by yielding the symbols of power (fasces) to his elder colleague, Lucretius.</summary>
<keywords>Pecunia, Roman coinage, Tyrannicide law, Temple of Saturn, Aerarium, Quaestors, Publius Veturius, Marcus Minucius, Lucretius, Fasces, Roman census</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues Plutarch's account of the Roman Republic's early days. The previous page concluded Publicola's legal reforms and the establishment of the treasury. Here, the narrative shifts to the shared consulship and a supernatural omen involving the Temple of Jupiter.</meta>

...guarded. But since Sulpicius died a few days later, and elections were held again, Marcus Horatius was chosen; he filled out the remaining time of the year with Publicola. While Tarquin was stirring up the second war in Etruria <note>original: "Τυρρηνίᾳ" (Tyrrhenia). The Greeks called the Etruscans "Tyrrhenians."</note> against the Romans, a great omen is said to have occurred <gloss>to take place</gloss>. 

While still reigning, Tarquin—having not yet finished the temple of Capitoline Jupiter—either because a prophecy had been given, or because it seemed right to him, ordered a terracotta chariot to be placed on the roof. This work was commissioned to certain Etruscan craftsmen from the city of Veii. Then, a little later, he fell from power. 

The Etruscans, having placed the clay four-horse chariot into the kiln, it did not undergo what is natural for clay to undergo in the fire—to be condensed and shrink as the moisture leaves. Instead, it expanded and swelled, and grew to such a size, strength, and hardness that it could hardly be removed until the roof of the kiln was uncovered and the walls were taken down. Since it seemed to the seers <note>original: "μάντεσι" (mantesi) - diviners or soothsayers who interpreted signs from the gods.</note> to be a divine sign of good fortune and power for those who possessed the four-horse chariot, the people of Veii decided not to surrender it to the Romans when they asked for it. 

They replied that this belonged to Tarquin, and not to those who had driven Tarquin out. A few days later, they held horse races. While the rest of the event provided the usual spectacle and excitement, as the winning four-horse chariot <note>original: "τέθριππον" (tethrippon) - a chariot drawn by four horses abreast, often used in racing and as a symbol of triumph.</note> and its charioteer were driving out of the racecourse nearly crowned with the victory wreath, the horses were spooked for no apparent reason. Whether by some divine power or by chance, they ran wildly toward the city of the Romans, carrying the charioteer with them. Since it was impossible to pull them back or soothe them, the man surrendered to the momentum and was carried along until they reached the Capitol, where they threw him out near the gate.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the miraculous terracotta chariot from Veii and transitions into the political struggle over the dedication of the Temple of Jupiter Capitolinus. It follows the account of Publicola’s consulship and his rivalry with Marcus Horatius Pulvillus.</meta>

...the reins of which they pull, as they call them. When this happened, the Etruscans <note>original: "Τυρρηνοὶ" (Tyrrhenians). The Greeks used this term for the Etruscan civilization.</note>, being amazed and frightened, allowed the chariot to be returned to the Roman craftsmen. Regarding the temple of Jupiter Capitolinus, Tarquin the son of Demaratus <note>This refers to Tarquinius Priscus, the fifth King of Rome, who originally came from Greece.</note> had vowed to build it while at war with the Sabines. It was actually built, however, by Tarquin the Proud <note>original: "Σούπερβος" (Superbus), the seventh and final King of Rome.</note>, who was either the son or grandson of the one who made the vow. He did not manage to consecrate it, for he was just a little short of finishing the work when he was driven out of power.

When the work was finally completed and had received its proper decoration, Publius Publicola felt a great ambition to perform the consecration <note>original: "καθιέρωσιν" (kathierōsin) — the formal religious ceremony that officially dedicated a temple to a god.</note>. However, many of the powerful men in Rome envied his fame. They were less bothered by the other honors he had earned as a lawmaker and general, but since this was a sacred rite <note>original: "ἁγιστείαν" (hagisteian)</note>, they did not believe such an honor should be added to his list of achievements. 

They began to encourage and incite Horatius to lay claim to the right of consecration for himself. Therefore, when a necessary military campaign arose that required Publicola’s presence, the opposition passed a decree that Horatius should perform the dedication. They led him up to the Capitol, believing they could accomplish this while Publicola was absent. Some say, however, that the two men cast lots, and it happened that Publicola was chosen to lead the army against his will, while Horatius was chosen for the consecration.

Knowing what was to occur during the full moon of the month of Metageitnion <note>An Athenian month roughly corresponding to late August or early September; Plutarch uses Greek calendar terms for his readers.</note>, Horatius gathered the entire crowd at the Capitol. When silence fell, Marcus <note>Marcus Horatius Pulvillus, the consul.</note> performed the various rites and, as is the custom, touched the temple doors while pronouncing the traditional words for the consecration. 

But Marcus Valerius, the brother of Publicola, who had been standing by the doors for a long time watching for the right moment, cried out: "O Consul, your son has died of illness in the camp!" This news grieved all those who heard it. But Horatius, not at all disturbed by the news, said only this: "Cast the corpse wherever you wish, for I do not accept the mourning," and he proceeded to complete the rest of the consecration ceremony.

But what was added was not true; rather, Marcus <note>original: "ὁ μᾶρκος" (Marcus). It seems that a similar fate regarding the consecration befell the second temple as well. 

For the first temple, which as has been said Tarquin <note>Tarquinius Superbus, the last King of Rome.</note> built and Horatius consecrated, was destroyed by fire during the civil wars. The second one was raised up by Sulla <note>Lucius Cornelius Sulla (c. 138–78 BCE), the Roman general and dictator.</note>, but the name of Catulus <note>Quintus Lutatius Catulus, a Roman statesman who completed the work after Sulla's death.</note> was inscribed upon the consecration, as Sulla died beforehand. When this temple was again destroyed during the riots under Vitellius <note>The civil wars of 69 CE, known as the Year of the Four Emperors.</note>, Vespasian enjoyed good fortune at the beginning of his rule <term>εὐποτμίαν (eupotmian): a state of being favored by a good spirit or luck</term>. He rebuilt it from the foundations to the end and lived to see it completed, but he did not see it destroyed, as it was shortly after. In this, he surpassed Sulla in fortune; for the former died before the consecration of the work, while the latter died before its destruction. For as soon as Vespasian died, the Capitol was consumed by fire. 

This fourth temple was completed and consecrated by Domitian. It is said that Tarquin spent forty thousand pounds <note>original: "λίτρας" (litras). A Roman pound (libra) was approximately 328 grams.</note> of silver on the foundations alone. But for this current temple in our own time, the cost of the gilding alone—even if one calculated it for the wealthiest private citizen in Rome—could not be justified, as it amounted to more than twelve thousand talents <note>A talent was a massive unit of currency and weight; 12,000 talents would be an astronomical sum, equivalent to millions of silver coins.</note>. 

The columns were cut from Pentelic stone <note>Fine-grained white marble from Mount Pentelicus in Attica, Greece, prized for its purity.</note>, having a beautiful proportion of thickness to length. I saw them myself in Athens. But when they were recut and polished again in Rome, they did not gain as much in smoothness as they lost in symmetry, appearing too thin, hollow, and lean. 

Therefore, if anyone who wondered at the luxury of the Capitol were to see even a single hall in Domitian’s palace, or a royal portico, or a bathhouse, or a suite for his concubines, they would say what Epicharmus <note>A Greek comic playwright and philosopher.</note> said to the profligate...

<meta>This page transitions from the description of Domitian’s reconstruction of the fourth Temple of Jupiter Capitolinus to the historical narrative of the war between the fledgling Roman Republic and Lars Porsena, who sought to restore the exiled King Tarquin.</meta>
