“You are not a lover of humanity. You have a disease: you delight in building.” One might have dared to say such a thing to Domitian <note>Emperor Domitian (reigned 81–96 CE) spent immense sums on the fourth reconstruction of the Temple of Jupiter. Plutarch is critiquing his obsession with monumental architecture.</note>. “You are not pious, nor do you love beauty; you have a disease: you delight in building,” just like the famous Midas, who wished for everything to turn into gold and stone <note>A reference to the mythological King Midas, whose "golden touch" became a curse; Plutarch suggests Domitian’s building projects were a similarly destructive obsession.</note>. So much, then, for these matters.

Tarquin <note>Tarquinius Superbus, the seventh and last King of Rome, who had been expelled.</note>, after the great battle in which he lost his son in a single combat against Brutus <note>Lucius Junius Brutus, one of the first consuls of Rome.</note>, fled for refuge to Clusium. He entreated Lars Porsena, a man who held the greatest power among the Italian kings and had a reputation for being both noble and ambitious. Porsena promised to help him. First, he sent a message to Rome, ordering the citizens to receive Tarquin back. When the Romans did not obey, he declared war against them, specifying the time and the place where he intended to invade, and arrived with a large force. 

Publicola was chosen consul for the second time in his absence, with Titus Lucretius as his colleague. Upon returning to Rome, and wishing to surpass Porsena in high-mindedness, he founded the city of Siguria <note>original: "Σιγουρίαν" (Sigourian). This refers to the Latin colony of Signia.</note> while Porsena was <term>καὶ</term> <note>original: "καὶ" (and). The manuscript here emphasizes the proximity or the boldness of the act despite Porsena's presence.</note> already nearby. After fortifying it at great expense, he sent seven hundred colonists there, acting as if he were bearing the war easily and without fear. 

But when a strong assault occurred and the wall was suddenly captured, the guards were driven out by Porsena. As they fled, they fell back into the city of Rome in a confused mass, with the enemy close behind them. Publicola rushed out to help in front of the gates and, having joined battle by the river, he held his ground, though he was hard-pressed by the sheer numbers of the enemy. This continued until, having received severe wounds, he was carried off from the battle on a litter. When his colleague Lucretius suffered the same fate, despair fell upon the Romans, and a flight toward the city brought on terror. The enemy were already pushing across the wooden bridge <note>The Pons Sublicius, the oldest bridge in Rome, made entirely of wood to allow for quick dismantling in times of war.</note>...

[The city] ran no small risk of being captured and overcome. But first, Horatius Cocles—having with him two of the most distinguished men, Herminius and Lucretius—took his stand at the wooden bridge <note>The Pons Sublicius, the oldest bridge in Rome, made entirely of wood so it could be quickly dismantled in an emergency.</note>. This Horatius had received the surname "Cocles" from the loss of <insert>one</insert> of his eyes in war. However, as some say, it was because of the flatness of his nose, which was so sunken that there was nothing to divide the eyes, and his eyebrows were <unclear>blended</unclear> together. Because the common people wished to call him "Cyclops," but through a slip of the tongue, the name "Cocles" <note>original: "κόκλιον" (koklion). Plutarch is explaining how the Latin word for "one-eyed" (Cocles) was supposedly a corruption of the Greek "Cyclops."</note> prevailed and took hold. 

This man, standing in front of the bridge, fought the enemy until his companions behind him had cut the bridge down. Then, throwing himself into the river with his armor, he swam across and reached the opposite bank, even though he had been struck in the hip by a Tuscan <note>original: "τυρρηνικῶι" (tyrrhēnikōi). Furthermore, he gave him as much of the public land as he could plow in a single day. In addition to these honors, they set up a bronze statue of him in the Temple of Hephaestus <note>The Vulcanal, an open-air sanctuary to Vulcan (Hephaestus) near the Roman Forum.</note>, consoling the man for the lameness that resulted from his wound with great honor. 

While Porsena was pressing the siege against the city, a famine also fell upon the Romans. Additionally, a second army of Tuscans invaded the territory on their own. But Publicola, while keeping watch and guarding the city, thought it necessary to hold out against Porsena. However, when he went out against the [other] Tuscans, he engaged and routed them, killing five thousand of them. 

Regarding the story of Mucius <note>Mucius Scaevola, famous for thrusting his hand into a fire to prove Roman resolve.</note>, which has been told by many and in different ways, the version most widely believed must be related. This was a man good in every virtue, and in matters of war, the best. Plotting to kill the...

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Roman war against Lars Porsena. Having previously described the defense of the bridge by Horatius Cocles, the text now transitions to the legendary exploit of Gaius Mucius, who would earn the name Scaevola.</meta>

->+<-

[Mucius, seeking to assassinate] Porsena, entered into the enemy camp, wearing **Etruscan** <note>original: "Τυρρηνίδα" (Tyrrhēnida). The Greeks called the Etruscans "Tyrrhenians."</note> dress and using a similar dialect. After circling the platform where the king was sitting—not knowing him clearly by sight, and fearing to ask who he was—he drew his sword and killed the man he thought most likely to be the king among those seated there. Being seized for this act, he was interrogated. While a brazier containing fire was standing nearby, prepared for Porsena to perform a sacrifice, Mucius held his **right hand** over the burning flesh. He stood there looking at Porsena with a cheerful and unmoving face, until the king, filled with wonder, released him. Porsena then returned the sword, reaching it out from the platform. Mucius stretched out his **left hand** to receive it. Because of this, they say he received the surname **Scaevola**, which signifies "Left-handed" <note>The Latin name "Scaevola" is derived from *scaevus*, meaning left-handed.</note>.

Having conquered Porsena’s fear—or rather, having conquered the king's virtue by his own virtue—he said he would grant him a favor which he would not have spoken under compulsion. He spoke with a warning: "For three hundred Romans, possessing the same resolve as I, are wandering through your camp, watching for their opportunity. I, having been chosen by lot and making the first attempt, am not burdened by my fortune in failing to kill a good man; I would rather you became a friend to the Romans, if ever it should be more fitting for them to have such." Porsena, hearing this, believed him. He yielded toward a settlement, not so much—it seems to me—out of fear of the three hundred, but rather because he loved and admired the spirit and the **virtue** of the Romans.

This man is called both Mucius and Scaevola by all. Athenodorus <note>Athenodorus Cananites (c. 74 BC – 7 AD), a Stoic philosopher and teacher of Augustus.</note>, the son of Sandon, in his work addressed to Octavia, the sister of Caesar, says he was also called *Postumus*. However, **Publicola** <note>Valerius Publicola, the Roman Consul.</note> himself did not consider Porsena so much a heavy enemy as a man worthy of becoming a great friend and ally to the city. He did not shrink from having Porsena act as a judge in the legal dispute against **Tarquin** <note>Tarquinius Superbus, the exiled last king of Rome, who was attempting to regain his throne with Porsena's help.</note>; instead, he was confident and frequently challenged him as...

<summary>Mucius Scaevola attempts to assassinate King Porsena but kills a secretary instead; he proves his Roman resolve by burning his own hand, leading to a peace negotiation. Publicola begins to treat Porsena as a potential ally and arbitrator rather than a mere enemy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Valerius Publicola, specifically the peace negotiations with the Etruscan King Lars Porsena. [Porsena] chose the man <note>referring to Publicola</note>, and judged that [Tarquin] had been **justly** deprived of his rule. But when Tarquin replied rather harshly that he would accept no judge—and least of all Porsena, if he were no longer an ally—Porsena became annoyed. Having judged the situation well, and since his son Aruns was also entreating him and acting with earnestness on behalf of the Romans, he brought the war to an end. On the condition of the <term>boundaries</term> <note>original: "σταθμοῖς" (stathmois). These were the territorial markers or landmarks of the land the Romans had taken from the Etruscans, which they now agreed to return.</note> which they had cut off from the **Etruscan** <note>original: "Τυρρηνίδος" (Tyrrhēnidos). The Greeks referred to the Etruscans as Tyrrhenians.</note> territory, they were to send back the prisoners and receive back the deserters. As security, the Romans gave as hostages ten noble youths wearing <term>purple-bordered robes</term> <note>original: "περιπορφύρους" (periporphyrous). This refers to the *toga praetexta*, the garment edged with purple worn by the children of the Roman nobility.</note> and as many maidens. Among these was also **Valeria**, the daughter of Publicola. After this action, Porsena dismantled all his warlike preparations because of her <note>This likely refers to the trust established by the exchange of high-ranking hostages, including the consul's own daughter.</note>.

The Roman maidens went down to the river to bathe. At a spot where the bank curved around the river in a uniform shape, it provided a particular stillness and calm to the current. Seeing no guard, nor any others passing by or sailing across, they were moved to swim across, despite the heavy current and deep eddies. Some say that one of them, named **Cloelia**, crossed the ford on horseback, urging on and encouraging the other girls as they swam. When they had arrived safely and came before Publicola, he was neither amazed nor pleased. Instead, he was distressed that he would appear inferior to Porsena in <term>keeping faith</term> <note>The Roman concept of *Fides* (trust/loyalty) was central to their identity; Publicola feared the escape would look like a Roman plot to break the treaty.</note>, and that the daring exploit of the maidens would be blamed as a **treacherous act** by the Romans. 

Therefore, having gathered them together, he sent them back again to Porsena. However, those followers of Tarquin, having perceived this beforehand, set an ambush for those escorting the girls. They attacked in greater numbers as the party was hurrying to cross the river. Even so, the guards defended themselves, and Publicola's daughter **Valeria** dashed through the middle of the fighters and escaped. Some of her servants, breaking through the skirmish with her, saved her.

<meta>This page continues the biography of Valerius Publicola, following the escape of the Roman hostages. The previous page ended with the Roman maidens preparing to swim across the Tiber to escape King Porsena’s camp.</meta>

It went well for them, as they were not unwillingly mixed among those who were fighting <note>This refers to the Roman maidens who, led by Cloelia, swam across the Tiber under a hail of darts, though the text here picks up with their reception after the escape.</note>. When Porsena perceived this, he quickly came to the rescue of the girls; and after the enemy <note>In this context, the Roman guards or pursuit party.</note> had fled, he restored them to the Romans. When Porsena saw the maidens brought back, he **marveled** at the deed of Cloelia. Having encouraged the others, she had led them out. Upon hearing the name of **Cloelia**, he looked at her with a cheerful and bright face. He then ordered a horse to be brought and presented it to her, beautifully adorned as a royal steed.

Some offer this as a wondrous testimony, saying that Cloelia crossed the river on a horse; but others say no, but that she swam with the other children of the <term>Etruscans</term> <note>original: "Τυρρηνόν" (Tyrrhēnon). While the text says "of the Etruscan," it refers to the group of hostages held by Porsena.</note>. There is a statue of the maiden placed for those traveling toward the **Palatine Hill**, an equestrian statue of her on a single horse. However, some say it is not of Cloelia, but of **Valeria** <note>original: "Οὐεστερίας" (Ouesterias). This is a scribal variation or error for 'Valeria,' the daughter of Publicola mentioned on the previous page.</note>. Porsena, having made peace with the Romans, demonstrated his own virtue and great magnanimity toward the city.

For he commanded the Etruscans to take only what they could carry; leaving the camp full of plenty of grain and wealth of every kind, he handed it over to the Romans. For this reason, even in our time, when selling public property, the heralds first announce "**the goods of Porsena**," honoring the man and preserving an everlasting memory of his grace. A bronze statue of him stands near the **Senate-house**, simple and archaic in its workmanship.

After these events, when the **Sabines** invaded the territory, Marcus Valerius—the brother of **Publicola**—and Postumius Tubertus were appointed consuls. While the greatest matters were conducted by the counsel and presence of Publicola, Marcus was victorious in two great battles. In the second of these, without losing a single Roman, he slew thirteen thousand of the enemy. As a prize for these <term>triumphs</term> <note>original: "θριάμβοις" (thriambois). The triumph was a civil ceremony and religious rite of ancient Rome, held to publicly celebrate and sanctify the success of a military commander.</note>, a house was built for him at public expense.

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Valerius Publicola. The previous page concluded with King Porsena withdrawing from Rome and leaving behind a camp full of supplies for the Roman people as a gesture of goodwill, as well as the discussion of the statue of the hostage Cloelia.</meta>

->+<-

...at public expense on the Palatine <note>The Greek text here uses "paraliois" (by the sea), but contextually refers to the Velia or Palatine hill in Rome where Publicola’s house was famously situated. This may be a scribal error in the manuscript.</note>. Of these houses, his was the one that was gated; then, because his house opened toward the public square, he made it so that the outer doors of that house alone should open outwards. This was done as a concession of honor, with the public space always being prioritized. They say that all Greek houses previously functioned in this way, taking the evidence from comedies: that those who are about to go out knock and make a sound on their own doors from within, so that there would be a warning for those approaching or standing in front of them, so that they might not be struck by the door as it opened into the narrow street.

In the following year, Publicola was made consul for the fourth time. There was an expectation of war, as the <term>Sabines</term> <note>An ancient Italic tribe neighboring the Romans, often in conflict during the early Republic.</note> and Latins were joining forces. At the same time, a certain superstition <note>original: "δεισιδαιμονία" (deisidaimonia). In a Roman context, this refers to a religious awe or the fear of divine omens (prodigies).</note> gripped much of the city. For the women who were then pregnant were failing to give birth normally, instead casting out deformed offspring; not a single birth was completed perfectly.

Wherefore, after consulting the **Sibylline books** <note>A collection of oracular utterances, kept in the Temple of Jupiter, consulted by the Roman Senate in times of crisis.</note>, Publicola propitiated **Hades** <note>original: "τῷ ᾅδη" (tō hadē). While Hades is the Greek name, in a Roman context this refers to Pluto or Dis Pater, the god of the underworld.</note> and revived certain games <note>original: "ἀγῶνας" (agōnas). These were likely the Ludi Saeculares or similar ritual contests.</note> prescribed by the oracles. Having settled the city into a more pleasant state of hope toward the divine, he then turned his attention to the fears coming from men. For a great preparation and coalition of the enemy was appearing.

Now there was a certain **Appius Clausus** among the Sabines, a man powerful in wealth and famous for his physical strength, but excelling most of all in virtue, reputation, and skill in speech. But he did not escape the fate that happens to all great men: he was envied. His detractors used his attempts to stop the war as a pretext, claiming he wished to increase the power of the Romans so that he might seize a tyranny and enslave his homeland. Perceiving these rumors—and seeing that the masses were listening to them and clashing with those who were pro-war and of the military faction—he feared a trial. But since he had a faction and power of his own among his relatives, he was able to endure the...

->the insurrection.<-

<meta>This page continues the biography of Valerius Publicola during his fourth consulship. It follows the omens and superstitions that troubled Rome, leading into the political maneuvering between the Romans and the Sabines.</meta>

This situation brought about a delay and hesitation in the war for the **Sabines** <note>An ancient Italic people located in the central Apennines, frequently both rivals and allies to early Rome.</note>. **Publicola**, therefore, made it his task not only to be informed of these matters, but also to stir up and further provoke those involved in the **factions** <note>original: "στασι-" (stasi-), referring to *stasis* or civil discord. The Sabines were divided between those who wanted war with Rome and those who wanted peace.</note>, for he had suitable men for this purpose. These men spoke to **Clausus** <note>Attus Clausus, a wealthy and influential Sabine leader who favored peace with Rome. He would later be known by the Roman name Appius Claudius.</note> on Publicola's behalf, saying: 

"Publicola considers you a good and just man, and believes that you should not seek to take revenge upon your own citizens with evil, even though you are being wronged. But if you wish to save yourself and change your residence, he invites you to move, both publicly and privately, in a manner worthy of your own virtue and the **brilliance** of the Romans."

As Clausus considered these words many times, it appeared to him that this was a necessary course of action, and the best one available. He called upon his relatives and persuaded many of them to follow him; thus, he uprooted five thousand households with their wives and children—people who were of a particularly peaceful and quiet disposition among the Sabines—and led them to Rome. 

Publicola, having prior knowledge of their arrival, received them kindly and eagerly with all the rights due to them. He immediately integrated these households into the **citizen body** <note>original: "πολιτεί-" (politei-). This refers to the *politeia*, the rights and duties of a citizen.</note> and assigned to each family two <term>plethra</term> <note>A unit of land measurement, roughly 100 feet square or about a quarter of an acre.</note> of land near the **Anio** <note>A major tributary of the Tiber River in central Italy.</note> river. To Clausus himself, he gave twenty-five plethra of land and enrolled him in the **Senate** <note>original: "βουλῇ" (boule). The council of elders and highest governing body in Rome.</note>. This was the beginning of his political life, which he used so wisely that he quickly rose to the highest rank and gained great power. He left behind the family of the **Claudii** in Rome, a lineage no less illustrious than any other.

Though the affairs of the Sabines were thus divided by the migration of these men, the **demagogues** <note>original: "δήμαρχοι" (demarchoi). While this often means 'tribunes' in a Roman context, Plutarch uses it here to describe the popular agitators among the Sabines.</note> would not allow the causes of this unrest to be settled in silence. They complained bitterly that Clausus, who could not persuade his city while present, would now achieve its destruction as a fugitive and an enemy. Feeling they had done no wrong to the Romans from whom they were now separated, they all set out with a large army and encamped near **Fidenae** <note>An ancient town located about five miles north of Rome, often a flashpoint for conflict.</note>. They placed an ambush in wooded areas just before Rome...

...at **Fidenae** <note>The OCR reads "σι Καύχοις," which appears to be a transcription error for "ἐν δὲ Φιδήναις" (in Fidenae), the location of this historical battle.</note>, two thousand **hoplites** <note>original: "ὁπλίτας" (hoplitas). If the Romans followed them in a **double-course** <note>original: "δίαυλος" (diaulos). This term usually refers to a double-lap race in a stadium; here it describes the Romans pursuing the decoys back toward the city gates.</note> toward the city, the Sabines were to flee until they led the enemy into the ambush. <insert>L'</insert> <note>The letter 'L' (Lambda) in the margin likely serves as a section marker or reference to a source.</note> **Publicola** himself, having learned of this on the same day from deserters, <insert>L'</insert> counter-arranged his plans for everything and divided his forces. **Postumius** <note>Postumius Albus, Publicola's colleague in the consulship.</note> was assigned two thousand heavy infantry and, setting out while it was still evening, occupied the hilltops where the **Sabines** were waiting in ambush and kept watch over them. The commander **Lucretius** <note>Spurius Lucretius, a prominent Roman official.</note>, having all the light-armed and most noble troops within the city, was urged to attack the horsemen who were driving off the plunder. Publicola himself, leading his army, circled around the enemy. By chance, a deep fog fell, and at dawn Postumius attacked those in the ambush, rising up from the heights <unclear>having arisen</unclear>. At the same time, Lucretius set his men upon the horsemen who had ridden out first. Then Publicola attacked the enemy's camps. On all sides, the Sabine cause was battered and destroyed. The Romans pursued those who were no longer even defending themselves but fleeing, as the Sabines' own hopes became their ruin. One group was saved by the other, as they fought separately and paid no heed to the rest. Instead, those from the fortifications ran toward those in ambush, while those in ambush fled back toward the camp; thus, the fugitives ran into the very people they were fleeing to for help, and those who needed help did not expect to be able to provide it. The fact that not all the Sabines perished, but some survived, was due to the city of **Fidenae** being nearby, which provided refuge. This was especially true for those from the camps who escaped quickly. But those from Fidenae missed their mark and were destroyed, or were led away alive by

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Valerius Publicola’s military triumphs over the Sabines. The previous page described the Roman counter-ambush at Fidenae and the clever tactical maneuvers led by Publicola and his colleague Postumius to trap the Sabine forces.</meta>

-> + <-

This success was so complete that the Romans, although usually accustomed to attribute all great achievements to divine intervention, considered this to be the work of the general alone. Indeed, from those who took part in the battle, one could hear it said that **Publicola** had delivered the enemy to those outside to be dealt with as if he had almost shut them up in a cage—rendering them as helpless as if they were lame or blind. The common people were also greatly strengthened by the wealth gained from the spoils <insert>of</insert> the war and the sale of prisoners. 

After Publicola had celebrated his triumph and handed over the governance of the city to the consuls appointed to succeed him, he passed away <note>The OCR reads "ἀθέως" (atheos), which literally means "godlessly" or "without god." This is likely a transcription error in the manuscript for "ἀπέθανεν" (died) or "ἀγαθῶς" (nobly), as the context describes a virtuous end.</note>. He finished his life in as perfect a manner as is possible for human beings, and especially for those who are considered truly **noble and good** <note>original: "καλοῖς καὶ ἀγαθοῖς" (kalois kai agathois). This term refers to the Greek aristocratic ideal of a person who possesses both physical excellence and moral virtue.</note>. The people, feeling they had not rewarded him enough while he lived and owing him every debt of gratitude, voted that his body should be buried at public expense. 

Each citizen contributed a small coin—a **quadrans** <note>original: "τεταρτημόριον" (tetartemorion). This was a tiny fraction of a drachma, representing the Roman *quadrans*, the smallest denomination of currency, showing the universal respect of the people.</note>—to honor him. The women, by a private and unanimous agreement, mourned for the man for a whole year—a tribute both honorable and enviable. He was buried within the city, near the **Velia** <note>The OCR "Φηλιαμένῳ" is a corruption of the Velian Hill in Rome.</note> in the area of the **Valerii** <note>The OCR "Ἰουβίτοις" likely refers to the burial place of the Valerius family.</note>, because his own estate was found to be insufficient to cover the costs. Thus, the entire family was granted the right of burial there. 

Today, however, none of his descendants are actually interred at that spot. Instead, they carry the deceased to the location and set the body down. Someone then takes a lighted torch and briefly touches it to the ground and then immediately removes it, as if to perform the burial, before withdrawing. This ritual serves as a living testimony that the family still possesses the legal right of burial there, though they decline the actual honor out of modesty and carry the body away to be buried elsewhere. 

We see, then, how far this exceeds the usual standard of honors. It is worth noting that the declaration **Solon** made to **Croesus** regarding **happiness** <note>A famous historical anecdote where the Athenian sage Solon told the wealthy King Croesus that no man should be called happy until he has died a noble and stable death.</note> applies even better to **Publicola** than to **Tellus**. Solon said that Tellus was blessed because of his good fortune, virtue, and his fine children; yet Solon did not celebrate him in his poems as a truly great man...

<meta>This page continues the comparison between the Athenian lawgiver Solon and the Roman founder Publicola. The previous page concluded the narrative of Publicola's life and his public funeral; this page transition moves into a philosophical "Comparison" (synkrisis), a standard feature of Plutarch’s Parallel Lives, evaluating which man achieved a greater state of "happiness" or "blessedness" based on Solon's own criteria.</meta>

...[Tellus's] children achieved no great reputation, nor did any of them rise to glory through high office. **Publicola**, however, while still alive, was foremost in power and reputation among the Romans because of his virtue; and even after his death, among the most illustrious houses and families <note>The Greek word "στέμμασιν" (stemmasin) literally means "wreaths" or "crowns," but in a Roman context, it refers to the genealogical charts or "pedigrees" displayed in the atria of noble houses.</note>, the descendants of **Publicola**, the **Messallae**, and the **Valerii**, even down to our own day—a span of six hundred years—still attribute the glory of their noble birth to him. Furthermore, **Tellus** <note>Tellus was an Athenian citizen whom Solon famously described to King Croesus as the happiest of men because he lived in a prosperous city, had fine children, and died bravely in battle.</note> met his end at the hands of his enemies, falling in battle like a brave man while remaining in his rank. But **Publicola**, by killing his enemies—which is far more pleasant than falling oneself—and seeing his fatherland victorious through his own leadership and generalship, and being honored with a triumph, died having achieved a state very close to those things envied and blessed by **Solon**. 

Indeed, regarding the lines Solon spoke in response to **Mimnermus** <note>Mimnermus was a Greek elegiac poet who wished for a painless death at age sixty. Solon corrected him, wishing instead for a death that would be mourned by friends.</note> concerning the length of a human life:

> May my death not come unwept; but to my friends
> May I, in dying, leave behind grief and sighs.

One might consider **Publicola** a truly happy man in this regard. For in his passing, he caused tears, longing, and a heavy heart not only for his friends and kinsmen, but for the entire city and many thousands of citizens. Even the Roman women mourned him as though they had lost a son, a brother, or a common father.

**Solon** says, "I desire to have wealth, but I do not wish to possess it unjustly," <note>original: "χρήματα μὲν οὖν ἱμείρουσιν ἔχειν... ἀδίκως δὲ πεπᾶσθαι" (chremata men oun himeirousin echein... adikos de pepasthai). This quote is from Solon's Fragment 13.</note> believing that divine justice surely follows. **Publicola**, however, possessed the ability not only to avoid gaining wealth through evil means, but also to spend it nobly, doing good for those in need. Thus, if **Solon** was the wisest of all men, **Publicola** was the most fortunate. For those blessings which Solon prayed for as the greatest and most beautiful, Publicola managed to acquire, and he kept them for his own use until the very end. Thus has **Solon** brought honor to **Publicola**, and he [Publicola] in turn has honored **Solon** in the con- <meta>catchword: -stitution (πολιτείᾳ).</meta>

<meta>This page continues the "Comparison of Solon and Publicola" from Plutarch's *Parallel Lives*. Having established the personal virtues and deaths of the two men on the previous page, the text now evaluates their political achievements, specifically how Publicola adapted Solon's democratic principles to the Roman Republic and how Solon addressed the economic crisis in Athens.</meta>

Having established the finest example, he manfully governed the **democracy**. He stripped away the pomposity of the office <note>The office referred to here is the Roman consulship, which initially held much of the power of the old kings.</note>; he made it unpretentious and painless for all, yet he made use of many of **Solon's** laws. For indeed, by establishing various magistracies, he made the common people masters of the state. And for those facing trial, he granted them the right to appeal to the people, just as **Solon** gave that power to the jurors. Nor did he make the city <gloss>just as Solon did</gloss> any less well-proportioned in its governance. He increased the number of festivals, nearly doubling them in number. The establishment of treasurers <note>The *quaestors*, who were officials in charge of the public treasury (the *aerarium*).</note> over the public funds also originated from him, so that the high magistrate would not have too much leisure to handle the treasury, nor find easy opportunities for wrongdoing by becoming master of both the political actions and the public money.

But the hatred of tyranny in **Publicola** <note>original: "Ποπλικύλα" (Poplikula)</note> was even more intense. For if someone should attempt to set themselves up as a tyrant, while the one [Solon] punishes those who act unjustly through a legal process, the other [Publicola] grants permission to kill them even before a trial. Although **Solon** correctly and justly takes <gloss>some</gloss> pride in the fact that, even when circumstances offered him the chance to be a tyrant and the majority of the people would have accepted it willingly, he refused the power; no less a <gloss>certain</gloss> claim to authority belongs to **Publicola** for taking an office that was tyrannical in nature <note>The early Roman consulship held the absolute power (*imperium*) of the former kings.</note> and making it more democratic, and for not exercising the absolute power that was available to him. 

And **Solon** seems to have been the first to realize this: that the people fare best when they have the finest leaders, neither letting their liberty loose into chaos nor being oppressed. But unique to **Solon** was the <term>discharge of debts</term> <note>original: "χρεῶν ἄνεσις" (chreōn anesis). Historically known as the *Seisachtheia* or "shaking-off of burdens," Solon canceled existing debts to prevent citizens from being sold into slavery.</note>, which more than anything else secured liberty for the citizens. For equality before the law is of no use if the debts of the poor are not removed; instead, in the very places where they seem most to enjoy their liberty, they are actually most enslaved to the rich—in judging, in governing, and in speaking—serving their creditors because of their financial contracts. But what is even greater is that while every cutting-off of debts <note>original: "χρεῶν ἀποκοπῆς" (chreōn apokopēs)</note> is usually followed by civil strife, he alone used it like a risky medicine...

<meta>This page continues the "Comparison of Solon and Publicola" from Plutarch’s Parallel Lives. Following the discussion of how Publicola (the Roman) adapted Solon’s (the Athenian) laws, the text now evaluates whose political system was more stable and how they each handled military and civil crises. This page highlights the tragic end of Solon’s constitution compared to the triumph of the Roman Republic.</meta>

He [Publicola] dissolved the accusation strongly, opportunely, and sufficiently by means of his own **virtue** and **reputation**, having overcome the injustice and slander of the matter. 

Regarding the whole constitution, Solon was the more illustrious in its beginning; for Publicola followed in his path and, by means of his example, achieved the greatest and most significant of public affairs. Yet, in the end, the other [Publicola] was more fortunate and enviable. For Solon himself lived to see his own constitution dissolved. Thus, this was a bitter <unclear>first-offering</unclear> <note>original: "ἀπαρχὴ" (aparchē); refers to a sacrificial first-fruit or, here, the beginning of a disaster.</note> of the civil wars, which destroyed the order of the city. 

For the one [Solon], as soon as he had established the laws, left them behind on wooden tablets <note>The *axones*: revolving wooden pillars on which the laws of Solon were inscribed and displayed in Athens.</note> and abandoned writings, and went away from Athens. But the other [Publicola], becoming the sole ruler and magistrate for many years, established and settled the laws into the very nature and character of the citizens. 

Furthermore, while Solon was not able to prevent **Peisistratus** <note>The tyrant who seized power in Athens shortly after Solon's reforms.</note> from gaining power, even though he intended to oppose him, there was a bitter joint-dissolution <unclear>of</unclear> the tyranny in Publicola’s case. For this man [Publicola] cast out and overthrew a kingship that had been powerful for many years and was already dominant. He achieved this by offering an equal intention and a similar purpose, and by using a finishing power to achieve virtue.

As for military matters, Solon indeed—for he never appeared in arms—is witnessed to have used a feigned madness <note>Solon famously pretended to be insane so he could safely recite a poem in the marketplace urging the Athenians to fight for the island of Salamis, which it was otherwise illegal to mention.</note> for the sake of **Salamis**, and he went forth out of love for it. But the other [Publicola], having cast aside all shame, rose up against his master <note>The "master" here refers to the exiled King Tarquin the Proud, who attempted to regain his throne.</note> in the midst of the greatest dangers, and he detected the treason. He was the primary cause of the wicked being punished and unable to escape; he not only cast the physical person of the tyrant out of the city, but he also cut out their very hopes of return. Already, to the matters at hand, he brought a mind and spirit of resistance, standing firm and unswerving...

And though he [Publicola] stumbled slightly, he brought about greater results, just like that man at that time. While he withdrew from the rest of the territory, he secured his own territory within; and with the very forces by which he intended to protect the city, he won over the besieging camp itself. Having set himself up to be the judge <gloss>to become</gloss> of the war and having prevailed, he obtained a victory that was no less beloved than winning a battle. For he both ended the war and left them with the provisions of war, through his **virtue** and **noble character** <note>original: "καλοκαγαθίας" (kalokagathia); a Greek ideal of being both beautiful in soul and good in action.</note>, qualities which the ruler [Porsenna] admired in him above all else.

<margin>Themistocles</margin>

# The Life of Themistocles

As for **Themistocles**, his family background was somewhat obscure in terms of fame. For his father was Neocles, a man not among the very prominent in Athens; he belonged to the deme <note>An administrative district or "neighborhood" in ancient Athens.</note> of **Phrearrhioi**, from the tribe of **Leontis**.

<meta>The previous page concluded the comparison of Solon and Publicola. This page marks the beginning of the Life of Themistocles, shifting from the legendary founding figures of Athens and Rome to the Persian War era. The text opens with an investigation into Themistocles’ lineage and his early education.</meta>

He was illegitimate on his mother’s side, as they say: "Abrotonon, a Thracian woman by birth; who bore to the Greeks the great Themistocles." <note>This is a quote from a poem by Amphicrates. Themistocles' status as a "nothos" (a child of a non-Athenian mother) significantly influenced his early social maneuvers.</note> Phanias, however, records that the mother of Themistocles was not a Thracian, but a woman from Caria <note>A region in western Asia Minor (modern Turkey).</note>; and that her name was not Abrotonon, but Euterpe. Neanthes adds to this that she was from the city of Halicarnassus in Caria. 

For this reason, because the illegitimate <term>nothoi|children born of one non-Athenian parent</term> were enrolled at **Cynosarges**—this is a gymnasium dedicated to Heracles outside the city gates, chosen because Heracles himself was not a "legitimate" god among the deities, but was burdened by the status of illegitimacy through his mother, who was a mortal—Themistocles persuaded some of the well-born young men to go down to Cynosarges and exercise <gloss>to strip for wrestling and oiling</gloss> with him. By doing this, he seems to have craftily abolished the distinction between the illegitimate and the well-born. 

Furthermore, he was associated with the family of the **Lycomidai** <note>An ancient Athenian priestly family.</note>, as the records show; for he himself restored the initiation shrine at Phlya, which belonged to the Lycomidai and had been burned by the barbarians <note>referring to the Persian army under Xerxes in 480 BCE</note>, and he adorned it with paintings, as Simonides has recorded. 

While still a child, it is generally agreed that he was full of energy—clever by his very nature, and by his own choice, a man of great affairs and politically minded. For in his moments of relaxation and during breaks from his lessons, he did not play or idle about like the other boys; instead, he was found practicing various speeches and composing them for himself. These speeches were typically an accusation or a defense of one of the other children. 

Because of this, his teacher used to say: "My boy, you will be nothing small, but certainly something great—either for good or for evil." Furthermore, when it came to his studies, he would learn those subjects intended to form character or pursued for pleasure and liberal grace only slowly and without enthusiasm. However, regarding those subjects said to improve practical intelligence or the ability to act, he was clearly interested beyond his years, as if trusting in his own natural talent. Consequently, later on in life, during what are called the "liberal" and sophisticated gatherings, when he was being...

<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion of Themistocles’ parentage and his childhood efforts to bridge the social gap between "well-born" Athenian children and those of mixed heritage at the Cynosarges gymnasium. It ended mid-sentence describing the general consensus on his character.</meta>

...so that he might appear <gloss>himself</gloss> to defend himself more forcefully [against his critics]. He used to say that he did not know how to tune a lyre or handle a psaltery, but that if he were to take over a city that was small and obscure, he knew how to make it illustrious and great. And yet, Stesimbrotus <note>A 5th-century BCE writer from Thasos known for his biographies of Athenian politicians.</note> claims that Themistocles was a student of Anaxagoras and that he studied under Melissus the natural philosopher; however, he is not very accurate with his chronology. For Melissus was a general against Pericles (who was much younger than Themistocles) when Pericles was besieging the Samians. Anaxagoras, too, was a contemporary of Pericles. 

Therefore, one might rather pay attention to those who say that Themistocles was an admirer of Mnesiphilus of the Phrearrhii <note>A deme, or district, of ancient Attica.</note>. Mnesiphilus was neither an orator nor one of the so-called "natural philosophers," but he made a profession of what was then called "wisdom," which was actually political shrewdness and practical intelligence. He preserved this tradition as if it were a sect <note>original: "hairesis" — here referring to a specific school of thought or practical tradition</note> inherited in succession from Solon. Those who came after him mixed this with legal arts and shifted the focus of their training from actual deeds to speeches; these men were called **Sophists** <note>Originally meaning "wise men," this term later became a label for professional teachers of rhetoric who were often criticized for prioritizing persuasion over truth.</note>. It was with this Mnesiphilus, then, that Themistocles associated when he was already entering public life. 

In the first impulses of his youth, Themistocles was unstable and inconsistent. He followed his own nature without the guidance of reason or formal education, and this nature produced great changes in his pursuits in both directions. <note>An 'X' mark appears in the manuscript text here, likely a reader's notation or a mark for later correction.</note> His character often veered toward the worse, as he himself later admitted. He used to say that the wildest colts become the best horses, provided they receive the proper training and breaking in. 

As for the stories that some people attach to these things—inventing a tale that he was disowned by his father, and that his mother committed suicide out of grief over her son's disgrace—these seem to be false. On the contrary, there are those who say that his father tried to divert him from public affairs by pointing out the old triremes <term>trireme|the standard warship of the era, powered by three banks of oars</term> lying abandoned and neglected <meta>catchword: neglected.</meta>

<summary>This page discusses Themistocles' intellectual influences, specifically his rejection of traditional arts in favor of political "wisdom," and refutes rumors of his early family disgrace.</summary>
<keywords>Themistocles, education, Stesimbrotus, Anaxagoras, Melissus, Mnesiphilus, Sophists, youth, triremes</keywords>

<meta>The previous page discussed Themistocles' early education, his rejection of traditional musical arts in favor of the "wisdom" of political shrewdness, and his association with Mnesiphilus, who followed the practical tradition of Solon.</meta>

...just as he did toward the demagogues <note>In ancient Athens, a "demagogue" (leader of the people) was a politician who championed the cause of the common people, often using oratorical skill.</note> who became utterly useless, not possessing the favor of the masses. However, it seems that Themistocles took up political affairs quickly and with youthful energy. He was strongly mastered by a drive for glory, because of which, desiring from the very beginning to be first, he boldly threw himself into opposition against those who were powerful and prominent in the city. 

He was especially set against Aristides, the son of Lysimachus, who followed a path opposite to his own. It seems that their enmity had an entirely juvenile beginning. For they were both in love with the beautiful Stesilaus <gloss>who was from Iulis</gloss> <note>Iulis was a city on the island of Ceos, known for its intellectual and artistic life.</note>, as Ariston the philosopher has recorded. From this point on, they continued to be at odds even in public affairs. 

Moreover, the dissimilarity of their lives and characters seems to have increased their difference. Aristides, being gentle by nature and a man of noble character <note>original: "kalokagathikos" — a term describing the Athenian ideal of being both "beautiful and good," or a true gentleman.</note> in his ways, conducted his politics not for favor or for glory, but for the best interest, along with safety and justice. Thus, he was often compelled to oppose Themistocles, who was constantly stirring up the people and introducing great innovations, resisting him to check his growth. 

It is said that Themistocles was so carried away by glory and was such a lover of great deeds due to his ambition, that while still young, when the Battle of Marathon against the barbarians <note>In this context, "barbarians" refers to the Persians, who invaded Greece in 490 BCE.</note> had occurred and the generalship of Miltiades was being celebrated, he was often seen deep in thought by himself, staying awake through the nights and declining his usual drinking parties. 

And to those who asked and marveled at the change in his lifestyle, he would say that "the trophy of Miltiades would not let him sleep." For while others thought the total destruction of the barbarians at Marathon was the end of the war, Themistocles thought it was only the beginning of greater struggles, for which he <gloss>always</gloss> anointed himself <note>The term "anointed" (eleiphe) refers to an athlete preparing for a contest in the gymnasium, symbolizing his preparation for the defense of Greece.</note> for the sake of all Greece and trained the city, already looking forward to the future from afar. ...from the revenue of the silver mines at Laurium <note>Laurium (modern Lavrio) was a mining district in southern Attica. A major discovery of silver there around 483 BCE provided the funds for Themistocles' naval project.</note>, which the Athenians were in the habit of distributing among themselves. He alone dared to step forward before the assembly of the people and say that they should give up this distribution and instead, with these funds, build triremes <term>triremes: the "super-weapon" of the 5th century BCE—a fast, agile warship with three banks of oars designed to ram and sink enemy vessels</term> for the war against the Aeginetans <note>The people of the island of Aegina, located in the Saronic Gulf. At the time, they were Athens' primary naval rival.</note>. For this conflict was then at its height in Greece, and the Aeginetans controlled the sea with a great number of ships.

This made it easier for Themistocles to persuade the citizens; he did not use the threat of Darius <note>Darius I, the Persian King who launched the first invasion of Greece, ending at the Battle of Marathon in 490 BCE.</note> or the Persians to intimidate them. For the Persians were far away, and there was no firm fear that they would return. Instead, by utilizing the people's anger and rivalry <note>The Greek text cuts off at "φιλονει" (philonei-), likely the beginning of "philoneikia," meaning love of strife or rivalry.</note>...
